#2894. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guidance; Extension of Comment Period (PDF)

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations (PDF)

Agencies seek comment on proposed third-party risk management guidance and issue statement on community bank engagement with core service providers

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

20th Street and Constitution Avenue N.W., Washington, DC 20551

#2888. Market's Nvidia obsession obscuring an opportunity in this name

The market is unable to divert its attention from anything not related to the ongoing saga that is Nvidia and its embattled former CEO Jensen Huang. It's understandable, given both the detail of the story and Nvidia impact on the market. It remains the tech story du jour, and it's getting plenty of ink.

I think investors should remember there are other big names reporting this week outside of Nvidia that deserve time and attention. One such name is The Walt Disney Company (DIS).

After a prolonged period of strategic transitions, the company may finally be turning the corner. Driven by a renewed focus on the bottom line, management's cost discipline now supports a runway for double-digit earnings-per-share (EPS) gains through fiscal 2027, even as it continues to trade at about its 5-year average forward multiple of 14.3x, well below the broad market multiple of more than 18x. Trading back to a market multiple, justified if it establishes above-market EPS growth, suggests ~26% upside, which is also consistent with a sum-of-the-parts analysis that points to an intrinsic valuation of $140 to $160 per share versus the Friday, November 13th closing price of $118.97.

Despite broader macroeconomic anxieties, the theme park and cruise divisions are showing surprisingly resilient demand. Domestically, the metrics are highly encouraging: attendance is climbing, and more importantly, per-capita spending continues to rise.

On the digital front, Direct-to-Consumer (DTC) Disney's streaming margins currently sit in the low teens, respectable given the segment's previous lack of profits. But the real upside scenario requires looking ahead: if Disney can scale these DTC margins to 20% or higher, the stock could at least trade at a multiple comparable to streaming king Netflix (which would imply a share price of ~ $139 or so.

With linear TV stabilizing, the parks firing on all cylinders, and streaming marching toward higher profitability, Disney offers a highly asymmetric risk-reward profile. The magic is returning to Disney's financials, and the current share price heavily discounts a business fundamentally engineered for double-digit growth.

Right now, the options market is pricing a ~6% move by the end of the week (the company reports Tuesday, pre-open), which is below the 8 quarter median move of 7.4%. Anticipating a "vol crush" after earnings, I like selling December premium to help finance the purchase of a longer-dated call - a diagonal call/spread risk reversal.

When selecting an option trade consider the key things that affect its value and how those things differ from simply buying (or selling) the underlying asset. For these option trades, let's consider three sensitivities (Greeks): Delta, the rate at which the option position changes in value for changes in the underlying price. Theta, or decay, which measures how much the value of an option declines as time passes, and Vega, the change in an option's price associated with changes in the implied volatility of an option.

In the case of the diagonal risk reversal each of these factors plays into the structure to our benefit. The delta of the structure is less than 100, meaning that the p&l associated with moves up or down will be less for one spread than they would be for 100 shares of stock.

We like this because the stock has already had a decent run recently, and the option structure will lose less than the stock will if it gives that back. Because nearer dated expiration decay more rapidly as a percentage of the options value than longer dated options do (and out of the money options also exhibit accelerated decay), the shorter dated December options we're selling should decay in value more quickly than the longer dated March options we are buying, even though the March option has a higher premium. Vega can work in our favor as well due to "vol crush". When option premiums are elevated due to a catalyst such as earnings we should expect that much of that premium will be sucked out or crushed once the event has passed.

This will impact both the December and March options to a degree, but remember that the March options also capture another earnings event in February, so some measure of event variance (elevated option premium) will remain in the March expiration to account for that.

#2884. Nets' Josh Minott, Bulls' Matas Buzelis fined $25,000 after making obscene gestures toward fans

Brooklyn Nets forward Josh Minott and Chicago Bulls forward Matas Buzelis were fined $25,000 by the NBA after making obscene gestures toward the fans the league announced Monday.

Minott's incident occurred at the end of the Nets' 129-110 road loss to the Memphis Grizzlies on Saturday.

Buzelis' occurred that same evening, and came with 11:19 left in the Bulls' 117-106 win over the Minnesota Timberwolves, per the NBA.

Buzelis' incident was caught on the broadcast. After hitting a free throw, the forward flipped off someone in the crowd.

Minott's obscene gesture occurred after the game ended, per the NBA's release. Video of his gesture wasn't immediately available.

Minott played just 1:46 minutes in the contest, going 0-for-1 from the floor. He was also responsible for a turnover.

The NBA did not announce additional details regarding Minott's gesture, though it may have come immediately after the game, when Minott threw up — and bricked — a final second 3-ball while down by 19.

Minott, 23, has seen his role reduced by a fair amount this season. After a midseason trade brought him to the Nets last season, Minott played in 16 games with the team, averaging 19.3 minutes a game.

In nine games this season, Minott is averaging just 6.7 minutes per contest.

#2883. Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh stresses importance of AI to economy, but says it could ‘disappoint’

[The stream is slated to start at noon ET. Please refresh the page if you do not see a player above.]

Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh on Monday highlighted the potential economic benefits of artificial intelligence while warning that the emerging technology also could lead to widespread job losses or fail to deliver on its lofty expectations.

Speaking in San Francisco before a blue-ribbon task force he assembled to examine productivity and employment, the central bank leader stressed that AI "will have significant impact on the economy, with important implications for monetary policy down the road."

"I stress that while I'm not here to offer predictions, I'm not exactly impartial about American leadership in a time so filled with both challenge and possibility," he said in prepared remarks before his productivity and jobs task force.

In his remarks, Warsh used the term "super intelligence" instead of artificial intelligence and described it as "an entirely new force in economic life and society" that has already begun to transform economic activity. President Donald Trump recently ordered that the U.S. government now use the term "super intelligence" almost exclusively instead of AI.

Since taking the Fed chair position, Warsh has emphasized the importance of AI and its potential to improve productivity and ultimately to be a disinflationary force on the economy.

In his remarks Monday, he noted that AI could lead to productivity gains that could accelerate real economic growth beyond its current range while keeping employment near full capacity. On the downside, he said "less positive" outcomes include job displacement through automation that could raise unemployment.

At the same time, he outlined a third scenario where AI could simply "disappoint."

"After significant capital investment and stock market gains, expected returns could turn down and equity valuations could fall, leading to an economic slowdown and a more notable rise in unemployment," he said. "In time, the economy would recover, but without the productivity gains."

The potential for AI advancement has driven stock market valuations higher and led to companies raising capital to continue competing.

Monday's conference features discussion panels with business leaders, economists and academics. The final session will be a fireside chat that will include Warsh and task force leader and AI advocate Marc Andreessen, co-founder of Andreessen Horowitz.

Warsh created four other task forces addressing topics such as Fed communication, data reliance, balance sheet policy and inflation frameworks.

The chairman noted that this was the first visit he has made to a regional bank.

#2878. California sues OpenAI over Hugging Face, other AI model loss of control

California Attorney General Rob Bonta on Monday sued artificial intelligence giant OpenAI for allegedly endangering the public by developing advanced AI models without adequate safeguards and monitoring.

"OpenAI disregarded public safety, knowingly tested potentially dangerous AI models without proper oversight and safety measures, and misled consumers and the public," Bonta said at a press conference.

Bonta cited the "infamous" incident in July, where two OpenAI models broke out of a testing environment, gained unauthorized access to the open internet, and breached the internal systems of open-source developer platform Hugging Face.

OpenAI said the models were trying to find information that they could use to cheat on an evaluation, and succeeded. The break-in at Hugging Face, which Nvidia acquired for roughly $13 billion in September. rattled industry researchers and prompted government officials to call for more stringent oversight of the AI industry.

On Monday, Bonta said that OpenAI later disclosed to his investigators that it was aware of 80 incidents "involving loss of control and access to third-party networks and websites."

"But the company failed to provide the detailed information we requested to evaluate these incidents; this is concerning, and it puts the public at risk," he said.

Bonta's suit, filed in state Superior Court in San Francisco, alleges OpenAI violated California's Unfair Competition Law and accuses the company of being a public nuisance as defined by state law.

The attorney general told reporters he believed in AI's technological power, and the potential of AI models to "achieve scientific breakthroughs, boost economic growth, guard against cyber attacks, and benefit all Californians."

"But AI systems must be developed and designed safely," he said. "The companies that develop these models have a moral and yes, a legal responsibility to keep public safety at the forefront."

Bonta's suit comes as Congress has been presented with, but not passed, a comprehensive AI safety bill. AI companies, including OpenAI and Anthropic, along with critics of AI, have called for such legislation on the heels of the Hugging Face break-in and other incidents that have raised concerns about the safety of AI models.

Other AI companies, including Anthropic, Meta and Google, have since disclosed incidents where their own models went rogue. But OpenAI is the first to be sued in connection with such incidents by California.

OpenAI spokesperson Drew Pusateri, in a statement on the lawsuit, said, "Since the Hugging Face incident, we've undertaken an extensive and transparent effort to understand and address misaligned model activity."

"We've published a detailed technical report, commissioned and published a separate independent report, notified affected organizations, strengthened safeguards across our research systems, and created a framework for additional identification and disclosure of misalignment," Pusateri said.

"This lawsuit appears to single out OpenAI over a challenge that's been identified by every major AI lab. We take misalignment seriously, and we have engaged — and will continue to engage — constructively with the Attorney General's office to share information and advance strong, practical safety standards for the entire industry," Pusateri said.

Bonta's lawsuit adds to OpenAI's growing list of legal woes.

In June, Florida Attorney General James Uthmeier sued OpenAI and CEO Sam Altman, alleging that the company knowingly released an unsafe product that could harm users.

OpenAI is also facing lawsuits alleging that the company's ChatGPT platform led users to experience harmful delusions and, in some cases, to commit suicide.

California's lawsuit comes almost three months after a suit by Bonta and a coalition of other state attorneys general ended with Meta agreeing to pay $16.7 billion after being accused of misrepresenting the extent of child mental health harms caused by its apps, including Facebook and Instagram. California could receive $1.5 billion to $2.1 billion from that agreement.

OpenAI confidentially filed its prospectus with the Securities and Exchange Commission in June, setting the stage for a potentially massive initial public offering in 2027.

It is not clear how, if at all, Bonta's lawsuit will affect the IPO.

Lise Buyer, partner at IPO advisory firm Class V Group, said she had not closely followed news about the suit.

But Buyer, in an email, said, "Potential IPO buyers weigh a laundry list of risk factors, some more heavily than others."

"Investors generally do their best to assess timing and a continuum of potential outcomes," Buyer said. "Often, they will speak with uninvolved law firms in calculating the potential outcomes. Then, the risks, the timing and each investors' preferred holding period factor into the price they are willing to pay - assuming they have the option to buy or pass."

"Overall the aggregate belief about the risks — all risks — factor in to the IPO price," she said.

Earlier Monday, OpenAI announced agreements with four independent evaluators to help support its safety work.

The organizations, Apollo Research, METR, Transluce and Goodfire, will be granted "deep access" to OpenAI's internal systems and conduct distinct pilot assessments around model behavior and safeguards, OpenAI said. The company could decide to expand the agreements in the future.Altman pledged to grant independent evaluators "employee-like access" to the company in September, shortly after Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei said his company would do so as part of his call to slow down the pace of advanced model development.Independent evaluators have taken on outsized importance following high-profile safety breaches, including the Hugging Face incident, this year.

President Donald Trump encouraged AI companies to work with an "independent external auditor" as part of a "morally binding" accord he unveiled in September.

#2875. U.S. crude oil declines to $85 a barrel on recovering Middle East exports

Oil prices are falling Monday as shipments through the Strait of Hormuz recover and tensions between Iran and the U.S remain at a stalemate.

Brent crude fell 3.55% to $92.44 per barrel Monday while West Texas Intermediate crude futures settled down 3.58% to $85.39 a barrel.

The International Energy Agency said on Friday it expects global supply to exceed 1.7 million barrels a day in the fourth quarter of 2026, reversing its earlier forecast for a deficit as Middle East exports recover faster than global demand and even as shipping attacks continue.

Meanwhile, Russia's crude oil production rose to 8.73 million barrels a day in October, a 210,000 barrel-a-day surge, according to the IEA.

Goldman Sachs said in a Sunday note it expects Brent to average $88 a barrel in 2027 and fall to $78 by December 2027 as the market returns to a surplus.

The bank believes Middle East supply to rise above its 2025 average as producers use ship-to-ship transfers and add pipeline capacity.

#2874. If Brian Keefe were fired, who would be the Wizards’ interim head coach?

In my poll last week, I asked what you think the Washington Wizards should do if they continue to play sub .500 basketball through December. A majority believe that head coach Brian Keefe should be fired.

That sounds easy: firing a head coach. And I get why. Washington is now 4-10 this season and on a seven game losing streak.

The harder part: who becomes the Wizards' interim head coach if they make that decision?

This is where we need to take a look at the Wizards' coaching staff. Ultimately, here are the names who stand out as possibilities:

Steve Clifford: Clifford was an NBA head coach for a decade from 2013-24 which include two stints with the Charlotte Bobcats/Hornets and the Orlando Magic. His cumulative record is 340-461. Clifford could be an interim right away should Washington make a mid year change. He is currently a coaching advisor for the Wizards.

Adam Caporn: He is the lead assistant coach for the Wizards. Caporn is also the head coach of the Australia men's national basketball team, the Boomers. He was the Long Island Nets head coach in the G League from 2021-22. If Washington is looking for a younger coach who also leads one of the world's best men's national teams, Caporn could be on that short list.

Cody Toppert: Toppert was the Capital City Go-Go's head coach and led them to three straight playoff runs in the G League.

Patrick Ewing: The former Georgetown men's basketball head coach spent six years in D.C., leading the Hoyas to the 2021 NCAA Tournament. However, the Hoyas weren't a Big East contender and he had a sub .500 record coaching them. Before returning to the Wizards for his second stint as an assistant coach, the Naismith Hall of Fame Center helped develop post players for multiple teams.

None of this implies the Wizards are about to fire Keefe. But I'll admit this team, after a solid start, has more talent on paper and is still winning games at a similar rate to… the last three years. It's one thing for the Wizards to be losing more than two out of three games. It's another matter when Anthony Davis and Trae Young are starting games and that record isn't much different than the last three years.

#2872. Jim Cramer says Nvidia’s leadership shake-up won’t derail the AI trade

CNBC's Jim Cramer said Monday that Nvidia's leadership shake-up won't derail the artificial intelligence boom.

Cramer's comments came after another rough day for AI-related stocks in the wake of Jensen Huang's resignation from Nvidia last week. Investors have been worried that Huang's departure as CEO could jeopardize the chipmaker's financial support for the broader AI ecosystem, especially computing providers known as neoclouds. Nvidia is set to report earnings Tuesday night.

Cramer argued that demand for computing power extends far beyond Nvidia and its former CEO.

"The most important thing you need to know is that Nvidia is not going to bring down the western world," the "Mad Money" host said. "It is not going to bring down the data center world."

Under Huang, Nvidia built an extensive investment portfolio, including a $5 billion stake in Intel last year and an additional investment in AI cloud provider CoreWeave earlier this year. Nvidia had helped anchor CoreWeave's IPO in 2025 and participated in a 2023 Series B round for the company. In addition to taking equity stakes in other companies, Nvidia has provided other financial guarantees to customers, such as a $105 billion backstop for a sprawling data center project intended for OpenAI.

JPMorgan strategist Michael Cembalest, who publishes the closely followed Eye on the Market commentary, estimated that Nvidia's broader financial commitments may have reached $530 billion, based on the company's latest quarterly securities filing. Still, Cramer rejected the notion that the AI industry depends entirely on the chipmaker's backing.

"You can't overstate the importance of Jensen Huang to all of the AI investment that we've seen and expect to see," Cramer said. "But the idea that he was somehow all of the AI world is ludicrous."

Cramer expects interim CEO Colette Kress to take a more conservative approach, potentially scaling back some of Nvidia's financial commitments. While that could pressure heavily indebted companies, he said strong demand for computing power will sustain the broader AI build-out.

"Will there be a rein in of guarantees?" Cramer asked. "I hope so. Will Nvidia be more conservative? I am sure of it. Will artificial intelligence be stopped in its tracks? Are you kidding me?"

More generally, Cramer expressed confidence in Kress, praising her financial discipline and expecting a measured outlook when Nvidia reports earnings Tuesday. "I think interim CEO Kress will present a balanced non-hyperbolic view of the situations at Nvidia tomorrow night," he said. "I do not think it will cause a tsunami of selling."

A successful Anthropic IPO later this week could further restore confidence in the AI trade, Cramer argued.

"If the Anthropic deal works later this week, look out – we might see investors scrambling to get back into the same stocks that were sold today," Cramer said.

#2869. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2867. 3-man Fastbreak: Navigating without Duren

Sitting at 8-4, Detroit finds itself right under Boston and Philadelphia at the top of the conference – having already knocked off both squads. What makes that start even more impressive is how much of it has come with Jalen Duren sidelined by a hamstring issue.

It shouldn't come as a shock to see the Pistons maintaining their place among the Eastern Conference elite, but the bigger story might be how long it takes the rest of the field to gel. Contenders like Indiana, Orlando, and Toronto entered the campaign with big ambitions, yet all three are currently stumbling below .500.

Here are a few key takeaways from this recent run of action.

Fresh off a impressive 60-win campaign, Detroit has quieted critics who viewed their summer moves with skepticism. Retaining their core talent with long-term deals has paid immediate dividends, as their roster continuity continues to fuel early momentum.

CHARLOTTE, NC – NOVEMBER 2: Cade Cunningham #2 of the Detroit Pistons looks on during the game against the Charlotte Hornets on November 2, 2026 at Spectrum Center in Charlotte, North Carolina. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. Mandatory Copyright Notice: Copyright 2026 NBAE (Photo by Kent Smith/NBAE via Getty Images) | NBAE via Getty Images

While the Pistons have routinely handled lesser opponents, their battles against heavyweights like New York, Philadelphia, and Los Angeles revealed familiar flaws. During offensive droughts, the ball movement stalled, resulting in over-reliance on Cade Cunningham to create individual magic while the surrounding unit remained static.

Is it a real cause for concern yet considering they've been down their second best player? Not in the short term.

This foundational identity guarantees postseason basketball and likely home-court advantage, yet doubts regarding their ceiling remain. Rumors of offseason trade pursuits suggest the front office recognizes the missing piece, but until a major acquisition materializes, lingering questions about their championship credentials will remain.

Detroit's identity remains largely unchanged from the past two seasons, but the offense is clearly evolving, especially without their All-NBA big man.

Last season, Detroit led the league with 57.9 points in the paint per game. This year, that figure has fallen to 47.2, around the middle of the pack. Duren's absence since the second game has certainly played a role, but the shift also reflects the team's offseason moves. Detroit moved on from Isaiah Stewart and Tobias Harris while adding better floor spacers in John Collins and Isaiah Jones. Duncan Robinson has also taken on a larger role, posting career highs with 17.4 points per game while shooting 43.1% from three.

That improved spacing has helped fuel Detroit's early success, but defense remains the team's foundation, with Detroit still leading the league in several defensive categories. Once Duren returns, it will be worth watching whether the offensive approach changes.

In the three games since Jenkins replaced Paul Reed in the starting lineup on November 8, Jenkins averaged 24.7 points, 5.7 rebounds, 4.0 assists, 2.3 steals and 4.3 three-pointers per game. The undrafted guard made occasional starts last season, but only when Cunningham was injured or resting. This year, the temporary promotion while Duren remains out shows that J.B. Bickerstaff trusts Jenkins with a larger role.

May 7, 2026; Detroit, Michigan, USA; Detroit Pistons guard Daniss Jenkins (24) celebrates a three-point basket in the second half against the Cleveland Cavaliers during game two of the second round of the 2026 NBA Playoffs at Little Caesars Arena. Mandatory Credit: Rick Osentoski-Imagn Images | Rick Osentoski-Imagn Images

Since emerging in Detroit last season, Jenkins has gone from a promising two-way player to a legitimate contributor on a contending team. Jenkins has also shown a willingness to embrace any role and play with maximum effort. The question is no longer whether Jenkins can be a reliable role player, but how large that role can become as the stakes rise.

At 25, Jenkins still has room to grow. He is shooting 42.4% from three this season on 4.9 attempts per game and is averaging fewer turnovers than last year despite playing eight more minutes per night.

That chip on the shoulder may be Jenkins' greatest strength, and continued growth could be an underrated factor in how far the Pistons go this season.

#2866. Warren Buffett donates $5.5 billion, says kindness 'compounds' in Thanksgiving letter

Billionaire Warren Buffett donated Berkshire Hathaway shares worth about $5.5 billion to four philanthropic foundations, stepping up his annual charitable giving from the prior year.

Buffett, 96, converted 6,150 Class A Berkshire shares into 9,225,000 Class B shares and gave 3,690,000 of those shares to The Susan Thompson Buffett Foundation, and 1,845,000 shares each to The Sherwood Foundation, The Howard G. Buffett Foundation and NoVo Foundation — the foundations run by his three children, Susie, Howard and Peter Buffett.

Following the donations, the chairman emeritus of Berkshire still owns 182,140 Class A shares — roughly $162 billion.

"Last Thanksgiving I wrote that kindness is costless but also priceless. A year later, I would only add that it compounds, and at a better rate than anything Charlie and I ever bought," wrote Buffett, who was succeeded as chairman in September by his son Howard, in his annual Thanksgiving message.

The letter was much shorter than his message last Thanksgiving. Charlie Munger, former vice chairman of Berkshire, passed away in November 2023.

Berkshire shares have been on a tear recently, up more than 19% since the start of the fourth quarter and ending last week with four consecutive record high closes. That compares with the S&P 500's decline of 1.2% so far for the fourth quarter. Berkshire has been seen as a safe haven as investors have worried about the artificial intelligence buildout, corporate drama and geopolitical risk.

The shares are now outperforming the S&P 500 for the year by about 7.5 percentage points.

In his letter, Buffett reiterated that all of his Berkshire shares eventually will be donated to the four foundations, with the goal being to increase the size of the grants each year.

Buffett wrote that the gifts were a signal of his confidence not only in his three children, but also in Berkshire CEO Greg Abel:

"My children have given thoughtfully to worthy causes for decades, and I trust 100% both their abilities and their fidelity. ... You heard plenty from me in September about my confidence in Berkshire. That confidence is as strong as ever," he said.

Referring to Munger and himself, Buffett added, "Both of us received beyond measurement costless benefits from a few teachers such as Ben Graham, Joe Rosenfield and Tom Murphy. Because of this we not only prospered mightily but were able to pass along many of the benefits we received."

"Warren continues to share his wisdom with us all during this holiday season. His example reflects the kindness he encourages in all of us," said CEO Abel of the letter.

Buffett closes his message with this advice: "This Thanksgiving, thank one of those selfless people in your own life. Better yet, be a benefactor for someone else."

#2864. Aaron Glenn makes stunning Jets change after ‘superpower’ boast backfires

With his defense struggling and the losses piling up, Jets coach Aaron Glenn made a big change Sunday.

Glenn surrendered defensive play-calling duties to Brian Duker, the team's defensive coordinator, in the loss to the Bills. The move is stunning because Glenn took over play calling in the offseason and called it his "superpower." But the Jets are currently 22nd in points allowed and 30th in rushing yards against. They have held an opponent to fewer than 20 points only twice in 10 games this season.

Glenn said he did not remove himself completely from calling the defense, but he put Duker in charge.

"I gave Duker the lead when it comes to that," Glenn said Monday. "Again, I'm still a huge part of what we call. It's been a collaboration throughout the whole season, to be honest with you. I had the lead in the games up to this point. This game, I wanted to give him the lead. If there's anything that I wanted to get called, I would definitely get on the headset and let him know it. If there were some calls I didn't like, I would let him know it."

The move worked pretty well … for a half. The Jets held the Bills to 10 points in the first half. Then, the Bills hit three big plays in the second half and scored three touchdowns. The Jets lost 31-12, and the defense gave up 30 or more points for the third straight week.

Glenn opened the door to a change last week, saying he would consider anything to help the Jets turn things around.

Jets head coach Aaron Glenn reacts on the sideline during a game against the Buffalo Bills at MetLife Stadium, Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. Charles Wenzelberg/NY Post

"Anything that we as a staff, anything that I can do as a head coach, can do to help us win games, I'm willing to do it and I'm not afraid to do that," Glenn said. "I gave him the lead, but I was very involved in what we ran."

The Jets are 2-8 and have lost five straight games. It seems likely that Glenn and his staff will be fired at the end of the season unless the Jets pull off a miraculous turnaround.

Jets defensive coordinator Brian Duker speaks to the media before practice in Florham Park, N.J., Thursday, Nov. 12, 2026. Bill Kostroun for NY Post

The Jets travel to Los Angeles this week to face a Chargers team that was winless entering Monday night's game with the Ravens. Glenn would not say whether Duker will call the defense again this week.

"That's something that we'll see moving forward," Glenn said. "I don't want to sit there and say that this is how it's going to be from each week going out. But again, I'm going to do whatever it takes for us to go out there and try to win a game."

Jets safety Dane Belton said the move to Duker did not affect the players.

"I don't think there was a difference," Belton said. "I think those guys work very closely together. For me, it was just going out and completing my job. Getting the play call and communicating to everyone and making sure we're on the same page and just executing. As far as the play call itself, I think they're very similar. I think they have very similar mindsets in calling the defense. I think there was not much difference from my perspective."

Glenn was the Lions defensive coordinator from 2021-24, and his success in Detroit was one of the reasons the Jets wanted to hire him as their head coach in 2025. Glenn took a CEO approach in his first season, and defensive coordinator Steve Wilks called the defense. Glenn fired Wilks during the season last year. He decided to call the defense himself this year and hired Duker, whom he worked with in Detroit, as his defensive coordinator.

Buffalo Bills' James Cook III (4) runs past New York Jets' Brandon Stephens (21) for a touchdown in East Rutherford, N.J., Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. AP Photo/Adam Hunger

Glenn praised Duker's performance Sunday.

"I thought he was calm," Glenn said. "I thought he got the calls out to [Demario Davis] in a good amount of time. I thought some of the calls he made were really good calls. As I've always said, calls and execution is something that goes hand in hand. That's something that offense, defense and special teams, we've got to improve on."

#2861. Kalshi plans to launch stock trading soon

Leading prediction market platform Kalshi will offer stock trading within the next few months, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC.

The platform's move to offer equity trading is the boldest so far in its ongoing push into traditional financial markets and comes amid an escalating fight by states and lawmakers against its sports trading business. Sports contracts continue to account for a large share of trading on the platform.

In September, the platform introduced perpetual futures tied to precious metals such as gold and silver after it had originally filed in July to offer beyond prediction market event contracts with the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, the federal regulator overseeing derivatives contracts. Perpetual futures are futures-style contracts that have no expiration date.

Kalshi also received approval to list perpetual futures tied to cryptocurrencies in late May.

The prediction market's stock trading offering was enabled by its purchase of broker-dealer FB Securities in July, the source said. FB Securities was founded in Delaware in 2024 and became registered with the Securities and Exchange Commission in December 2025. The broker did not respond to a request for comment.

The Wall Street Journal first reported that Kalshi plans to introduce stock trading.

Shares of Robinhood, which offers some prediction markets alongside equity trading, fell on Monday, though the stock was already lower amid a broader market sell-off. Robinhood closed down by 7.8%.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2855. Berkshire Hathaway raised its stake in Delta in the third quarter. It also trimmed a key bank holding

Berkshire Hathaway made several moves in the stock market during the third quarter, adding to key positions and trimming others.

The Omaha-based conglomerate, now led by CEO Greg Abel, increased its Delta Air Lines stake by 12%, scooping up 7.13 million shares. The position was worth $5.37 billion at the end of the third quarter.

Abel succeeded legendary investor Warren Buffett, 96, at the start of this year. Buffett continues to control roughly 13% of Berkshire common stock, Reuters said Monday, after the former CEO donated $5.5 billion to nonprofit foundations.

Berkshire boosted its stake in Delta as the Atlanta-based carrier's shares tumbled 11% in the third quarter, hurt by rising jet fuel prices due to the U.S.-Iran war that pressured the entire airline industry. That marked the stock's worst quarterly performance since the first quarter of 2025, when it plunged 28%. Delta last month also cut its full-year earnings forecast, citing a surge in fuel costs.

To be sure, buying that dip has been a winning investment thus far. Delta is up 3.8% so far in the fourth quarter, while the S&P 500 has dropped 3.1%. Shares are also up nearly 15% over the past month.

Berkshire also trimmed its holding in Bank of America by nearly 6%, selling more than $1.6 billion worth of stock.

BofA was under pressure in the third quarter, falling more than 4%, as interest rates surged alongside stubbornly high inflation and expectations of tighter Federal Reserve monetary policy. At one point in September the 30-year Treasury bond yield surged above 5.6% to its highest level since 2002.

Despite the share sale, Bank of America remains a top Berkshire Hathaway holding, with the conglomerate's stake valued at $24.8 billion at the end of the third quarter.

Berkshire also initiated a position in Bending Spoons and nearly doubled its stake in homebuilder Lennar in the latest quarter.

#2852. CNBC's The China Connection newsletter: As Nike and Lululemon struggle in China, Xiaohongshu is helping local brands take off

Hi, this is Evelyn, writing to you from Beijing. Welcome to the latest edition of The China Connection — a snapshot of what I'm seeing and hearing from local businesses.

While the world watched the Nvidia saga around Jensen Huang unfold, Chinese retailers quietly wrapped up the local version of Black Friday, known as Singles Day. What can foreign brands learn?

As Lululemon, On Running and other Western consumer brands have sought to expand in China, so have a crop of local brands with English names.

Monday Sleeping Club, Crying Center, AW Project, an action a day, Liegee. The list of trendy new Chinese brands has grown rapidly, despite flatlining retail sales nationwide.

"Their success rests on one shared formula," said Cynthia Zhang, analyst, Euromonitor International.

"Build a non-product asset first — credible fabric quality, an emotion, an aesthetic reputation, or proprietary fit data — then monetize it through founder-led content, Xiaohongshu-driven discovery, tight supply chains, and the quiet-luxury and Guochao [national pride] mood that prizes craftsmanship over conspicuous logos," she said.

For Liegee, one of its three founders, Cocoa Lin, told me its sales surged by roughly 160% during the latest Singles Day period to the tens of millions of yuan, equivalent to a few million U.S. dollars.

The brand launched in 2022 in Hangzhou to meet a perceived gap in clothes for early career women looking to express their personality, Lin said. She said the brand, which has over 100,000 fans for its store on Alibaba's Taobao e-commerce site, largely uses internet influencers and celebrity product seeding to generate awareness.

The brand uses Xiaohongshu, on which it has over 22,000 followers, for catalog-style photos and marketing videos. It opened a "Liegee Motel" pop-up in Shenzhen last month.

"We believe online and offline will have a closer connection in the future," Lin said in Mandarin via translation, adding that "as lifestyles become more experience-oriented, this energy can be transmitted to online."

It's a dynamic that Western brands have struggled to crack.

Nike reported a 22% sales drop in Greater China in the quarter ended Aug. 31, while Lululemon reported a 2% drop in mainland China comparable sales in the quarter ended Aug. 2.

Adidas runs at least six official Xiaohongshu accounts, with two notching well over 200,000 followers each. Nike and Lululemon each operate one official account, with over 300,000 followers each.

For a country of around 1.4 billion people, Xiaohongshu has captured a key consumer audience.

The social media app, whose layout is a mix of Instagram and Pinterest, has focused on women and young people in China's largest cities, and recent expanded into less developed parts of the country, according to Questmobile. Xiaohongshu recorded average daily users of 120 million in October, a fraction of the 422 million on Taobao and 717 million on Douyin, the report showed.

The app has also attempted to build up its own e-commerce business in order to monetize.

"RedNote [or Xiaohongshu] has been a key platform for product discovery and research, especially for female consumers shopping in the beauty, skincare, lifestyle, fashion, and health categories. Now, we're seeing more transactions within RedNote itself," said Jacob Cooke, co-founder and CEO of WPIC Marketing + Technologies. The company helps foreign brands sell in China and other parts of Asia.

Alibaba, JD.com and ByteDance's Douyin still dominated this year's Singles Day sales with a combined 1.68 trillion yuan in sales over the Oct. 7 to Nov. 11 period, according to Syntun.

What's changed more fundamentally is the mood of the Chinese consumer, including more affluent ones.

In the era of double-digit annual growth in real estate prices, consumers pursued conspicuous consumption to meet "aspirational" desires, Christine Peng, head of Greater China consumer research, UBS, told reporters last week.

"Now those needs no longer exist," she said in Mandarin, translated by CNBC.

"The rise of these trendy brands reflects how consumers now seek to define their own identities and pursue personal enjoyment, while still remaining mindful of price and quality," Peng said.

She said wealthy consumers are also embracing domestic brands, and no longer just buying traditional luxury brands.

Alice Huang tied to Nvidia's biggest Chinese competitor

Alice Huang has owned stakes in companies that have benefited Huawei, private intelligence firm Tesari shared exclusively with CNBC. Some in Beijing worry the Jensen Huang affair will negatively impact U.S.-China relations.

Exports in October jumped by 35.2%, while imports soared 41.6%, both beating Reuters' estimates. But industrial output, retail sales and investment figures for October all missed estimates. China and the U.S. discussed tariffs and agricultural trade ahead of Xi-Trump meeting, the Chinese Commerce Ministry said Saturday.

Alibaba announces plans to list T-Head, its Nvidia chip rival

The Chinese tech company disclosed the Hong Kong Stock Exchange will allow it to spin off its semiconductor subsidiary T-Head.

Nov. 18 - 19: APEC Economic Leaders' Meeting in Shenzhen

#2848. Aschenbrenner’s Situational Awareness made AI bets again before October rout

Leopold Aschenbrenner's artificial intelligence-themed hedge fund Situational Awareness, which nearly collapsed in July, loaded back up on AI stocks as it returned to the financial markets at the end of the third quarter. But all of its holdings are down big the last month and a half.

According to a new regulatory filing, as the fund got back on its feet Aschenbrenner took an equity position in Australian neocloud company SharonAI valued at more than $440 million, along with equity stakes in AI-centric plays Cleanspark, IREN, Bitdeer Technologies and Fermi. The Cleanspark stake is worth almost $39 million, while Iren and Bitdeer stakes are more than $21 million and more than $20 million, respectively. Fermi, meanwhile, has a value of more than $11 million.

The fund also owned call options at the end of September in neocloud Nebius, memory chip name Sandisk, the Roundhill Memory ETF (DRAM) and data center power play Bloom Energy. Sandisk and Nebius were key holdings that almost took out his fund over the summer.

All of these Situational Awareness holdings have been routed since Sept. 30. SharonAI is at the top of that list, seeing a 58% loss in October. It's also fallen 11% so far this month. IREN was down 41% in October, while Nebius declined 35%. Last week, Nebius plummeted more than 16% in a single day after the company issued a weak full-year forecast.

It's not known whether the hedge fund manager got out of those positions during the October AI selloff. The 13F filing is just a snapshot of what the hedge fund held at the end of the quarter.

Aschenbrenner — who launched the fund in 2024 after rising to fame that same year with his AI manifesto also entitled Situational Awareness — re-entered financial markets a bit in September, sources told CNBC at the time.

The Monday filing shows he picked up right where he left off.

The near-collapse of his hedge fund over the summer forced him to sell all of its public equity positions to Ken Griffin's Citadel. The fund had $45 billion in assets at its peak, but that tumbled to around $10 billion, consisting mostly of private stakes.

Stocked tethered to the AI trade took a hit in the most recent quarter as investors grew concerned about a slowdown in development of the technology. Those fears were spurred by comments from industry leaders such as Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei and OpenAI CEO Sam Altman back in September, with the latter saying that the company going public this year would be "ill-advised."

Since then, pressure on the trade has only continued.

In recent weeks, investors have worried about how Democratic wins in the midterm elections could hinder data center development, whether some technology companies are using too much debt to finance the AI buildout and what role Nvidia will play in that financing with Jensen Huang now out as CEO at the company.

Losses in Nvidia as well as other AI-related stocks dragged down the three leading U.S. indexes during Monday's session as investors continued to assess the fallout of Huang's departure.

#2847. Analysis: The Fed's chairman sees an AI revolution coming. Now he has to figure out what it means

Artificial intelligence is about to sweep through the economy. The Federal Reserve is bracing for impact.

"We are at a hinge point in the U.S. economy," Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh said at a conference he convened in San Francisco on Monday. But exactly when and how the economy could turn, and what that means for interest rates and inflation, isn't yet clear.

The meeting of Warsh's AI task force saw the new Fed chairman publicly asking the questions Americans will ultimately expect him to answer. He hinted at a few of his private views on whether white-collar workers could soon face massive job losses, but he largely left the speculation to an array of experts he has assembled to advise the Fed.

With the technology racing ahead, Warsh won't be able to put off giving his own answers much longer. AI lab Anthropic plans to price the shares for its initial public offering on Wednesday and could raise as much as $64 billion at a $1.4 trillion valuation. Vast spending from AI firms is prompting the Fed to contemplate raising interest rates in December to slow down the rate of price increases as the tech sector heats up the economy.

Meanwhile Americans are souring on some aspects of the technology. The Nov. 3 midterms saw voters enact an array of state-level measures to slow down construction of the data centers that are essential to powering AI.

Warsh moderated three out of four sessions at Monday's event and was involved in selecting the speakers. Among them was one of the few prominent Democrats who was willing to speak out against that anti-data center trend.

It is "a very troubling sign" that so many anti-data center measures passed in the midterms, Gina Raimondo said. She is a former secretary of commerce under President Joe Biden and a former governor of Rhode Island.

Stopping AI "would be very bad for America" and for the people who voted for the measures, Raimondo said.

Warsh didn't say whether he shares that view, and how it would translate into monetary policy isn't exactly clear.

The event was an unusual one for the Fed. The central bank employs a small army of Ph.D. economists who can rapidly weigh in on anything affecting the U.S. and global economies. But in keeping with his longstanding critiques of the Fed as insular and outdated, Warsh has chosen to draw expertise from outside the institution to help it figure out how to think about what AI will do to productivity and jobs.

Warsh has assembled a task force on that issue, alongside four others devoted to aspects of Fed policymaking.

The public heard Monday from one of the three official members of the task force: venture capitalist Marc Andreessen. Warsh went to Stanford University with Andreessen's wife, and the two have been longtime friends. Another task force member, Stanford economist Chad Jones, is on leave and working at Anthropic and couldn't speak during the company's blackout period ahead of the IPO. The third, Xbox CEO Asha Sharma, didn't attend the event.

Andreessen is hugely optimistic about the promise of AI as a technology, but believes the government won't let Americans experience its full effects. Heavy regulation in areas such as healthcare and education mean technology can't replace people's jobs in those sectors, he said.

"To the extent our economy is a free-market economy, then maybe you could see the, let's say, extremely rapid displacement scenarios people are talking about," Andreessen said. "But we've mostly outlawed the kind of job transition that … both the utopians the doomers talk about."

Warsh returned repeatedly to one scenario for the economy that draws on past experience. The so-called China shock unfolded after that country joined the World Trade Organization in 2001 and moved heavily into low-cost manufacturing. Americans saw prices on clothing and furniture drop rapidly, for instance, but at the cost of severe job losses. One widely cited estimate found the U.S. lost a million manufacturing jobs and 2.4 million jobs overall in the years that followed.

Warsh suggested to one of the panelists that the widespread adoption of AI might produce its own version of the China shock, this time focused on service workers.

"I don't want to hold this too tightly," Warsh said. "But if in some ways over the last generation or generation and a half, the China shock, for lack of a better word, lowered prices of goods in the United States, the way that I heard you talking about the philosophy of this new technology is it might do something for services."

In other words, AI could do, say, legal or administrative work more cheaply than a human. That could lower prices but put many people out of a job.

Warsh asked panelists whether "we should expect disinflation over the medium term, from what AI could do to services."

They largely suggested it was possible, which could mean the Fed might eventually want to lower interest rates over the long term.

But at the end of the day, it is Warsh and his fellow Fed members who will need to have conviction on the answer.

There are still many steps between the task force discussion and any kind of change in Fed policy. First, the three task force members will have to assemble their points of view into a report for Warsh sometime in the next few months. Then the Fed itself will need to debate those results and decide whether or how to use them to shape monetary policy.

But with AI growing bigger every day, the Fed may need to make up its mind sooner rather than later.

#2843. Trump’s accounts traded up to $97.5 million in Nvidia as Huang gained White House access

President Donald Trump has had plenty of reason to cheer the rise of Nvidia.

The chipmaker helped propel the stock market that Trump is fond of touting. Its billionaire co-founder, Jensen Huang, became one of the president's favorite businessmen. And by the end of last year, Nvidia had become one of Trump's three largest individual stock holdings, worth between $12.5 million and $58 million, up from between $615,000 and $1.3 million a year earlier.

Since returning to the White House, Trump's investment accounts have bought or sold Nvidia shares at least 103 times — transactions worth a combined $27 million to $97.5 million, according to a new CNBC analysis of his annual and periodic financial disclosures, which were most recently updated through August.

By midday Monday, Trump's Nvidia stake, based on the value he reported holding in August, would be worth $4.5 million to $57.7 million with stock priced around $157. But the filings give broad dollar ranges for holdings rather than the number of shares, leaving his current stake unclear.

Now, Huang's resignation as Nvidia's CEO on Thursday, following revelations about his relationship with a woman suspected of spying for China and reports of affairs with company employees, has put those trades back in the spotlight.

Among them was a sale of $500,001 to $1 million on May 18, the first Monday after Trump returned from a state visit to China with Huang. Five days earlier, before Huang boarded Air Force One during a refueling stop in Alaska, Secretary of State Marco Rubio had briefed Trump on intelligence concerning Huang's relationship with the suspected spy, The New York Times reported.

Trump on Monday denied being briefed. Asked about the reported briefing from Rubio, Trump said, "I haven't heard that," adding that he did not know whether the woman, Alice Huang, was a Chinese spy.

The president's financial accounts also bought smaller amounts of Nvidia shares in the days immediately before and after the May 18 sale, part of a broader trading blitz. Such flurries have defined his second term: Trump disclosed more than 21,000 trades in 2025 alone, compared with roughly 500 during his entire first term, according to CNBC's analysis.

A White House spokesperson told CNBC independent advisors manage Trump's portfolio without his input. Trump Organization spokesperson Kimberly Benza said the arrangement is designed to "avoid even the appearance of a conflict of interest." The disclosures offer no evidence Trump directed the trades or that his advisors had access to classified intelligence.

Trump has long used the stock market as a scorecard for the economy, touting its record highs — sometimes when highs have not actually been reached — despite the turbulence caused by his tariffs and the Iran war.

"Look, we have the highest stock market we've ever had," Trump told ESPN commentator and political talk show host Stephen A. Smith on Thursday when pressed about Republicans' midterm losses.

Nvidia alone accounted for about 13.5% of the S&P 500's 2026 gains through September, according to an analysis by Choate Wealth that looked at how chip stocks have driven the year's performance.

Huang's rise in Trump's Washington has been nearly as striking as Nvidia's on Wall Street. The two have met or appeared together at least 15 times since Trump returned to office, according to a CNBC review of public records and news reports. Huang in June told The Associated Press that Trump even "calls me in the middle of the night."

At Trump's Jan. 20, 2025, inauguration, several Silicon Valley titans occupied prime seats, but Huang wasn't there. The two men had never met.

That changed on Jan. 31, 2025, when Huang visited the White House, capping a bruising week for Nvidia: Chinese AI startup DeepSeek had wiped $593 billion off the company's market value in a single day, according to LSEG data cited by Reuters.

Two days before the meeting, Trump's money managers bought between $1 million and $5 million in Nvidia stock.

For Huang, much of the relationship hinged on access to China. In April 2025, the administration effectively halted sales of Nvidia's H20 chips to China, prompting a $4.5 billion charge, according to an SEC filing. Huang argued that withholding U.S. chips would only accelerate China's development of its own.

The administration eventually relented. After another meeting with Trump in July 2025, Huang announced from Beijing that Nvidia expected to resume H20 sales. A week after Trump confirmed an August 2025 deal calling for 15% of the revenue from the company's chip sales to China to go to the U.S. government, his accounts also bought between $5 million and $25 million in Nvidia stock, one of several large tech investments that day.

Huang was also becoming a fixture of Trump's international diplomacy, joining him in Saudi Arabia and the U.K. Nvidia, already a $1 million donor to Trump's inaugural fund, would pledge another $25 million to his planned White House ballroom on condition of anonymity, The Washington Post first reported.

But behind the scenes, intelligence officials first briefed Trump last fall on Huang's relationship with the woman suspected of spying for China, The New York Times reported.

Trump nevertheless continued to ease restrictions on Nvidia's business in China, announcing Dec. 8 that the company could sell its more powerful H200 chips. His accounts had sold as much as $1 million in Nvidia stock four days earlier and bought more the day after.

In the first eight months of 2026, Trump's accounts made another 38 Nvidia trades worth a combined $9.4 million to $28.3 million, according to CNBC's analysis. A disclosure released Oct. 8 — the day Trump awarded Huang the National Medal of Science — showed purchases of up to $1.6 million and sales of up to $1 million in August alone.

Trump stood by Huang as Nvidia's board weighed his future last week, urging him to fight to keep his job, the Times reported.

With Huang out and Nvidia's shares tumbling, Trump described him Thursday as a "tremendous talent" who was "going through hell" — in the same interview in which he boasted of the market's record highs.

Trump is already floating a comeback for Huang, telling reporters Monday that reinstating him "certainly isn't a bad idea." But Nvidia's board is not considering his return, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC. The person asked not to be named because they were discussing internal matters."

#2842. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2839. Robert Saleh: We've put more on Cam Ward's plate and he's proving he can handle it

The Titans lost their third straight game on Sunday, but head coach Robert Saleh continued to like what he saw from quarterback Cam Ward during Jacksonville's 40-23 win.

Ward was 28-of-39 for 231 yards, two touchdowns and an interception while running three times for 39 yards. That marked a season-high in completion percentage for Ward and he has completed at least 64.5 percent of his passes in all three of the losses.

That run coincides with what Saleh called a decision to "shift our philosophy with how we wanted to approach offense and put more in his plate with regard to being a general at the line of scrimmage." Saleh said that Ward has done "a really nice job taking command" and has shown the Titans what they were hoping to see in the process.

"To be fair to Cam, we've said from the beginning we were going to give him every opportunity to prove that he is a franchise quarterback of the Tennessee Titans," Saleh said at a Monday press conference. "So, philosophically, the change was: How are we going to evaluate whether or not is he capable of being our franchise quarterback if we don't give him the opportunity to prove it? And I think over the last three weeks he has done that. Or, at least is doing it."

The Titans scored 84 points in their first six games and they've put up 83 in the three games since the tweak in their offensive approach. That step in the right direction has not resulted in wins, but it is reason to hope that will change if Ward is able to sustain the improvement that the Titans have seen over the last few weeks.

#2836. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2833. Raiders TE Brock Bowers day-to-day with ankle injury that was 'not as bad' as feared, HC Klint Kubiak says

The Las Vegas Raiders appear to have averted a crisis: Star tight end Brock Bowers is day-to-day with an ankle injury, Raiders head coach Klint Kubiak told reporters on Monday. That's a much more favorable outcome for an injury that was initially feared to be serious.

Bowers exited Sunday's loss to the Seattle Seahawks early after appearing to suffer the injury during the first quarter. In Monday's press conference, Kubiak said that the team was "still evaluating" Bowers' injury, but that things were better than originally expected.

"It's not as bad as we thought, but still is a significant injury, so we'll keep monitoring him," Kubiak said.

He was ruled out just before the start of the second half.

The Raiders went on to lose the matchup 21-13 after the Seahawks scored two third-quarter touchdowns, behind an impressive performance from backup quarterback Drew Lock. Las Vegas quarterback Fernando Mendoza had some solid moments in his first NFL start, but couldn't pick up the win.

Bowers missed the first two games of the season after undergoing a meniscus trim. But since returning, he has been a crucial piece of the Raiders' offense: The tight end leads the team in receiving with 676 yards and six touchdowns.

If Bowers does end up out this weekend, it'll be a tough blow. The 4-6 Raiders are set to take on the Denver Broncos on Sunday in a big AFC West matchup.

#2829. James reaches assists milestone, keeps seeing plays ‘before they actually happen'

James reaches assists milestone, keeps seeing plays 'before they actually happen' originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Tyrese Maxey admitted that he wasn't entirely ready for LeBron James' first pass of the afternoon Sunday.

Maxey cut through the paint and James' bounce pass was perfect, but the Sixers' All-NBA guard missed his layup. He's not fully accustomed yet to James' precognitive gifts.

"There's still some times when you're surprised," Maxey said after the Sixers' win over the Pacers. "… I was cutting through just to cut and create space. The ball hit me in the chest and I missed the layup. I was like, 'Ah shoot, my bad.' He was like, 'Yeah, you weren't looking.'"

James has been trying to teach his teammates that he'll spot openings they may not notice.

"I just tell my guys, 'Always pay attention to me when I've got the ball,' because I definitely see plays develop before they happen," James said.

For the most part, James' teammates adhered to his request on the latest historic day in a milestone-filled career. By setting up VJ Edgecombe's layup with 1:19 left in the fourth quarter, James moved into third place on the NBA's all-time assists list, passing Jason Kidd.

The 22-time All-Star recorded 11 assists, his most so far as a Sixer, and now sits at 12,092 in his career. Chris Paul (12,552 assists) and John Stockton (15,806) are the only two players above James.

"It's obviously super humbling," James said. "It's something that I didn't foresee in my career, but I knew assists meant a lot to me. Just the movement of the ball, being able to find my teammates, putting the ball on time and on target.

"And also just being linked with some of the greats that have ever played this game and a dear friend of mine, J-Kidd. It's really special."

James is at or near the top of many all-time leaderboards. He's first in NBA history in points, games and minutes, sixth in steals and fifth in triple-doubles.

Passing has always been a core element of his game.

"First of all, he's got the vision and the awareness," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "He's got the size to see. I think he's got some anticipation. Just a half-second before a play happens, he knows how the defense is moving. … And he throws crisp passes. Some of those are cooking out to the corner three guys, so it doesn't let the close-out get there nearly as quickly."

Whether it's a long-range rocket, a drive-and-kick dish or a cross-court feed from the post that suggests he might have eyes in the back of his head, James has always enjoyed finding a wide range of ways to help his teammates.

"For someone that talented, he's very unselfish," Maxey said. "He really prides himself on getting guys open."

As James recalled Sunday, he had exceptional passing tools long before he entered the NBA 24 seasons ago.

"Since I was a kid, I've had the ability to see plays before they actually happen," he said. "Seeing movement, seeing cracks in the defense before they were even open … it's something that's been with me since I was a kid.

"I just had that vision and it just stuck with me throughout my playing days."
