#2826. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

Holdings are as of June 30, 2026, as reported in Berkshire Hathaway's 13F filing on August 14, 2026, except for:

The full list of holdings and current market values is available from CNBC.com's Berkshire Hathaway Portfolio Tracker.

#2824. Colts Look Even Smarter For Dumping DeForest Buckner After Slow 49ers Start

The Indianapolis Colts were wise to sell at the NFL trade deadline. While they certainly could have gone even further by trading Jonathan Taylor and Daniel Jones, they still sent a lot of guys to other teams in exchange for draft capital. One of the more obvious trades they made was to send veteran defensive tackle DeForest Buckner to the San Francisco 49ers in exchange for a fourth-round pick. That move looks even wiser given San Francisco's defensive struggles.

While the 49ers exploded for 46 points on offense against the Dallas Cowboys on Sunday, San Francisco's defense nearly gave the game away by allowing 40 points. After the game, Buckner said to the media: "Winning feels like a loss to a bunch of us," since the defense did not play well at all. The 49ers were hardly able to get any pressure on quarterback Dak Prescott the entire game and Buckner himself only had two quarterback pressures all game which suggests that the Buckner trade was not as immediately beneficial as San Francisco was hoping it would be.

Sunday was only Buckner's second game back with the Niners so it is far too early to say the move is a bust from San Francisco's perspective or anything like that. Maybe he just needs a little more time to get used to the defense the 49ers run but from the perspective of Indianapolis it seems like the Colts got rid of the veteran at the perfect time.

With him set to hit free agency after the season, there was really no reason to hold onto him especially since a reunion in free agency felt fairly unlikely given the fact that the Colts seem to be heading towards a rebuild. They got a fourth-round pick for him which may not have been the maximum they could have received, but the organization was classy enough to respect Buckner's wish to return to San Francisco where he started his NFL career.

Now, Indianapolis can use that pick to draft a replacement for Buckner or try to improve the team in some other way. It's pretty obvious that if the Colts are going to turn the ship around it is going to have to come from within which means they absolutely have to draft well.

The Colts have 10 draft picks in the 2027 NFL Draft and there is a chance they can get even more if they make more trades in the offseason. That puts them in a good position to get some young talent in the organization as they try to turn things around.

#2823. Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return

Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

PG-13 was rated R in his return to Philadelphia.

The middle finger Paul George directed toward one baseline fan only reinforced that rating.

Tuesday night was expected to revolve around Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum sharing an NBA court as opponents for the first time. Instead, the other headliner in the summer's blockbuster trade stole the show.

George scored a game-high 36 points in Boston's 123-101 win over the Sixers. He shot 14 for 22 from the floor, made six of his 11 three-point attempts and scored 14 consecutive Boston points in the fourth quarter.

The game was tied at 79 with 3:19 remaining in the third quarter. Boston outscored Philadelphia 44-22 from that point forward.

The Celtics went nine minutes and 20 seconds without making a two-point field goal during the second half. Instead, they made nine consecutive field goals from three, the second-longest such streak by a Sixers opponent over the last 30 seasons.

Payton Pritchard opened the fourth with consecutive threes. George followed with eight straight points, helping the Celtics begin the quarter on a 14-0 run that turned a competitive game into a rout.

George's return carried a complicated history. His two seasons with the Sixers were repeatedly interrupted by injuries, but he also played a significant role in their most memorable playoff run in years.

Last spring, George helped the Sixers overcome a 3-1 deficit to eliminate Boston in seven games. He averaged 17.4 points and shot 55 percent from three during the series.

Tyrese Maxey was happy to see his former teammate Tuesday, even if he disliked what followed.

"It was good to see Paul," Maxey said. "I love him, love his family. … He gave us everything he had while he was here. We know what he did in the playoffs last year. Great person, great family, and we'll see him again."

Maxey then added one important qualifier.

George instead scored from nearly everywhere.

"He had some open threes. He had some pull-up threes. He had some middies," Maxey said. "He played within the game, too. That's what Paul does. He plays within the game. He takes what the defense gives him, and we gave him too many open looks tonight.

"PG, we know what PG does. We shouldn't even be allowing him to get up 11 threes, and that's on us."

Nick Nurse watched George attack the Sixers in several ways.

"He really had it going," Nurse said. "He'd post up, and-one, fallaways. He went down the lane, high archer off the glass, and-one. He scored in about every way he could tonight."

George heard boos during his return and said the arena carried the atmosphere, though not quite the intensity, of a playoff game.

"This building can be very electric as well," George said. "It was fun. It was fun to play in this environment."

One interaction extended beyond the usual reception for a returning player.

During a stoppage, the ball rolled toward a fan seated along the baseline. George said the fan looked directly at him before dropping the ball instead of returning it.

"There was a fan being an a–," George said. "The ball rolled to him. He looked me dead in my eyes, dropped the ball on the ground as if I'm a dog to go fetch or go get it, instead of passing the ball to me like a normal fan would do.

"And so, yeah, then I rolled him a middle finger in return."

#2821. Raiders RB Ashton Jeanty Under Fire For ‘Letdown’ Season

The Las Vegas Raiders still haven't solved their run game issues. Against the Seattle Seahawks, they only had 65 yards on the ground.

Running back Ashton Jeanty rushed for 18 yards on nine carries. He's now averaging 3.1 yards per carry. The Raiders' offensive line has been atrocious at run blocking, but at some point, Jeanty needs to break away for a couple of big runs.

He was a top-10 draft pick, which is rare for a running back, so he hasn't come close to living up to expectations. The great running backs can overcome a bad offensive line, but Jeanty hasn't made any splash plays on the ground this season.

Even though he's still young in his career, CBS Sports' Carter Bahns has labeled Jeanty as a "letdown" this season.

"Jeanty was initially projected as a can't-miss running back prospect after his Heisman Trophy finalist senior campaign at Boise State, but he hasn't translated his dominant ball-carrying ability to the NFL," Bahns wrote. "His 41% rushing success rate as a rookie was as underwhelming as the 975 yards and five touchdowns he ran for in the Las Vegas Raiders' disaster of a season, and even though the team has taken a sizable step forward in 2026, Jeanty's production on the ground has regressed by a meaningful margin."

The Raiders made a bold decision to draft Jeanty before fixing the offensive line. In hindsight, they'd almost certainly change their strategy.

The hope was that Jeanty was so skilled that he'd be able to overcome substandard offensive line play, but that hasn't been the case. It might be too early to officially label Jeanty a draft pick, but he's getting dangerously close.

"The split between Jeanty and Washington wouldn't be uncalled for if the former were a mid-round pick," Bahns wrote. "But this is last year's No. 6 overall selection. The Raiders are not completely free of fault. The team averages a league-low 1.5 yards before contact per rushing attempt, the third-lowest mark of any team since Pro Football Reference began tracking the stat in 2018. According to NFL Next Gen Stats, the Raiders' 34 tackles for loss on called runs and 58 stuffed runs both lead the league through Week 10 (with the Ravens and Chargers still to play at the time of writing). However, it is evident Las Vegas is not getting their investment's worth from a player who was supposed to be a no-doubt bellcow back."

The Raiders have the second-worst running game in the NFL right now. It's going to be hard to win a lot of games when you can't run the ball, especially with a rookie quarterback.

Four of the Raiders' last seven opponents have top-10 run defenses, which doesn't help, but at some point, they need to have a good game on the ground. Klint Kubiak's offense is heavily reliant on the run, so not having that threat has limited the team. The Raiders may not be able to fix the rushing offense this season, but it's going to be a huge priority in the offseason.

#2820. Always one of a kind, Oubre reflects on Sixers stint, why he chose Pacers

Always one of a kind, Oubre reflects on Sixers stint, why he chose Pacers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Kelly Oubre Jr. experience switched sides Sunday afternoon at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Back in Philadelphia with the visiting Pacers, Oubre was his usual self. There's never any risk of mistaking Oubre for somebody else.

"Love him," Pacers head coach Rick Carlisle said pregame. "Such an upbeat guy. He's such a unique person. Love the wardrobe, all the different stuff. He's a very authentically one-of-a-kind type guy."

Carlise then detailed Oubre's various basketball qualities, some of which he showed in Indiana's 131-124 loss. The 30-year-old lefty wing scored 16 points and grabbed four rebounds. His day also included a theatrical imitation of Jaylen Brown's off-arm push after the Sixers' star was whistled for an offensive foul.

Sixers fans grew familiiar with Oubre's strengths, weaknesses and eccentricities during his three seasons as a Sixer. He wound up becoming the team's third-longest tenured player behind only Joel Embiid and Tyrese Maxey. Oubre played 178 regular-season games and averaged 14.9 points, 5.4 rebounds, 1.6 assists and 1.3 steals.

After the Sixers replaced president of basketball operations Daryl Morey with Mike Gansey, Oubre signed a two-year contract with the Pacers in free agency.

"Mike came over from Cleveland and I heard whispers that he wanted some of his Cleveland guys," Oubre said before the game in the visitors' locker room. "And I was like, 'OK, cool. I'm a Philly guy. So I guess when he comes over, I will no longer be a Philly guy.' That was kind of the situation at hand.

"No bad blood because business is business at the end of the day. They have a great team over there, and they put together a team that they think is the best for them. I'll be all right elsewhere. I figured it out."

Oubre thought Indiana made sense as his next stop.

"It was the pace, the culture, the attitude that this team has," he said. "It's a fast-paced style of basketball. It's a bunch of guys who work collectively as a unit and kind of figure it out from there.

"I thought that would be a great opportunity for me to come and and kind of free-flow play, not have to think. I was wrong. Been thinking a lot, but I'm starting to figure it out and starting to get my rhythm here. … It's a great culture. Coach Rick Carlisle is a legend, Hall of Fame coach. And they wanted me. I love people who want me."

As Oubre settled into a new city, the Sixers had a stunning, transformational summer.

Gansey traded for Jaylen Brown, signed LeBron James, and added veteran role players like (former Cavalier) Dean Wade, Anfernee Simons and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope. Oubre followed along.

"It was must-see TV, like (for) everybody else," he said. "As a basketball fan, it was pretty cool to see where I was kind of reinvent and get new life and new energy. So I was happy with that. Would I have loved to be a part of it? Of course.

"But at the end of the day, I'm a grown man and I've got to keep it moving and figure it out for myself. It was entertaining. … I'm kind of happy for all the people that have sacrificed over these years. … I'm trying to put a road block in that road for y'all."

Oubre joined the Sixers on a minimum-salary deal in September of 2023 after averaging 20.3 points the past season with the Hornets. Head coach Nick Nurse pitched Oubre on his potential to be a valuable, multi-dimensional player for good teams.

"I was literally about to be done with basketball," he said. "Coach Nurse called me multiple times and said, 'I want you to play for me.' And I was like, 'You know what? This guy really seems to care. He really wants me.' So thank you for just pulling me out of that pit and that hole that I was in four years ago. I got some new life in my basketball career.

"I'm always thankful for that and I love Nurse for that. Just appreciative for my time here. The battle wounds and scars that I've been able to build up, it made me a better man."

Throughout his Sixers stint, Oubre often spoke of growth, evolution, adaptation and self-discovery.

He talked again Sunday about his desire to "Be water" and how life as a Sixer changed him.

"I grew a lot, man," Oubre said. "Just the mental fortitude of being able to withstand adversity. Being able to wake up and not necessarily know which soliders are going to battle, just having to put on different hats on any given night. At first it was challenging, because I'm thinking about all the logistics and things and like, 'This is not what they asked me to do.' But at the end of the day, I just bought into being somebody that can be like water and grow with the team.

"It's been a blessing to be able to play with (VJ Edgecombe), be able to watch Tyrese (Maxey) ascend. Those are the guys where we were front line a lot. Just going through war with those guys was amazing. It just taught me mental fortitude. If I can make it through this, I can make it through anything."

Oubre felt he developed a meaningful connection with the Philadelphia fan base over his three years.

"When they talk about the City of Brotherly Love, I think they're talking about your soul, not necessarily about what you can offer them and what you do," he said. "Everybody in this city works hard. They wake up, go to work, keep the city going. And us as the prima donna basketball players, the least we can do is go hard and sacrifice for them so that they have something to cheer about when they come home from work.

"I think that was the relationship that we had. I'm forever indebted and forever grateful to the people in the city of Philadelphia."

#2817. Cooper Flagg on what it was like to play in front of Maine-heavy crowd vs. Boston Celtics

While the outcome of the game was about as far as one might want for one's ball club, the Boston Celtics' 131-94 drubbing of the Dallas Mavericks still enabled Cooper Flagg to show out in front of as close to a home crowd as the Maine native will get in an NBA arena as he put up 17 points, 3 rebounds, 4 assists, a steal, and a block for the Mavs at TD Garden Sunday (Nov. 15) night.

"I feel confident. I'm confident getting to my spots," said Flagg after the loss, who had a bit of a tough time connecting from those spots as Boston held him to 5-of-15 overall and Dallas to 40 percent from the floor overall. "I know the shots I want to get, make or miss. I'm happy with the result, and I know where I want to be on the court"

"It was great, always, being back in New England," said Flagg with a reference to his many fans who made the trip from Maine to see him play. "It's always a really warm welcome and a ton of support. So it's always a pleasure to be able to play in the New England region and see all the love and support."

"It's special," continued the rising Mavs star. "Like I said, just being able to come here and play and feel that love and support. Every time, it's going to be really special and it's not something I'm going to take for granted."

"So, it's such a blessing to have people that will show up and love and support me like that, and I'm not going to take it for granted."

#2816. Cowboys have no edge to beat 49ers, out-coached and outplayed again

Another year, another uncompetitive Dallas Cowboys defense that doesn't leave much left to play for in a losing season by mid-November. The box score will say the Cowboys lost by just six points to the San Francisco 49ers, but the full 60 minutes of football proved the now 8-1 49ers and now 4-6 Cowboys are simply in different competitive tiers for a wide facet of reasons.

Also for the second year in a row, the Cowboys scored exactly 40 points in a home game and did not win – giving up the most yardage they've ever allowed in a home game along the way to lose 46-40. In this spot against their other archnemesis the Green Bay Packers last year, the Cowboys managed a tie. That game still serves as the only non-loss on head coach Brian Schottenheimer's record against NFC teams that aren't the New York Giants, Washington Commanders, or Philadelphia Eagles in the NFC East, as this most recent week ten loss makes it 0-9-1 in such games.

The Cowboys run defense gave up 214 rushing yards, allowing at 7.4 yards a carry. Also for the third week in a row, the Cowboys faced exactly 16 third-down attempts on offense, a number that is too high. Dallas was lucky enough to win the penalty differential 11-5, with the 49ers having 65 more penalty yards against them, but it ultimately did not matter.

It will be a busy week for the Cowboys as they turn the page from this game, preparing for two winnable games next week on Sunday and Thanksgiving Thursday. Let's get to some notes on how they fell short against the 49ers, which makes both games next week feel damper, as the team will be fighting to be .500 at best.

It will be a wash, rinse, and repeat week for Brian Schottenheimer and the Cowboys coaching staff as they had their fingerprints all over yet another loss, and the criticism will surely follow. This Cowboys loss to the 49ers was hardly an outlier though, as we know it was their fifth straight spanning back to the 2021-22 playoffs.

One thing the Cowboys have not been nearly good enough at doing at any point over this entire stretch of time is recognizing individual matchups they can take advantage of and scheming to do so on a consistent basis. This one sentence could be the mission statement for the Kyle Shanahan 49ers, and while the Cowboys have shuffled the coaching and personnel against Shanahan constantly, it's Kyle that continues to come out with the wins.

This was evident even in the way both teams got their first points of the game in the first quarter, as part of a bigger picture first half where the Cowboys put up a good fight just to get worn down and exposed in the second. The Cowboys needed a roughing the passer call from their own former defensive tackle Osa Odighizuwa to move the chains on the opening drive, and were held to a field goal without completing a pass.

The 49ers answered quickly with their first of six touchdown drives on the game. With the Cowboys having both DaRon Bland and Joey Porter Jr. on the field against a hobbled group of 49ers wide receivers, the matchups on the outside actually had a chance to tilt in Dallas' favor. Shanahan's response to this was a masterclass, as he found the Cowboys own hobbled position group in the backend which was safety, and went after them.

George Kittle drew a holding penalty against Caleb Downs to negate a sack, and then Kittle went to work for the first of several times against backup safety Markquese Bell. This was a matchup dominated by Kittle, as he took a screen pass for 20 yards one play before the touchdown to Deebo Samuel on another screen. Malik Hooker was the other safety out of position for the first of what was also many times this game on the touchdown, and the 49ers made it a point to test the tackling of the entire Cowboys defense, especially in the secondary. This was a test Christian Parker's defense failed.

Parker's defense did get Porter Jr. matched up against Kittle for a key third-down stop on the ensuing 49ers possession to earn a three and out, but the scary trend of the Cowboys offense not rising to the occasion of scoring, or even holding onto the ball when their defense does get a stop, reared it's ugly head again. The Cowboys promptly went three and out themselves with an incomplete pass to Lamb on third down.

The best singular example of the Cowboys making offense look as easy as it came for the 49ers in this game was on George Pickens' second touchdown, the slant pass that always seems to work yet isn't called often enough. Pickens' touchdown could have put his team up two scores if Quinnen Williams finished a sack on 3rd-and-12 on the previous 49ers drive, something he made up for by getting a red zone sack to keep the 49ers out of the end zone in the red area – something that's been nearly impossible for defenses this season.

Instead, the door was left open for the 49ers to respond again, and the defense couldn't get Brock Purdy on the ground again. Extending the play, he hit Christian McCaffrey for his first backbreaking touchdown of 74 yards, and just like that the 49ers were up 17-16.

Another third-down incompletion to Lamb put the defense back on the field one last time before halftime, getting the desperation stop to preserve the one-point deficit. Only trailing by one at halftime could still be seen as a win considering how tough the 49ers are as a matchup for the Cowboys, but the way it came about with both the offense and defense being stressed more than needed planted the seed for the second half runaway.

The second half felt like a runaway because Shanahan dug even deeper into his bag of tricks to dictate the terms of this game to the Cowboys defense, getting a Deebo Samuel 70-yard reverse touchdown to go up 31-19. The ultimate dagger came with McCaffrey matched up against Von Miller for a 66-yard touchdown to go back up two scores late at 46-32.

Everything the Cowboys did offensively to storm back late felt empty, hollow, and like a rerun of a bad movie we've seen too many times before anyway, but seeing McCaffrey sprint right down the middle of the field untouched to put it away might be the image of the season for why the Cowboys still are where they are.

One other thing that proved itself out on the scoreboard along the way of Shanahan calling a better game for his offense compared to Schottenheimer for the Cowboys offense was the mobility of Brock Purdy compared to Dak Prescott.

Purdy was comfortable and in control almost all game, though the Cowboys did get to him for five sacks. His scramble ability was a real difference maker though, and came naturally for big play after big play as he completed 78.6% of his passes for 13.9 yards per attempt – a career high.

Compare this to yet another game where things felt laborious for the Cowboys offense for long stretches, and like the weight of the world was on Dak Prescott's shoulders again too, and the home team not being able to rely on Dak's mobility makes it an even sadder state of affairs.

Purdy's scrambles were unscripted plays that should be the equivalent of "50/50 balls" in basketball, but it was more like "90/10" for the visitors in week ten. Prescott's throws from outside the pocket were forced and off-target. Some were scripted plays that served as more examples of Schottenheimer being in over his head, and ultimately failing this franchise QB.

Wasted season after wasted season is becoming the story of Prescott's career, his mobility has not been a true strength of his game for some time, and now it just feels like yet another thing that's come and gone without the Cowboys getting anything out of it.

In all of those ways this loss felt familiar, but it did make history for Prescott as it was the first of his career with five touchdowns. It was also just the second loss in his career losing with four or more touchdowns, the other one also coming at home. Prescott was 0-5 targeting CeeDee Lamb in the first half, as his consistency getting involved in these games mirrors the Cowboys win-one-lose-one-win-one pattern. Meanwhile, Purdy was completing passes to Keenan Allen who just showed up to the 49ers on Wednesday after the trade deadline.

One team had it easy at AT&T Stadium on Sunday, and it was the one with more fan support in the stands, and one team that actually calls that building home had it hard. The Cowboys are now 7-13-1 from 2024 to now at home.

#2814. Observations after Sixers shake off slow start, Embiid injury scare to win

Observations after Sixers shake off slow start, Embiid injury scare to win originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers brushed off their blowout loss to the Celtics and got back in the win column Thursday night.

They overcame a first-quarter deficit as high as 17 points and earned a 123-112 home win over the Raptors. The Sixers moved to 10-3 and Toronto fell to 5-7.

Tyrese Maxey had 27 points and eight assists. Joel Embiid added 20 points.

Jaylen Brown tallied 18 points and six rebounds. VJ Edgecombe posted 15 points, eight rebounds and a season-high eight assists. Anfernee Simons scored 17 points and shot 5 for 6 from three-point range.

RJ Barrett recorded 31 points and 14 rebounds. Immanuel Quickley had 26 points and six assists.

The Sixers were without LeBron James (low back/sciatica management), Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain), Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear).

The team told reporters Thursday morning that Bona will be re-evaluated in approximately one week and that Barlow participated in shootaround. The 23-year-old forward also went through a pregame workout on the Xfinity Mobile Arena floor. There's no formal timetable for his return.

Toronto's sidelined players included Kawhi Leonard and Scottie Barnes.

The Sixers will face the Pacers on Sunday afternoon in Philadelphia. Here are observations on their victory over the Raptors:

Dean Wade began the game in James' normal spot. The Sixers started slowly, missing their first foul field goals and opening 1 for 8 from three-point range.

He stayed down after setting a ball screen and having an apparent knee-to-knee collision with Ja'Kobe Walter. The Sixers called timeout with 8:30 left in the first quarter and Embiid grabbed at his right knee. He eventually rose with assistance from teammates and Sixers head athletic trainer Kevin Johnson, then limped back to the locker room. Jabari Walker entered.

At the 5:26 mark of the first quarter, Embiid checked back in to cheers from the home fans.

"I knew that he didn't twist it or anything," Maxey said. "I knew it was a knee-to-knee, so I wasn't too worried. He kept saying, 'I just got kneed.' That still hurts, though."

Embiid ended up logging 28 minutes, though he certainly wanted to play more.

"He was very, very motivated to play," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "It was hard to get him out for his scheduled rests tonight, which is a good problem sometimes."

While Embiid's return was a major positive development, the Sixers fell further behind. The Raptors' lead swelled to 32-16 with a Jamison Battle three-pointer and Nurse burned a timeout.

Simons sunk a buzzer-beating three to conclude the first quarter, cutting the Sixers' deficit to nine points.

"Those are big momentum plays that usually have some weight to them and carry you into the next moment," Nurse said.

The Sixers kept rolling against a shorthanded Toronto team playing on the second night of a back-to-back. Simons nailed a triple to kick off the scoring in the second quarter. Brown buried a mid-range jumper and Edgecombe jammed in a crowd-pleasing dunk to give the Sixers a 35-34 edge.

Brown and Edgecombe shined in the second quarter both as individuals and as a duo. The Sixers need Edgecombe to attack a bit more offensively when he shares the floor with Brown and bench players. It doesn't hurt for the second-year guard to have that clarity about his role in those units.

"He's one of our better playmakers," Maxey said of Edgecombe. "He's really good at getting downhill and creating for himself and others."

The Sixers' only three bench rotation players were Walker, Simons and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope (11 points, five assists). In addition to Walker, Wade got center minutes behind Embiid.

Embiid picked up his third foul with 2:23 left in the second quarter and had to take a seat. The teams were tied at 60 apiece through two periods.

The game remained even for much of the third, but Maxey fueled an 11-0 Sixers run late in the quarter.

His speed and the Sixers' collective focus on pushing the pace whenever possible allowed the team to get regular points before Toronto could set its defense. Maxey zoomed in for a layup, then tossed the ball ahead to Brown for an easy bucket. Brown assisted a Simons three. Walker converted a fast-break lay-in and canned a three on the first possession of the fourth quarter. All of a sudden, the Sixers held a 101-87 lead.

Brown, Edgecombe and the bench gave the Sixers more good minutes early in the fourth quarter. They sealed the deal without any late drama. And the fans chanting for a Tacko Fall appearance got their wish with a final-minute entrance by the 7-foot-6 center.

The highest-volume contract is currently the 30-strike call expiring Friday, a 90-cent contract that needs the fund to rally 19% into the weekend to pay off.Traders may want to be wary of how the fund traded around the SpaceX IPO: DXYZ surged almost 150% in May before unwinding all the gains in the weeks ahead of SpaceX's debut. Since then, the fund has barely moved, trading around $26.There are other bullish signs surrounding Anthropic's IPO in the derivatives markets. Current prices for perpetual futures on the blockchain exchange Hyperliquid are implying an opening price of $98, a big pop from the $70-$78 price range quoted in the company's amended S1 filing. The AI giant is expected to price Wednesday evening and begin trading on the Nasdaq on Thursday under the ticker ANT.

#2805. ‘Hard to win': Simons, Walker carry Portland lessons into Sixers roles

'Hard to win': Simons, Walker carry Portland lessons into Sixers roles originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Anfernee Simons and Jabari Walker shared the floor for 128 games in Portland. The Trail Blazers won 43 of them.

They sat beside one another in the Sixers' interview room Thursday night after a victory and discussed what the other 85 taught them.

"Whether you've got a big role or a small role, it's hard to win in the NBA," Simons said. "We've seen it firsthand."

Their roles were considerably different then.

Simons was an established starter entrusted with creating and scoring. Walker, a 2022 second-round pick, was trying to establish himself in a rotation. During their three overlapping seasons, Portland went 33-49, 21-61 and 36-46. The Blazers missed the playoffs each year.

Both are now reserves on a Sixers team constructed to contend. In nine games they've both appeared in this season, Philadelphia is 7-2.

They provided 26 points, 11 rebounds and six three-pointers in the Sixers' 123-112 win over the Raptors at Xfinity Mobile Arena. Simons scored 17 points and shot 5 for 6 from three-point range. Walker recorded nine points and eight rebounds, including four on the offensive glass.

The Sixers outscored Toronto by 24 points during Simons' 23 minutes. Neither former Blazer committed a turnover.

Their production helped Philadelphia overcome a 17-point first-quarter deficit and improve to 10-3 without LeBron James.

"You learn once you come off the bench to kind of read the game before you get in there," Simons said. "Obviously, we started off kind of slow. You just want to go in, bring some energy, get some stops, get out and run. I think everybody who got off the bench brought energy."

Simons supplied an immediate lift with a deep three-pointer at the first-quarter buzzer. His shot ended an 8-0 Sixers run and trimmed Toronto's lead to nine.

He remained involved during an 11-0 burst late in the third quarter, knocking down a three and setting up Walker for a basket. Walker then made a three early in the fourth before Simons scored on the Sixers' next possession.

When Toronto pulled within six with less than five minutes remaining, Simons answered with his fifth three-pointer.

Making those shots is the most obvious way Simons can help a roster already featuring James, Joel Embiid, Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown. He said the Sixers have not made him feel as though every opportunity must go in.

"They encourage us to keep shooting the ball," Simons said. "Knowing that a team has that much confidence in us to keep shooting makes the game that much easier. Whether you miss or make, as long as we get the right shots in the right moments, I think everybody can live with the misses and makes."

Simons spent seven seasons in Portland before playing last year with Boston and Chicago. His move to Philadelphia reunited him with Walker and Rayan Rupert, another former Blazer who joined the Sixers on a two-way contract during the offseason.

Knowing familiar faces awaited him helped Simons enter another new locker room.

"It made the transition seamless," he said. "Just being able to have some comfortability with guys that I played with for multiple years. It's been fun, kind of feeling like a little bit of old times. Me, Bari and Rups have been through a lot."

Walker's path to Philadelphia began a year earlier and required an additional step.

After playing 188 games over three seasons with Portland, Walker signed a two-way contract with the Sixers in July 2025. He earned a standard contract in February and has since pushed his way into the backup-center conversation despite standing 6-foot-7.

Walker believes some of the work he did on losing teams was easily missed.

"When I first got to the league, I was part of a rebuilding process," he said. "I just felt like some of the little things I did didn't always get noticed when you're losing. I told myself, if I ever got to be part of a winning organization, then I'd go out and expand my abilities."

Rebounding has made Walker difficult for Nick Nurse to overlook.

Walker credited his timing and strong hands to lessons passed down by his father, former NBA center Samaki Walker. Those instincts have become especially useful while the Sixers piece together their frontcourt behind Embiid.

"Rebounding is a skill I kind of honed in on," Walker said. "My dad was a good rebounder. He was known for his rebounding and his timing. I think it's just a gift that he transferred to me."

Walker has made it clear that he expected to earn a role this season.

"I don't know if I was delusional, but I always felt like I was going to play and I was going to be a part," he said. "I read something where they said it's a surprise, but if you were around me this summer, it wouldn't be a surprise. This is expected, and I just want to continue to get better."

The freedom Simons once had in Portland is no longer necessary. Walker does not need a high volume of touches, either. Both understand that playing beside the Sixers' stars requires shooting when open, moving the ball and handling the less glamorous work.

"You've got to find ways to impact the game multiple ways, even if you're not scoring," Simons said. "Whether it's cutting, making the right read and pass to Joel, being the right swing pass to VJ, boxing out on defense or just being in the right position."

Their opportunities will fluctuate. Simons may take nine shots one night and only a few the next. Walker's minutes will depend on matchups and the health of Philadelphia's frontcourt.

Winning has changed what they are being asked to do. Their three years together in Portland taught them why the adjustment is worthwhile.

"You feel appreciative of the times where you go into a situation where your role might be a little bit less, but you're winning," Simons said. "That's something you can't take for granted."

#2804. Sandy Brondello reacts to Tempo winning No. 1 pick in 2027 WNBA Draft lottery

Sandy Brondello reacts to Tempo winning No. 1 pick in 2027 WNBA Draft lottery appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

With her daughter beside her in Phoenix, Sandy Brondello celebrated Sunday as the Toronto Tempo secured the No. 1 pick in the 2027 WNBA Draft.

Speaking the day after the lottery, Brondello described her reaction to learning Toronto would have the first selection as the franchise prepares for its second season.

Toronto entered the lottery tied with the Houston Comets, formerly the Connecticut Sun, for the best chance of securing the top pick at 33% each. The Tempo won that selection after finishing their inaugural season with an 11-33 record.

Brondello said the celebration at home caught the attention of more than just her daughter.

"You know, there's a lot of screaming in my house. My dogs were very stunned," Brondello said. "They weren't sure what was going on and why we're screaming."

Toronto's roster includes Marina Mabrey, Brittney Sykes and Kiki Rice. The first selection gives the Tempo another opportunity to add to that group as they continue building the franchise.

According to ESPN's Alexa Philippou, Toronto is the first franchise to hold the No. 1 pick entering its second season since the Atlanta Dream in 2009. Atlanta selected Angel McCoughtry with that pick.

Houston received the second selection in Sunday's lottery in New York. The Washington Mystics will pick third, followed by the Seattle Storm and Portland Fire.

#2800. Sixers, Brown have a ‘humbling' night in loss to Celtics

Sixers, Brown have a 'humbling' night in loss to Celtics originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Whatever Jaylen Brown and the Sixers dreamed up for their first matchup against the Celtics soon flew out the window on Tuesday night.

Brown was whistled for two early fouls, the Sixers fell behind and their early-season flaws were on full display in a 123-101 loss at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

"We've just got to get better," Brown said. "Tonight was humbling. We got outplayed, out-executed. So we go back to the drawing board and get ready for the next one."

Over 26 minutes, Brown had 13 points on 4-for-9 shooting and five rebounds against the team that traded him away this summer.

As for the endless dissection of his relationship with former co-star Jayson Tatum, Brown didn't have much to add Tuesday. There was no public reconciliation, no obvious on-court animosity and generally nothing to satisfy fans hoping for some kind of fireworks on that front.

"I'm just focused on doing what's best for my team," Brown said. "Everything else … to be honest, I wasn't paying attention. But credit to Jayson; he played well tonight. Credit to the Celtics. They executed, they shot the hell out of the ball and they were the better team tonight."

Tatum scored 21 points, two of which came on a third-quarter slam over LeBron James. Paul George continued his fantastic start as a Celtic by dropping 36 points and draining six three-pointers.

The Celtics dominated from beyond the arc, shooting 22 for 49 (44.9 percent). The Sixers went just 11 for 37 (29.7 percent) on another night of underwhelming offense. The team sits 22nd in the NBA in offensive rating, 24th in three-point makes per game and 21st in three-point percentage.

"I think that we've got some guys that are obviously shooting much lower percentages than their last season or their career average — almost a handful of guys," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "And I don't think it's because they're shooting fallaway, totally contested ones. I think they're shots we want them to take. So I think they'll continue to take 'em and I'm pretty sure those will even out into their career averages."

Joel Embiid is at 37 percent from the floor and 4 for 33 from three-point range (12.1 percent).

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center got through an injury scare when he rolled his ankle late in the second quarter and hit a three early in the third. It was his first long-range make since the Sixers' home opener back on Oct. 22.

"It's a work in progress," Embiid said. "I think the biggest thing is just not thinking. I'm probably overthinking it. The rhythm of the game is different. I'm so used to having to create for myself, so it's a little bit different.

"But I think I'm slowly getting better. You could see as soon as I made a three, the next shot in the short corner … I didn't even think about it. I just shot it and let it fly, and I made it. … I think it's just about taking what's in front of you. Make or miss, keep working on your game. It's going to get there at some point."

Even with the Sixers second in the Eastern Conference standings at 9-3, Embiid believes the team's defense has "slacked off a little bit" in recent games.

"I don't really look at the record," he said. "I just look at the process of, 'Are we getting better every day?' We can always do a better job at all phases of the game."

"They're one of the top teams in the league and they've been together for a while," Brown said. "We've got a lot to work on over here, so we've got to get to work."

The Sixers will face the Raptors on Thursday night in Philadelphia.

They'll have to wait until Jan. 21 in Boston to see the Celtics again.

"I was looking forward to this," Brown said. "I was looking forward to playing well, but s— happens. Just get ready for the next one."

#2799. Maryland men’s basketball overwhelms Alcorn State in 117-57 beatdown

Through three games, it appeared Maryland men's basketball had a 3-point problem. The Terps had shot 21.5% from beyond the arc, the 357th-best mark in Division I.

Against Alcorn State on Monday, the Terps eased those fears, draining 11 first-half 3-pointers — including seven in a row — en route to a 117-57 blowout of the Braves. Maryland's 117 points were the most in a game since December 2000.

Against Virginia, it took until the first-half clock expired for Maryland's second 3-pointer to land. Against Alcorn State, it took 68 seconds.

Mike McNair Jr., restored to the starting lineup, opened the game with a make. Freshman Austin Brown, starting in his first game of the season, splashed another on the Terps' second offensive possession.

Both Brown and McNair were recalled into the starting lineup in favor of Andre Mills, who had struggled in the Terps' two losses. The guard's 6-of-28 slump visibly impacted his body language and confidence on the court.

But even Mills got in on Monday's fun. Just under eight minutes into the game, he peeled into the corner late in the shot clock, caught a pass from Baba Oladotun and heaved up a shot. It bounced off the front end of the rim and up, floating down through the net to break a streak of 10 consecutive misses from beyond the arc.

The redshirt sophomore made another 3-pointer on the Terps' next possession, too. He finished the game with 15 points on 4-of-6 shooting and three assists.

From the opening tip, head coach Buzz Williams took the opportunity to rotate his team, presenting a variety of combinations. That left the Terps out of position defensively, which Alcorn State used to its advantage by drawing fouls.

While the Braves went just 3-of-15 from the field to open the game, 7-of-8 free throw shooting kept the game within seven points just before the midpoint of the first half.

From there, though, the Terps exhausted their opponents. Alcorn State switched from man to zone defense, and Maryland capitalized, firing the ball around the perimeter until someone got open. That left DJ Wagner, who had 14 points, wide open at the top of the key to drain Maryland's seventh successive 3-point attempt.

The Terps finished the first half with 60 points, their most in an opening frame since 2000.

Maryland's deep shooting slowed after the break, but the scoring continued thanks to steady interior pressure. Pharrel Payne finished with 12 points against an Alcorn State frontcourt that logged 12 fouls trying to contain him and the rest of the Terps.

However, fouls were a frequent occurrence for both teams. The Braves finished the game with 29, but the Terps' 23 infractions were not far behind.

Kaden House's turnaround fadeaway near the free-throw line marked Maryland's 98th point, setting a new record for its highest total under Williams. Mills made it 100 on the fast break, collecting an outlet pass and rising high above the defender for an easy layup.

In a late cameo, Guillermo Del Pino finished with 11 points and four rebounds in eight minutes. His late snipe also made it 15 3-pointers on the evening for the Terps, which tied for the third-most in a game in Maryland history..

1. Sharing the scoring. Maryland's 117 points came despite no one player scoring more than 16 points. Six Terps finished in double figures on the day, and the only Terps to play and not score were Maban Jabriel and Alex K'Medehouto, who both entered the game late on.

2. Quick on the switches. Across the entire game, Maryland made 49 substitutions. Twice, Williams opted for a full hockey line change, bringing five fresh faces back into the fold.

3. Faulty on free throws. While the Terps earned several opportunities from the charity stripe, they made just 67% of them as a team against Alcorn State. Some players have shown a greater cause for concern this year than others: this year, Oladotun is 1-of-7, and Wagner is 4-of-8.

#2797. Tottenham reportedly reserve matching right clause to re-sign Kane from Bayern

According to a report by Matt Law in the Telegraph, Tottenham Hotspur still retain an option to re-sign Harry Kane.

Even after his recent contract extension with Bayern Munich, the London club apparently have a route to sign the Ballon d'Or winner.

The clause is a “matching right”, which means that Spurs can match any bid made on Kane should FC Bayern ever decide to sell him.

So, in theory if a club offer a €80million for Kane in 2028, Spurs could match it and Bayern would be legally bound to sell to the latter.

This clause goes back to the original deal and transfer fee negotiated between Bayern Munich and Daniel Levy back in 2023.

However, it's a clause that is likely to end up irrelevant, as every sign indicates that Harry Kane will finish his top level career at Bayern.

#2796. Rams’ injuries provide silver lining of depth chart changes

Using a punishing ground attack, the Los Angeles Rams methodically overwhelmed the Arizona Cardinals 34-6 in Sunday's Week 10 matchup. L.A. overcame another slow start, trailing the Cards well into the second quarter before the offense came alive to the tune of 436 yards. With injuries stacking up, the Rams offense was forced to improvise, both in new formations and personnel groupings.

Sets and names that Rams fans were not familiar with.

Head coach Sean McVay gave Arizona a steady diet of three tight end formations, but also mixed in two running back sets split on each side of QB Matthew Stafford's shot gun and he used a handful of jumbo blocking packages with six offensive linemen. It was bully ball and the Rams pounded out 200 yards on the ground.

The Rams wide receiver unit, 'The Island of Misfit Toys" edition, stood up quite well. Konata Mumpfield (48 reps), rookie CJ Daniels (46), Tutu Atwell (16), and the recent return from exile, Xavier Smith (9) all made plays. Running back Ronnie Rivers (17) chipped in 81 rushing yards. On the offensive line, Beaux Limmer (76) got the start at guard and after the Rams lost not one, but two centers, he moved into the pivot. Bill Murray (49) jumped in at guard, his first regular season action since Week 5 of 2024.

Is relying on a group of lesser-knowns down the stretch sustainable?

Here's a review of the snap counts from Sunday. Players, their snap counts and percentage of play time in bold.

Started out cold, but got into the groove as the second quarter progressed. A good job overall, but another bad interception needs to be noted. Staff feeds off emotion and excitement, the new offensive changes seemed to energize him. I didn't see him shooting the finger pistols, but he clearly was playing with emotion.

L.A. trotted out two running back formations on 17 plays and ironically, threw the football on every one of them. The Rams still ran for 200+ yards. Over this four-game win streak, the Rams have out-gained their opponents by nearly 450 yards on the ground. Corum gets the highest snap count, play time percentage, and number of touches in his career. To close out the final 12 minutes of the game, Rivers humped the rock on 15 consecutive plays.

Coming into the game with 261 total receiving yards amongst them, the Rams young corps booked 174 for Sunday's game alone. Even better was reeling in 70 percent of targets. Time and again, they found open areas, whether by scheme, created on their own, or the Cardinals ineptitude. All had catches for nice gains. Atwell's dropped pass near the goal line, may let Smith sneak by him on the depth chart.

L.A. used three tight ends on 63.5 percent of reps, 47 plays, running the football on 40 of them. They weren't just pack horses carrying the run game load, they combined to haul in six of seven passes and notch two touch downs.

Beaux Limmer, Kevin Dotson, Warren McClendon (76, 100%)

When you can pound out 46 rushing attempts, you've had a good showing by the big uglies. L.A. used Quessenberry as a sixth lineman on a handful of snaps, something fans have wanted to see. Poor McMahon, he gets a shot at his first extended action since Week 18 of the 2024 season and gets hurt. Shelton has had a solid season, it will be a big loss if he's out for extended time. It looked like he tweaked a hamstring getting out on a second quarter screen play. Limmer showed his versatility, it was his style of game, a slobberknocker run fest. Murray played fair to well, considering it was his first action since Week 5 of 2024 and cold call to boot.

Backup cornerback Damarion Williams rates a shout out, he's been a practice squad elevation in the two games since being signed. He's had a kick coverage tackle in both. Really gets after it and plays with hustle and emotion. Hard to say what the Rams will do with him once his three PS elevations are up, but if he stacks another good game after the bye, it will be a tough decision.

Dominating performance from the first line of defense. On only 36 defensive snaps, they allowed a paltry 34 yards rushing between the tackles and logged 10 tackles with 3.5 sacks. In support work, Hamilton has been getting good push in the past few games and Murchison got his hand up to knock down a short pass. With opposing teams forced into the short, quick passing attack, I'd like to see more hands up in the passing lanes.

While Garrett was quiet, Young stacked another solid outing. Stewart's time on the field has been trending steadily down and his production has run hot and cold. Bailey is still in the learning process, but if/when he can match game savvy with his athletic traits and effort, the Rams will have a keeper.

Interesting happenings here. Landman's snaps and play percentage have gone down for three straight games. It could be that he's been nicked up a lot this season, but worth watching. Most of reps for Dolac and Speights came late, after the Rams had put 31 points on the board. Dolac hasn't received a huge increase, but has double-digit reps in three of the last four games and produced well. Speights' play time is trending down ever so slightly over the past three weeks, but production has been very consistent.

If you're only giving up 108 yards passing, the secondary had a solid day. Two plays accounted for nearly half of that total. One for 21 yards into the soft area of a two-deep zone and Lake got burnt in man coverage for a 31-yard explosive. Lake hasn't been lining up much in the slot, about 15 percent of the time, and even less on wide receivers. The Cards caught him on one of those times. One thing that stood out was McCollough's snap counts and play percentage, down substantially from the past four games.

It feels as if the Rams have endured a long, arduous journey getting to 6-4, but have a grasp, however tenuous, of a wildcard seed. Now they've earned 10 days to scab up and get heads straight for the stretch drive. You cannot overplay, over state, or over hype the importance of the Thanksgiving Eve tilt with the Green Bay Packers. It is a necessary first step for a successful conclusion to the season.

There is reason for optimism. On offense, Sean McVay broke away from his standard approach. Yes, still controlled and methodical, but just enough of a tweak to freshen things up. Establishing a run game early, using an extra offensive lineman to gain a numbers advantage, lining up two running backs, and attacking the whole field, both vertical and horizontal. It's vintage McVay, just updated packaging. Defensively, L.A. has been, excepting the Buffalo Bills game, stingy versus both run and pass. Getting Aaron Donald and Myles Garrett together for 40-45 reps per game is close to a sure winning bet and the secondary likes the mixing in of man coverage with all the zone.

Although the Cards are a struggling team and L.A. would have likely won with the old ways, McVay put the Rams stretch opponents on notice, neither the offense or defense can be put into a box. Even if the offense goes back to Nacua/Adams pass-centric ways, opposing teams must account and game plan for the Rams new-look, multi-faceted offensive attack.

Shelton, a backup lineman on the Rams' Super Bowl championship team five years ago, hasn't missed a game because of an injury since 2022. He spent the 2024 season with the Chicago Bears, starting all 17 games before returning to the Rams as a free agent.

Los Angeles (6-4) has won four straight games, but has also lost several key players to injury.

The Rams are still expected to be without star receivers Davante Adams and Puka Nacua for the rest of the month. Nacua will miss at least three more games on injured reserve, while Adams isn't quite ready to return from his hamstring injury.

The Rams are in their truncated bye week before they host the Green Bay Packers on Nov. 25.

#2794. The extensive officiating adjustments worked, but were they necessary?

Friday's nationwide ground stop didn't affect any of the NFL teams that needed to travel from one place to the other for Week 10 games. It dramatically affected the officiating assignments.

But not because the officials assigned to work the various games couldn't get to where they needed to be. The league decided to reshuffle the deck in order to avoid the mere possibility that the officials wouldn't get to their assigned games.

It was a proactive approach by the notoriously reactive NFL. But it still may have been an overreaction. And it introduced the risk that something unusual could have occurred.

For the twelve games played on Sunday, the assigned referee worked only two of the games. One of those games was played on Sunday morning (New York time) in Germany, where travel undoubtedly occurred before Friday's shutdown of the aviation system.

Sunday night's game was due to be worked by Clete Blakeman as the referee, with umpire Scott Walker, down judge Andy Warner, line judge Kent Payne, field judge Karina Tovar, side judge James Coleman, back judge Jonah Monroe, replay official Chad Adams, and replay assistant Adam Choate.

Instead, from the official Game Book, this was the crew: referee Don Willard, umpire Terry Killens, down judge Dana McKenzie, line judge Jay Bilbo, field judge Jabir Walker, side judge Justin Larrew, back judge Dino Paganelli.

Chad Adams was still the replay official; however, all replay officials reportedly went to the league office and not their game sites.

Willard was not scheduled to work in Week 10; he ultimately served as the referee for two games. Killens was due to work the Jaguars-Titans game as a member of referee Alex Moore's crew; like Willard, Killens worked Dolphins-Colts and Steelers-Bengals. McKenzie, like Killens, is a member of Moore's crew, which was due to be in Nashville.

Bilbo is a member of referee Shawn Smith's crew, which was due to work Panthers-Saints. Walker, who also worked two games, is a member of Land Clark's crew, which was scheduled to work the Chargers-Ravens game on Monday night.

Larrew, the fourth two-games-in-one-day official, is assigned to referee Shawn Hochuli's crew, which was scheduled to work the Chiefs-Falcons game. Paganelli is also a member of referee Shawn Smith's crew, which was due to work Panthers-Saints.

Monday night's game, by the way, will be handled by referee Adrian Hill. Hill's crew was scheduled to work the Rams-Cardinals game on Sunday.

While we don't have the time, the patience, or frankly the desire to prepare a full list of the crews that were supposed to handle the 11 domestic Sunday games and the crews that actually did, the fact that 10 of the 11 referees ended up elsewhere says it all.

Here's the real question. Were they necessary? In lieu of performing major surgery to the officiating lineup for 11 Sunday games (and, apparently, the Monday night game), the NFL could have committed to getting every official where they needed to be, for the games they were scheduled to work.

Everyone else got to where they needed to be. The teams, the broadcasters, everyone. It presumably could have been done, if the NFL had decided to commit any and all resources to making it happen.

Which brings us to an inescapable conclusion. In order to avoid the hassle — and the expense — of ensuring that the officiating crews made it to their assigned locations before kickoff on Sunday, the league had no qualms about making massive, eleventh-hour changes to the assignments. Which included the unprecedented move of requiring four officials to work a game, to take a two-hour ride to another city, and to work another game.

For the details on working two games in one day, check out the story from Kalyn Kahler about the unprecedented doubleheader for four officials.

"It's exhausting because you're looking for something on every single play," ESPN rules analyst and former NFL V.P. of replay Russell Yurk told Kahler regarding the difficulty of working two games in a day. "You're analyzing every single play. You're watching your players. You're watching your keys, you're making judgment calls, and it doesn't really let up the entire game."

Fox rules analyst and former NFL senior V.P. of officiating Dean Blandino called assigning officials to two games a "last resort."

"[I]t would be like playing two football games back-to-back," Blandino told Kahler. "There's the physical aspect of running and the mental exhaustion after a game because you are doing a pre-snap routine for every snap, and then the eight-second play, and then a post-snap routine.

"The officials look like they are not exerting themselves the same way the players are, but they don't get breaks. They don't play one side of the ball. They are out there every snap and every kick. Then to say now I have to reset without any recovery time? That is a challenge."

The league could have embraced the challenge (and the expense) of getting all officials where they needed to be. It surely would have been easier than completely disrupting the existing assignments, forcing officials to work games they hadn't prepared to handle, and requiring NFL executive Ramon George (whom the league declined to make available for an interview with Kahler) to move the various pieces around in a way that made it all work, even if the "last resort" of using four officials for two games had to be used.

Given the wealth and power of the NFL (most if not all owners have a private jet, or jets), the league could have gotten everyone where they needed to be. Either it wanted the risk of a single snafu to be as small as possible (even if that meant blowing up the existing assignments) or it hoped to make the expense as small as possible.

It's hard not to think cost was a factor. And the NFL is very fortunate that Sunday's games went smoothly, after the major disruption to the officials' itineraries.

Still, these games are too important for a rolling of dice or the pinching of pennies, relatively speaking. It would have been, in our view, far more prudent to open the checkbook and spend whatever was needed to get the officials to the places where the officials were originally assigned to be.

#2793. Sean Payton: "There's no loophole" in the trade process

After last week’s failed trade that would have sent Chargers linebacker Daiyan Henley to the Broncos, L.A. coach Jim Harbaugh said that the Broncos used a “loophole” to squeeze the Chargers into better terms before the deal imploded.

The Broncos had no comment last week, and coach Sean Payton hadn’t spoken to reporters because Denver had a Week 10 bye.

On Monday, Payton was available to the media. And the first question asked what changed as to the Henley trade.

“Nothing changed,” Payton said. “It was already stated. Everything was pretty clearly spelled out. I’m not going to get into the specifics. He’s a prospect we thought — and still do [think] — very highly of. It just didn’t work out and it’s not uncommon with trades. That’s kind of where it went, and I’ll leave it at that.”

“There’s no loophole,” Payton said. “It just is part of the trade process every step of the way.”

He’s technically right, but there is a loophole. A team that has buyer’s remorse can try to rely on issues with the physical to negotiate better terms. And an unscrupulous team could finalize a deal before the deadline with the plan of trying to improve the deal later, under the guise of a failed physical.

The easy fix, when it comes to deadline deals, is to take the position that, after the window for making trades closes, the player either passes the physical and the trade goes through as negotiated or the player fails the physical and the deal dies.

There should be no “maybe” box when it comes to physicals. Especially after the period for making trades has ended.

#2792. Victoria Police arrest a man over theft of Makybe Diva’s three Melbourne Cup trophies

MELBOURNE, Australia (AP) — Makybe Diva's three Melbourne Cup trophies are missing after a heist at Flemington Racecourse.

Victoria state police on Tuesday said officers raided a home in Mount Waverley, in the southeastern suburbs of Melbourne on Monday night as part of investigations into the burglary and arrested a 36-year-old man, who was later released pending summons to appear in court.

"It is alleged two men attended the racecourse ... and smashed a cabinet inside the venue at 6:30 a.m." Monday, Victoria Police said in a statement.

Makybe Diva won the Melbourne Cup in 2003, 2004, and 2005 and the trophies had been on public display since September at Flemington Racecourse, where the southern hemisphere's most prestigious horse race is held annually on the first Tuesday of November.

Police said the trophies stolen from the glass display case were each valued at 1 million Australian dollars ($675,000).

Makybe Diva, known widely in local racing circles as "the people's horse," died in February at the age of 26. She was the only horse to have won the Melbourne Cup three years in a row in a race which was first held in 1861.

"Hopefully we can get them back in one piece," former owner Tony Santic told Melbourne radio 3AW. "It's more than devastating. I just can't believe it. Hopefully we get them back in one piece."

The Victorian Racing Club said it was "devastated" by the theft and it was in contact with Santic.
