#2791. 5 takeaways from the Steelers’ 38-18 loss to the Cincinnati Bengals

The Steelers fell woefully flat in a battle for the AFC North lead on Sunday Night Football, losing to the Cincinnati Bengals 38-13 in primetime.

As always, there are plenty of takeaways to be had:

The 20-point margin of victory probably speaks for itself here, no?

There were a solid five weeks of building hype for the Steelers, with a four-game winning streak and a Week 9 bye ahead of Sunday's Bengals matchup. However, despite the Steelers' last game being a dominant, 27-6 victory over the Cleveland Browns, I couldn't shake the feeling that Pittsburgh wasn't for real just yet. Frankly, we've seen enough good, not great Steelers seasons in recent years that the "Pittsburgh is a Super Bowl contender" narrative felt incredibly premature yet again, as predicted.

While the Steelers haven't been entirely unchallenged this season up to this point, the unavoidable truth was that the team hadn't exactly faced a murderer's row of quarterbacks outside of Joe Burrow in Week 3 (who saw success against the Pittsburgh secondary for 282 yards and three touchdowns) and Drake Maye in a down year for him and the Patriots in Week 2 (the Steelers still lost by 17).

And facing a white-hot Bengals offense in Cincinnati Sunday night, the Steelers didn't even look like they belonged in the same sport. It's still a quarterback-driven league, and the Steelers' effort on Sunday night showed what many have known all season: Pittsburgh doesn't have what it takes yet to consistently compete with elite quarterbacks, and the team doesn't have one itself.

It's worth clarifying that while a loss to the Bengals wasn't exactly unsurprising, the way in which the Steelers lost was unbecoming of a professional football team. The internet is already exploding with takes along the lines of "I've been a Steelers fan since the Paleolithic Age and I've never seen the team lose like this" — I feel like I see that exact post every season — but they're not entirely wrong.

But as the Steelers veterans clarified postgame, these losses are par for the course in the NFL. Teams will move on and rebound — something the Steelers have done time after time. However, even if the Steelers had protected Aaron Rodgers for a tick longer on each play, won more in the pass rush, or cleaned up miscommunications in coverage, it was clear the team just doesn't have the firepower to compete with a roster operating on the level Cincinnati was on Sunday.

Pittsburgh beaten the Bengals earlier this season. But there's a reason why coaches such as Mike McCarthy preach the differences of "November football." The games are tougher. The stakes are higher. And the Steelers, once again, didn't look like a team built for that stretch of the season.

Despite two weeks to prepare for what was without a doubt the biggest game of their season so far, the Steelers couldn't even manage to look competitive against the Bengals.

Is it time to be worried about the Steelers? Not necessarily, unless you legitimately believed they were part of the AFC's elite. But it was a brutal way to prove where Pittsburgh needs to grow before it can get back to contending status. To improve from the late Tomlin era, the Steelers need to win in the playoffs, and Sunday's matchup was the closest thing to postseason football Pittsburgh has faced all year.

TL;DR: Joe Burrow had 302 passing yards and three touchdowns… in the first half. Not great!

I don't think any NFL defense matches up well with the Bengals, but for the Steelers, it's an especially bad pairing. Pittsburgh relies on strong run defense and a chaotic pass rush that masks a vulnerable secondary. Cincinnati simply doesn't need to run that much because of its overpowered passing game. And Burrow is ruthlessly efficient, even versus pressure — and it helps that he's throwing to the best wide receiver duo in the league.

It's a perfect storm for the Steelers, and the results on Sunday showed that.

Film review of even Pittsburgh's big win over Cleveland in Week 8 revealed that the defense wasn't as lockdown against the pass as the numbers might suggest, and the Bengals' offense is tailor-made for exploiting any weaknesses in a secondary. The Steelers were beat through the air in every way imaginable on Sunday: over the top, missed tackles underneath, miscommunications, and simply losing matchups to better athletes.

Meanwhile, the Steelers couldn't counter with their star-studded pass rush. Burrow was pressured the second-least of any quarterback in Week 10 (pending Monday Night Football) at just 17.4%, with right tackle Amarius Mims once again stonewalling T.J. Watt and the Bengals having an answer for every blitz thrown at them by Steelers defensive coordinator Patrick Graham.

The coverage struggles were expected. But the Steelers' pass rush is too highly-paid and celebrated to drop off like it did in such a big game, even against an offensive line as underrated as Cincinnati's.

The Steelers' offense, like its defense, is still far from "bad" despite some games firmly in that territory. Over the course of the season, the Steelers' rushing game has proven to be one of the better ones in the league, but Pittsburgh is still somewhat limited in the games where it can succeed.

The patented "bully ball" approach really only works when you're punching down — and in a negative game script against the Bengals, the Steelers looked like they were floundering. A standout play in that sense was a third-and-long fake screen that resulted in a deep incompletion to tight end Darnell Washington. For one, it wasn't much of a surprise that the Bengals defense didn't bite on the fake short pass in that scenario. And for another, the Steelers ran that exact play last week!

Despite an extra week of preparation in a battle for the AFC North lead, the Steelers tried to run back the hits on offense instead of innovating.

Honestly, I'd put the Bengals loss more on the Pittsburgh offense than the defense. The game was always going to be a high-scoring one, but by the time the Steelers gained their second first-down of the night, they were already down two scores with 3:28 remaining in the second quarter. Pittsburgh had just 8:19 of time of possession at the half. No wonder the defense looks exhausted and uninspired!

One of the benefits of writing this column a bit later than usual is that there's a bit more time to digest the game and see some tape breakdowns, which backed up the eye test from watching the game: The Steelers' offensive struggles weren't just the fault of scheme and play-calling, but also poor execution on just about everyone's part.

The offensive line struggled to move Dexter Lawrence in the run game and protect Rodgers from pressure. DK Metcalf, despite scoring the rare NFL "octopus", had another game where he failed to make a contested catch and recorded a procedural penalty. And Rodgers, when he was kept clean, failed to capitalize on opportunities downfield.

Remember Joe Burrow's halftime numbers? Rodgers, on the other hand, was 4/12 for 25 yards at the break.

Big surprise: the loss to the Bengals was a group failure. When your team has clear flaws and quarterback play that can't cover it, you'll end up with results like Sunday's.

A common reaction to the Steelers' struggles on Sunday was that Pittsburgh was undermanned and missed an opportunity to fix that at the NFL trade deadline, which occurred Nov. 10.

Sure, an improvement at cornerback or wide receiver might've lowered the Bengals' margin of victory a bit. But to circle back to my first point in this article, the Steelers are not Super Bowl contenders in 2026 — and to build on that, they weren't a Josh Downs or Charvarius Ward away from reaching that status, either. If you replace Asante Samuel Jr. or Brandon Smith on the Steelers' Sunday roster with a better player, that still wouldn't have covered for the other 10 starters not doing their job.

Not to belabor the point any further, but it's important to recognize this Steelers team for what it is: A collection of young players building the foundation for an eventual franchise quarterback, while also punching above their weight class at times thanks to a core of veterans and an experienced coaching staff. At the deadline, Pittsburgh general manager Omar Khan rightfully saw that the team is not a win-now window, not falling for the hype of a 6-2 start.

That doesn't mean that the Steelers can't be fun in 2026. The big win over Cleveland in Week 9 proved that there's still a lot of potential on the Pittsburgh roster.

However, Week 10's loss was a stark reminder of how much growth is still required.

Not too many positives in this one, but I can rattle off a few: For one, it was good to see wide receiver/special teams ace Ben Skowronek back in the lineup this week. The Bengals muffed (but recovered) a first-quarter punt, and it was No. 15 who was in the middle of it.

The Steelers' interior rushers had some clear wins on Sunday. The pass rush wasn't always completely ineffective; Joe Burrow is just fantastic and navigating a messy pocket. I'm still rather high on the Pittsburgh interior D-line.

Keep in mind this same Bengals defense gave up 36 points to the Titans last week.

To my eyes, Kaden Wetjen looked a bit more shifty against the Bengals than in past games this season. He still averaged a middling 26.6 yards per return, but seems to be gaining his sea legs.

Payton Wilson logged a whopping 19 tackles on Sunday, but considering how often Steelers defenders were simply chasing down Bengals after a chunk gain, I don't think that's a stat worth spinning positively.

Pat Freiermuth's 27-yard touchdown was easily the Steelers' best offensive play of the game. That play was also one of the tight end's two catches on Sunday. I'm surprised Pittsburgh didn't try and utilize him more.

Mason McCormick's ankle injury looks like a long-term one, and his teammates' postgame comments seemed to agree. If and when McCormick misses time, that's a brutal turn of events for Pittsburgh, as the left side of the offensive line has been the team's greatest strength on offense this season.

Lew Nichols III finished with a team-best 43 rushing yards, averaging 7.2 yards per carry. Sure, those stats came in garbage time, but it was good to see the running back, who has impressed the last two preseasons, get some regular season production.

Daylen Everette continues to get some dimebacker snaps for the Steelers. An interesting use for the rookie, who was largely seen as a boundary corner coming out of the draft.

A friendly reminder that the Steelers are still the No. 6 seed in the AFC playoffs, 6-3, and second in the AFC North. They've split both of their played division series so far. The sky is definitely not falling. There's a lot of season left, and it could still be a fun one with the right expectations.

Next week, Pittsburgh will face the 2-7 Eagles on Sunday in Philadelphia, where the Steelers haven't won since the 1960s.

What are your takeaways from Steelers vs. Bengals? Agree/disagree with the ones above? Join the Behind the Steel Curtain community and let us know in the comments!

#2787. 49ers game review: Nick Bosa in career-worst drought. How bad did it look in Dallas?

Nick Bosa's bull rush remains bruising. Just ask Terence Steele.

In the second quarter Sunday, Bosa, the San Francisco 49ers' All-Pro pass rusher, extended his hands into Steele's chest and drove the Cowboys' 6-foot-6, 320-pound right tackle back into the lap of quarterback Dak Prescott.

As Prescott threw, targeting wideout CeeDee Lamb on a slant, Bosa hit Prescott's throwing arm, causing the ball to sail into the breadbasket of linebacker Dre Greenlaw, who dropped the gift-wrapped potential interception in the 49ers' 46-40 win.

It was a vintage Bosa. For many of his other 41 pass-rush snaps: Invisible Bosa?

Bosa earned the lowest Pro Football Grade (40.8) of his 89-game career in a game in which he didn't have a sack and the aforementioned play was his lone QB hit. Bosa, who missed five games with a calf injury, doesn't have a sack in four games he has played, the only time during his career he has gone sack-less in four straight games in the same season.

Bosa has two QB hits in 109 pass-rush snaps this season and his lack of stats is inspiring questions about whether his medical file is a contributing factor.

In September 2025, the ACL in Bosa's right knee was torn for the second time. The Chronicle spoke with two orthopedic surgeons in August about a second ACL surgery on the same knee, what they termed a "revision ACL reconstruction," and both said players often don't return to normal pre-surgery performance for 12 to 18 months.

After reviewing Bosa's snaps Sunday against the Cowboys, however, some context is required for a performance that wasn't up to his standards, but also didn't suggest he's a shell of himself.

Bosa did have four QB pressures - he twice had quick wins against Steele that forced Prescott to scramble - and the Cowboys devoted an extra blocker to Bosa on nine of his 41-pass rush snaps, using a tight end or running back to deliver a chip block on six plays. In the third quarter, two series after Bosa hit Prescott's arm in mid-throw, Bosa beat Steele with a speed rush, forcing Prescott to step up in the pocket before he fired a deep incompletion on 3rd-and-6.

A contributing factor to Bosa's relatively silent Sunday was the 49ers' shredded secondary, which routinely failed to hold up in coverage. As a result, Prescott rarely had to hold onto the ball for a few extra beats in a game in which he completed 30 of 51 passes for 343 yards with five touchdowns and an interception and was sacked only once.

Bosa had three offsides penalties, which was a factor in his career-low PFF grade. His pass-rush grade (60.4) was the 13th-lowest of his career.

The verdict? We obviously didn't witness the best of Bosa, but he wasn't far removed from landing a sack Sunday that would have quieted much of the noise. In fact, if Greenlaw hadn't dropped the would-be interception at the Cowboys' 45-yard line, Bosa would have had the 49ers' defensive play of the day.

But the play didn't result in a takeaway. And Bosa is still without a sack. A price of his $170 million contract is that the criticism, some of which may be unfair, will mount as long the drought continues.

QB Brock Purdy's flee-the-pocket, 74-yard touchdown toss to running back Christian McCaffrey was his most spectacular off-schedule play. Maybe? Purdy had two other great-escape completions while throwing for 135 yards on scrambles, matching the most in the NFL since 2018, according to Next Gen Stats.

In the third quarter, on 2nd-and-12, Purdy dashed to his right out of a fast-crumbling pocket, pointed to wide receiver Jordan Watkins in mid-scramble, seemingly directing him to run to the sideline, and delivered a 27-yard strike.

In the second quarter, on 3rd-and-12, Purdy escaped the grasp of All-Pro defensive tackle Quinnen Williams, darted to his left with three defenders in pursuit and fired an on-the-run, 23-yard completion to wideout Keenan Allen.

Fox play-by-play man Kevin Burkhardt: "… Running for his life, throws across his body and …"

Purdy couldn't always escape. He was dropped for five sacks and took a hellacious hit in the third quarter when he was sandwiched by linebacker Dee Winters and Jaishawn Barham. Purdy remained on his back for about five seconds and walked around to collect himself after slowly rising.

"There were a couple times it seemed like he was hurt," head coach Kyle Shanahan said. "He never told me that anything was wrong, so I'm assuming not. But he took some hits in that game."

The primary reason for Purdy's punishment: Connor Colby.

The second-year left guard had a woeful PFF pass-blocking grade (21.4) while often lined up against Williams. Not a fair fight. Williams beat Colby for a second-quarter sack and easily blew past him again later in the period, forcing Purdy to step up in the pocket and get dropped for a sack by defensive tackle LT Overton. Purdy absorbed the linebacker sandwich after Barham beat Colby.

The early returns on the Allen trade: positive. And they could be even better if Purdy didn't appear to misfire on two passes - 14 and 23 yards downfield, respectively - in which the just-acquired wide receiver was wide open over the middle.

Allen caught five passes for 61 yards on seven targets. If not for the off-target throws: seven catches for more than 100 yards on seven targets.

Shanahan said Allen was also open in the end zone in the second quarter when Williams dropped Purdy.

"Missed him in the red zone on a possible touchdown when we got sacked," Shanahan said.

With the 49ers leading 46-40 with 42 seconds left, it was notable that rookie running back Kaelon Black was on the field instead of McCaffrey and had the first two carries of the possession as the 49ers tried to run out the clock.

It will be interesting to see if that happens the next time the 49ers are in a similar situation.

On his second carry, Black fumbled after Williams ripped the ball from his grasp, but Black avoided a potential disaster by recovering the fumble. Not surprisingly, McCaffrey replaced Black on the next snap.

Black also nearly fumbled at the end of a reception earlier in the game, but he was ruled down before the ball popped up in the air after it was ripped away (Black caught it while on his back).

The Cowboys appeared to have made note that Black lost a fumble in a Week 7 loss at Atlanta. On Black's 25-yard touchdown run in which he bulldozed safety Caleb Downs at the 22-yard line, cornerback Cobie Durant made no attempt to tackle Black at the 12-yard line, swiping at the ball instead.

Speaking of fumbles: Linebacker Fred Warner clearly forced a fumble in the third quarter when he punched the ball from running back Javonte Williams' grasp, but Williams was ruled down. The 49ers didn't challenge the play because it wasn't clear who made the recovery.Warner indicated the 49ers would request a stat correction.

"It felt clean on the field," Warner said. "We'll see. We'll turn it in."

Warner is tied for the NFL lead with five forced fumbles and, if he's awarded another from Sunday's game, he'll remain on pace to break the league record of 10 held by Osi Umenyiora (2010) and Charles Tillman (2012).

"Just got to keep punching," Warner said.

#2785. Kingdom Keys revisited: Chiefs turn two keys in win over Falcons

Every week, "Kingdom Keys" identifies three things the Kansas City Chiefs must accomplish to win their upcoming game. Afterward, we revisit those keys to determine which ones Kansas City actually turned.

The Chiefs defeated the Atlanta Falcons 35-13 on Sunday, improving to 7-2 behind five touchdown passes from Patrick Mahomes and another strong defensive performance.

Kansas City clearly accomplished its first two keys. The third requires a little more nuance.

The offensive key was for Kansas City's wide receivers to punish Atlanta whenever the Falcons committed additional resources to stopping the run.

Chiefs wide receivers combined for 12 receptions, 222 yards and three touchdowns. They accounted for nearly 64% of Mahomes' 348 passing yards and three of his five touchdown passes.

Rashee Rice led the group with five receptions for 82 yards, including a 50-yard completion that helped set up Kansas City's fourth touchdown. Josh Downs made an immediate impact in his Chiefs debut, catching four passes for 53 yards and a touchdown after having only two full practices with Mahomes.

The production extended beyond the top two receivers.

Rookie Cyrus Allen caught two passes for 44 yards, including a 39-yard touchdown that extended Kansas City's lead to 21-3. Andrew Armstrong turned his only target into a 43-yard touchdown that put the game away in the fourth quarter.

The original goal was for the receivers to combine for at least 150 yards. They surpassed that mark by 72 yards.

The Falcons wanted someone other than the Chiefs' running backs to beat them. Kansas City's receivers provided a convincing answer.

Bijan Robinson entered the game averaging more than 125 yards from scrimmage per contest. Kansas City held him to 77.

Robinson rushed 16 times for 71 yards and caught four passes for just 6 yards. He scored Atlanta's only touchdown on a three-yard run in the fourth quarter, but the Chiefs prevented him from controlling the game.

Kansas City did allow Robinson to break runs of 17 and 14 yards. Outside those two carries, however, he gained only 40 yards on his other 14 rushing attempts.

The Chiefs were especially effective against Robinson as a receiver. His four catches produced only 6 yards, and two receptions lost yardage. Taking away those easy completions forced Michael Penix Jr. to throw elsewhere.

Kansas City also consistently put Atlanta behind schedule. The Falcons converted only five of their 16 third-down opportunities, finishing at 31.3%. Using third-and-7 or longer as the definition of third-and-long, Atlanta converted just two of 10 attempts.

One conversion came on a third-and-11 completion to Jahan Dotson in the second quarter. The other came when Drake London gained 20 yards on third-and-8 in the third quarter. Otherwise, the Chiefs repeatedly ended Atlanta's possessions in obvious passing situations.

The pressure also returned. Kansas City recorded three sacks, including one from Ashton Gillotte on third-and-7 that forced Atlanta to settle for a field goal. George Karlaftis recorded 1.5 sacks, and Chris Jones shared another.

Kansas City didn't completely eliminate Robinson, but it kept Atlanta's best offensive player from dictating the game. That was the goal.

Harrison Butker officially finished without a field-goal attempt, making this key impossible to grade as a success or failure.

However, that does not mean the afternoon was meaningless.

Late in the second quarter, Butker connected on a 41-yard field goal. Atlanta was called for defensive offside, erasing the attempt from the official statistics and giving Kansas City a first down. Mahomes found Downs for an 18-yard touchdown on the next play.

The kick did not count, but it still went through the uprights.

Butker also made all five of his extra-point attempts. Those kicks don't prove his season-long struggles are over, especially since most of his problems have come on field goals of at least 50 yards. Still, cleanly converting six kicks — including the nullified field goal — can help him start rebuilding his rhythm.

Butker still needs to prove he can consistently convert field goals under game pressure. Sunday simply did not provide enough official opportunities to make that determination. What it did provide was a small, positive step.

The Chiefs definitively accomplished two of their three Kingdom Keys against Atlanta.

The wide receivers delivered their best collective performance of the season, producing 222 yards and three touchdowns. Defensively, Kansas City held Robinson below 100 scrimmage yards and limited Atlanta to two conversions on 10 third-and-long opportunities.

Butker did not record an official field-goal attempt, leaving the third key ungraded. However, making five extra points and watching a 41-yard attempt split the uprights gave him something positive to carry forward.

Kingdom Keys final tally: Two accomplished, one ungraded

#2784. Delaware Blue Coats check-in: Labaron Philon lights it up

The Philadelphia 76ers' season has largely lived up to expectations so far. With such a loaded roster, it was inevitable that several players would struggle to find consistent playing time. As a result, the Sixers have sent some of their young talent down to Wilmington, Delaware, for valuable G League reps, including rookie Labaron Philon.

So, how have these players performed when given extended opportunities? Let's dive into the Delaware Blue Coats, who kicked off their season in recent weeks, and take a look at some of the bigger storylines emerging in the G League.

The Sixers, as expected, haven't used Philon much during his first dozen or so career games. The Sixers sent their rookie down to Delaware to play in their Nov. 13 matchup against the Maine Celtics. Philon would get the starting nod in this game, playing 36 minutes and recording 20 points, nine rebounds, eight assists and a steal on 7-of-18 shooting with just two turnovers.

Philon showed off his skill set well in this game, with no shortage of creation for himself and his teammates, as evidenced by his eight assists. He had several impressive finishes through contact, with his handle and playmaking ability on full display. While the efficiency left something to be desired, the process itself was good, and Philon looked like the scorer he's advertised as.

Philon would promptly get recalled after this game, which presented a good opportunity for him to get some needed reps in an organized and officiated professional setting.

Along with Philon, Justin Edwards was assigned to Delaware, playing in the back-to-back set against the Maine Celtics. In his first game, Edwards struggled mightily, shooting 1-of-10 from the field and recording two points, four rebounds, and two steals in 30 minutes. Unlike Philon, Edwards was not recalled after the first game and also played in the second matchup against Maine, scoring 23 points while recording two assists and two steals in 37 minutes of play on 9-of-17 shooting from the field.

The first game has unfortunately been Edwards' season in a nutshell. He has struggled to get going and often has a clunky offensive process. At times, it seems like he's overprocessing or doesn't know when to assert himself. The second game was much better, and at this point in his career, that should be the stat line we're accustomed to seeing from Edwards when on assignment.

It's notable that the Sixers didn't recall Edwards after one game in the G League. When called upon in the NBA, Edwards has looked lost out there. It might be beneficial to let him continue getting G League reps, with extended touches, to rebuild some of his confidence and rhythm.

The Sixers had two of their three two-way contract players, Caleb Love and Rayan Rupert, suit up for some G League action. Rupert played only in the first game, scoring 13 points in 34 minutes while shooting 6-of-12 from the field and 1-of-6 from three, along with four rebounds, four assists, and a steal. Love would play both games, scoring 22 points on 8-of-15 shooting in the first game and 35 points on 10-of-20 shooting from the field in the second game. He shot 7-of-15 from three in the second matchup and also recorded two steals.

The G League is a tough environment for a guy with Rupert's skill set (toolsy, low-usage, gap-filler) to shine, but overall, I thought Rupert did a decent job in the first game, which was a rough one for Delaware. Love, on the other hand, continues to be my personal favorite two-way contract player currently on the roster. He's a gifted scorer and shooter, and that was evident in both games he played. While he might be limited defensively, there is some value in having a guy deep on your bench who isn't afraid to let it fly, like we've seen with Anfernee Simons.

It's still early in both the NBA and G League seasons, with mixed results as expected. You can tune in to the Delaware Blue Coats, potentially featuring an assignee or two, on Monday, Nov. 16, at 7 p.m. The game will be streamed on the NBA app and NBA.com.

#2783. Germany vs. Netherlands preview: Team news and predicted lineups

The German national team head into their final Group A2 match of the 2026/27 UEFA Nations League in Berlin on Monday, hoping to secure a place in the quarter-finals.

Following a 3-1 away victory over bottom-placed Serbia, who have yet to pick up a point, Jürgen Klopp's side sit third in the group with eight points, level with second-placed Greece. The Netherlands lead the standings on 11 points after thrashing Greece 7-0 on Friday.

A victory over the Netherlands would guarantee Germany a place in the quarter-finals in March 2027, with the margin of victory and Greece's result in the simultaneous match against Serbia determining whether they finish first or second in Group A2.

Klopp's side would top the group if they beat the Netherlands and Greece fail to defeat Serbia. A German victory by at least five goals would also be enough to secure first place, regardless of Greece's result. If Germany win by a margin of four goals or fewer while Greece also claim victory, they would finish as group runners-up instead.

A draw would leave Germany's qualification hopes dependent on the result between Greece and Serbia. If Greece lose, Germany would advance to the quarter-finals in second place. However, if Greece pick up at least a point, Germany would finish third and miss out on the knockout stages.

A defeat against the Netherlands would also leave Die Nationalmannschaft third in the group, regardless of the result in the other fixture. That could have further consequences, potentially sending Germany into the League A/B relegation play-offs in March 2027.

If Norway beat Denmark the following day, Germany would be among the two lowest-ranked third-placed teams across League A's four groups and would therefore have to face one of the League B group runners-up in a two-legged relegation play-off.

Jürgen Klopp could make a couple of changes to his backline for Monday's clash with the Netherlands.

Rather surprisingly, Klopp left Nico Schlotterbeck out of the starting XI against Serbia, opting instead for Malick Thiaw. The Germany coach explained that Thiaw's inclusion would give his side an advantage when dealing with Serbia's long balls. However, with the Dutch team expected to favour a more technical, possession-based approach, keeping the ball on the ground, Schlotterbeck is likely to return to the starting line-up.

On the left side of defence, the more technically gifted Nathaniel Brown could come in, potentially replacing Maximilian Mittelstädt.

As for the Netherlands, Frenkie de Jong is set to miss the game after picking up a knock, with Teun Koopmeiners likely to step in alongside Borussia Dortmund's Joey Veerman in central midfield.

Otherwise, head coach Xavi Hernández has little reason to tinker with his starting XI following the emphatic 7-0 victory over Greece.

#2782. Patriots’ biggest problem to fix during 2026 bye week

Patriots' biggest problem to fix during 2026 bye week appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

There are plenty of problems the New England Patriots need to address during their Week 11 bye. However, one stands above the rest: What happened to the running game?

The Patriots' rushing attack has become almost nonexistent, and the numbers don't tell the entire story. New England is averaging 114 rushing yards per game, but it doesn't have a single running back averaging more than 40.5 yards per game.

Rhamondre Stevenson is averaging 40.3 yards per game, Corey Kiner is at 26.9, and TreVeyon Henderson is averaging 33.8. Henderson has appeared in only six games and is currently sidelined, leaving Stevenson and Kiner to carry the workload.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

Against the Detroit Lions, Kiner received 10 carries while Stevenson had just six. The Patriots finished with 61 rushing yards as a team, and backup quarterback Tommy DeVito accounted for 19 of them. That's simply not good enough, especially for a team that could be without its starting quarterback for an extended period.

Drake Maye suffered an ankle injury against Detroit and will undergo further testing to determine the severity. While his recovery timeline remains uncertain, New England has to prepare for the possibility that he misses significant time. That makes fixing the running game more important than ever.

If the Patriots want to salvage their season, they need to establish some consistency on the ground. That is especially true if Maye is unavailable when they return from the bye. DeVito struggled after taking over against Detroit, and New England cannot expect its backup quarterback to carry the offense with a poor receiving core.

Mike Vrabel needs to find a way to get more out of his running backs. It's not as if this group lacks talent. We have seen Stevenson and Henderson produce at a high level, while Kiner has shown enough to suggest there is something worth developing.

Kiner averaged 4.8 yards per carry on 12 attempts with the Arizona Cardinals last season. This year, he is averaging 4.5 yards per carry on 60 attempts. That's respectable production, especially for a running back still finding his footing in New England.

Stevenson, meanwhile, is averaging just 3.7 yards per carry. Henderson isn't much better at 3.9, although his opportunities have been limited by his absence. The Patriots need more from all three. Stevenson has to be more effective with his touches, Kiner needs to take advantage of the opportunity in front of him, and Henderson's eventual return should give the backfield another boost.

If things don't improve, New England may have to explore bringing in another running back. There is no reason to keep running the same offense if the current personnel cannot consistently move the football.

Vrabel has an entire bye week to evaluate what isn't working. He needs to communicate with his running backs, figure out how to get them more involved, and make the necessary adjustments up front.

The Patriots have bigger questions to answer with Maye's health, but the running game is something they can begin addressing immediately. If New England wants to stay competitive over the second half of the season, Stevenson and Kiner need to step up. Some believe New England isn't a playoff team anyway.

The bye week couldn't have come at a better time. Now it's up to Vrabel to make sure the Patriots come out of it with a much better plan on the ground when they face the winless Los Angeles Chargers in Week 12 (which could be flexed out of Sunday Night Football).

#2778. ‘He Can’t Seem To Fix It’ — Analyst Torches Mike Vrabel Over Patriots’ Ongoing Cultural, Defensive Issues

Mike Vrabel suffered consecutive regular-season defeats for the first time in his tenure as New England Patriots head coach after his team was demolished 35-10 by the Detroit Lions in a Week 10 showdown in Munich, Germany. The AFC East franchise failed to respond to its 45-27 Week 9 loss to the Green Bay Packers and has now slumped to a 5-5 record this season.

After the Patriots' latest meltdown, analyst Marc Bertrand slammed Vrabel's ongoing struggle with the team's culture and defensive identity.

During a segment on the "Zolak & Bertrand" show on 98.5 The Sports Hub, Bertrand laid the blame for the Patriots' struggles at the head coach's feet.

"How much of the blame for this goes to the top and Mike Vrabel? A ton. A boatload. It's his team," Bertrand said. "He's a culture guy. Do they have culture? Emphatic no! Do they have a good defense with him being a defense-minded head coach?

"You wanna pass on the opportunity to hire an offensive-minded coach or an offensive guru like a Ben Johnson, you don't wanna go that route. You wanna go the culture route and establish culture. They did that a year ago. Has it continued? No. That's number one.

BE THE GM OF YOUR FAVORITE TEAM: PFN's Free NFL Mock Draft Simulator

Bertrand further slammed the Patriots' defense, noting that, given Vrabel's background as a defensive coordinator, the unit needed to be much better than it is.

"Number two, how's your defense? You got a defensive-minded head coach. You're supposed to be able to say, 'With a defensive-minded head coach, we'll always have a defense that's able to make up for injuries.' And by the way, they don't have a ton of them on that side of the ball.

"But also, they'll be able to make adjustments in-game, scheme, prepare week-to-week for the opponent that they're playing. Have they done that? No. They haven't. So, where's the head coach? This is his team, it's his problem, and he can't seem to fix it."

Vrabel's Patriots have allowed 24.2 points per game and 345.1 yards per game, ranking No. 17 and No. 22 in the NFL, respectively. New England has also surrendered 239.0 passing yards per game and 106.1 rushing yards per game. Defensive coordinator Zak Kuhr's unit has earned the league's No. 18 PFN Defense Impact score of 72.9, well behind the main Super Bowl contenders.

SET YOUR LINEUPS: PFN's Free Fantasy Start/Sit Optimizer

Vrabel, the former Patriots linebacker, rejuvenated the Patriots after replacing Jerod Mayo last year, leading New England to its first AFC East title since 2019, first playoff appearance since 2021, and its best record since 2016 (14-3). Vrabel led the Patriots back to the Super Bowl, becoming just the eighth head coach to achieve that feat in their first season.

Vrabel's second season in charge was preceded by months of headline-grabbing news after his alleged affair with NFL insider Dianna Russini was uncovered, forcing him to miss the final day of the 2026 NFL Draft to attend counseling.

The Patriots season looks in danger of crumbling after quarterback Drake Maye suffered a significant lower-leg injury on the opening drive of the game against the Lions, which could potentially rule him out for the rest of the season. With the team's best offensive weapon missing and the defense falling apart, Vrabel's second season is not going according to plan.

#2777. Game Preview #14: Wolves vs. Trail Blazers Game Thread

The Timberwolves had done a great job in currying good favor so far this season with their play in Ant's absence, but that was put to the test in a Saturday night brutal loss to the Chicago Bulls. The Bulls were on an eight-game losing streak and took it right at the Timberwolves from the opening jump. Combine that with the bad shooting variance for Minnesota, and it was a tough pill to swallow after the highs of the five-game winning streak.

As with anything in life, it's not about about getting knocked down but more how you get back up. Minnesota has a great opportunity to do just that this week with multiple marquee matchups as they continue plugging along on their long home stand. Tonight's matchup features the first of a back-to-back against the same team in the Portland Trail Blazers

The Timberwolves have made this a staple of their season over the past few years, usually heading out to Portland to take on this squad in a mini series. This time, they get it at home with a great chance to right the ship against a Portland team that has come out of the gates red hot.

Who: Minnesota Timberwolves (9-4) vs. Portland Trail Blazers (9-3)

Where: Target Center – Minneapolis, Minnesota

For years, it was a question of when – not if – Chris Finch's trusted assistant coach and halftime interview icon Micah Nori would get the opportunity to run his own team. That chance came for him this summer as the Portland Trail Blazers moved on from Tiago Splitter and gave the charismatic Minnesota assistant a chance to head up the young Trail Blazers roster. While much was made about the terms of the deal – owner Tom Dundon opted for an atypical one-year contract – Chris Finch aptly silenced that discourse with one true statement: "I just know this. There's 30 of these jobs. They're hard to get."

Anyone who watch the Timberwolves since Micah Nori got here knew that he would someday get his chance to be the head coach of a team. His combination of basketball smarts with his charismatic personality combine into a great leader for any organization to get behind. Timberwolves players, coaches, front office and fans have an affinity for Coach Nori, and it's a certainty that he will get a nice homecoming when they face off for the first time tonight.

Saturday's loss was one that everyone wants to forget about. While it is frustrating and there are any number of things that can be dissected and agonized over, the beauty and curse of the NBA is that there is always another game on the horizon. The Timberwolves and specifically LaMelo Ball had gone on a tear over the best week plus to navigate their way to five wins in a row, and the Bulls game felt like a punch in the face after all that success. Ball shot four of 19 from three as the team shot 24% as a unit from downtown. That's a jarring drop-off from their usual numbers this season.

It will be important to see how Ball and the team respond from that matchup and look to keep the general arrow going up. Portland has been playing extremely good basketball as of late and they are very committed to a harassing style at the point of attack. LaMelo Ball has progressively gotten better at attacking those defenses early on in this season and he'll need to continue that trend as the Wolves look ahead to this mini two-game series.

The Rockets game is the blueprint, and something to watch is his ability to be flexible and bounce back after the loss. The mini series against the same opponent brings with it a chance to have a more in depth gameplan, so watching what LaMelo's focus is in these two games will show exactly how Finch wants to deploy him after a game like Saturday.

Jaylen Clark made one of his typical hustle plays before saving the ball and coming up wincing during the Bulls game. After walking back to the locker room immediately, he was diagnosed with a hamstring strain that will hold him out for a couple of weeks. Soft tissue injuries are nothing to mess around with and we are all wishing him a speedy recovery as he had been a crucial part of the non-Ant Wolves over this stretch of time. Looking ahead, it will be something to watch as to how Chris Finch backfills that time.

Finch had mostly been going with a 10-man rotation to start the season without Edwards (and when Shannon was healthy), but with Clark now unavailable it will be interesting to see how he navigates that going forward. If he still wants to keep 10 guys in the rotation, Cody Williams or Isaiah Evans would be the next logical players to get a crack at playing time. Cody Williams had a small chance earlier on in the season, but wasn't able to gain much traction when he was out there. He will most likely will shrink the rotation to nine, as was the case when the Wolves were without Terrence Shannon Jr., but it will be interesting to see if one of those end of the bench guys gets a shot at playing minutes in this time without Jaylen Clark.

Our blogging buddies are over at Blazer's Edge. Play nice!

#2776. Stephen Jones: We're playing "unacceptable" football, our fans deserve better

Sunday’s loss to the 49ers dropped the Cowboys to 4-6 on the season and magnified the season-long struggles that the team has had on the defensive side of the ball.

The 49ers scored three touchdowns that covered at least 66 yards on their way to compiling 584 yards in the 46-40 win. Those difficulties have led to calls for the team to move on from head coach Brian Schottenheimer and defensive coordinator Christian Parker.

The Cowboys have not indicated that any changes are on the horizon, but executive vice president Stephen Jones said on 105.3 The Fan on Monday that the team is “not doing what it takes to win these football games” right now. Jones outlined the places where the team is falling short and those areas were frequently on the defensive side of the ball, which led to question late in his appearance about his response to the calls for major changes to the coaching staff.

“I just think at the end of the day, I certainly understand their frustration because there’s no doubt that we’re frustrated,” Jones said. “We feel like the way we’re playing ball right now is unacceptable. Our fans certainly deserve better.”

Games against the Titans and Eagles in the next two weeks give the Cowboys a chance to get back to .500 heading into the final chunk of the regular season. That won’t erase the missed opportunities over the first 10 weeks, but it will give the current regime one more chance to show that they can correct the issues before the Cowboys decide if it is time to give others a chance to right the ship.

#2773. Embiid questionable, LeBron OUT for second of back-to-back in Brooklyn

Hopefully this is just all a part of the normal stagger.

The Sixers released the injury report for their second leg of this back-to-back against the Brooklyn Nets Monday and have listed Joel Embiid as questionable and LeBron James as out. The same reasons have been listed, right plantar fascia management and lower back/sciatica management, respectively.

Nick Nurse said ahead of their win over the Pacers that the team's load management program is what dictated Embiid missing that game. That would indicate he'd be good to go in Brooklyn, but the questionable designation raises an eyebrow a bit. Nurse also said earlier in the week he thought Embiid wasn't moving that great in some recent games.

The question was also broached to Nurse as to if it's better to play Embiid and James together in one leg to build chemistry. Nurse says that is something that's been considered, but the team's long-term management plans take priority to that. ESPN's Dave McMenamin just reported that James has asked the Sixers' coaching staff to be patient with him as he hopes to find his rhythm as the season goes on, hoping for a ramp-up in December.

The rest of the injury report hasn't changed. Adem Bona and Dominick Barlow remain out with their respective neck and back injuries. Brooklyn comes into this one with a clean slate of health. They're only listings on the injury report are two-way assignments, which includes the No. 28 overall pick in this past year's draft Joshua Jefferson, as well as 2025 first-round picks Ben Saraf and Drake Powell.

Should Embiid be able to go, this should be an easy win for Philadelphia. If he's not able to, things will get really tricky at the center position without James as an option there either.

“Those are things you don’t know until you get him here, though. … You might hear it, but once you get him here, you get the reality of it.”

The Chiefs will be back home in Week 11 to face the Cardinals. We’ll see if and by how much Downs’ production increases with more time in the building.

#2770. Detroit Lions re-claim EDGE rusher following Aidan Hutchinson injury

For the second time in the last 14 months, the Detroit Lions have claimed edge rusher Tyrus Wheat off waivers from the Dallas Cowboys. In a corresponding move, the Lions released cornerback Nick Whiteside from the 53-man roster.

At cutdowns in 2025, the Lions claimed Wheat off waivers and placed him on their active roster. Wheat was active for 15 games, though he was mainly a special teamer, playing 215 snaps in the third phase of the game and only 66 on defense. While his opportunities were minimal, he made the most of them, registering career highs in tackles (15), sacks (1.5), QB Hits (2), and PFF grade (82.3).

Wheat left the Lions in free agency to re-join the Cowboys—the team that drafted him—but has been limited to just five games played with minimal production (three tackles). The Cowboys released him on November 14th to make room for defensive lineman Jonathan Bullard, and the Lions—having just lost Aidan Hutchinson to injury—were quick to claim him.

With Wheat's experience as a rush end in the Lions scheme, expectations are that he'll get up to speed with the playbook quickly and could be incorporated into game action as soon as this weekend. While well-versed in the Lions system, expectations should reflect his involvement from last season: a core special teamer who can, on average, give the Lions 4-5 snaps a game on defense.

Whiteside spent most of the season on the Lions' practice squad but played in the last two games on the 53-man roster. It wouldn't be surprising to see if the Lions try to re-add Whiteside to the practice squad.

#2769. 2026-27 NBA News, Injury Updates Nov. 16: Stephen Curry injured, Dejounte Murray makes trade request

Welcome to our weekly Monday Morning NBA notebook, a quick hit of NBA news and injuries from around the league to catch you up on what happened in the past week. Maybe one of these weeks I can write a summary that doesn't start with critical injuries.

The Golden State Warriors have been one of the great stories of the young season, with rookie Yaxel Landeborg grabbing headlines and winning games. The Warriors have beaten the Lakers, Timberwolves and Nuggets at points this season.

But this was still Stephen Curry's team, and his throwback start — 27.8 points per game, shooting 45.3% from 3-point range — is the foundation of all that winning. Now he is out with an oblique abdominal tear and will be re-evaluated in a couple of weeks, but reportedly could miss more like a month. In the game where he got injured and the game he missed so far, the Warriors got blown out. It might be a rough few weeks coming up in the Bay Area.

Caleb Wilson hasn't been the best rookie out of this class so far this young season, but he's been the most entertaining, averaging 18.9 points and 6.2 rebounds a game. More importantly, he gives the Bulls and their fans a real direction for the future after too many years of mediocrity (at best).

Now he is out 4-6 weeks with a hand fracture. Which just takes the air out of the sails in Chicago. It shouldn't impact winning or (more often) losing, they have been about the same with him on or off the court this season, but he brought a joy to the Bulls that is going to be missing for a bit.

We have our first trade request of the season: Dejounte Murray's agent (Rich Paul of Klutch Sports) told the New Orleans Pelicans Murray wants to be moved, something the Pelicans came out and confirmed.

When the Pelicans traded for Murray in the summer of 2024 it seemed like a good move on paper, he was the kind of guard who could bring together a talented roster that wasn't quite fitting with Zion Williamson, Trey Murphy III and others. It didn't work out that way, as he ruptured his Achilles that first season in New Orleans and missed the majority of his first two seasons there.

Jeremiah Fears is the point guard of the future in New Orleans, and he and Murray do not mesh well as a backcourt. This is best for everyone. That said, after injuries and a couple of down years because of it, the market for Murray is not going to bring a big return, so don't be surprised if this drags out into 2027 and closer to the February trade deadline.

The NBA is going to take its talents to South Beach.

The 2028 NBA All-Star Game will be in Miami, the first time the NBA's midseason showcase has been in the city since 1990 (Magic Johnson was your MVP that year). Miami has everything the NBA wants in a venue — warm weather, plenty of hotel rooms for fans, and players love the city and aren't looking for an excuse not to attend/skip town early — but the game traditionally falls on Presidents Day weekend, which is also when the city hosts the massive Miami International Boat Show. That show took up hotel and convention space the NBA needed, but in 2028 the boat show is moving back a weekend, reports the Miami Herald. The NBA stepped into that gap.

This is a win for everybody — Miami was made for this game.

• Cooper Flagg making history. Flagg is having a "he has arrived" season — looking every bit a top 15 player in the world already — and this past week he made history: Flagg passed LeBron James to be the teenager with the most 40+ point games in league history, and he also passed Kobe Bryant and now has scored the second most points as a teenager. He turns 20 next month.

• Lauri Markkanen is out with a wrist injury. Utah has dropped 12 straight, and this isn't helping matters: He has a non-displaced fracture in his right hand and will miss about a month (until mid-December). It likely means more touches for Darryn Peterson and Ace Bailey.• In the Jayson Tatum vs. Jaylen Brown showdown, Paul George was the star, reminding everyone he is still very good at basketball (and the Celtics got a convincing win). If he is healthy in April and May, the Celtics are a playoff threat in the East.

• The Bulls' Matas Buzelis and the Nets Josh Minott each have been fined $25,000 by the league for "making an obscene gesture towards the spectator stands." As a rule, the NBA frowns on players flipping off paying customers.

• Sacramento added veteran center Mason Plumlee to the roster. The Kings have a lot of centers on the roster, but that's not working as planned: Domantas Sabonis starts but is slumping, the future at the five may be Maxime Raynaud but he is injured, then there is Dylan Cardwell and, for small-ball lineups, Precious Achiuwa. Plumlee is not going to see a ton of minutes but the Kings could use the mentor.

#2768. PGA Tour Boards approve changes to competitive structure, eligibility

The PGA Tour Boards approved on Nov. 16 the Future Competition Committee's final set of recommendations for a new competitive structure beginning in 2028 and also signed off on a player development program that the Tour said will "strengthen the meritocracy and opportunity at each level."

The Policy Board and the Enterprises Board met in Boca Raton.

The Future Competition Committee was formed in August of 2025 to evaluate the PGA Tour's existing model and strengthen its long-term competitive and commercial future. The Committee's initial set of recommendations were announced in June with the goal of getting the top players competing in the same tournaments more frequently, with a "true promotion and relegation" system.

"Having been on this Tour for almost 30 years, I can confidently say the PGA Tour is in the best position it has ever been," said Future Competition Committee Chairman Tiger Woods in a statement. "We set out last year to build the strongest possible version of the PGA Tour, and by listening to our fans, players and partners, we created a competitive model grounded in meritocracy, built to last and that offers the best opportunity for the next generation of players."

PGA Tour Commissioner Brian Rolapp said during a virtual news conference with the media on Nov. 16 that the 18-month work of the FCC was officially at an end.

"The FCC's process was inclusive, rigorous and highly collaborate," he said. "We held 16 formal FCC meetings in addition to working groups, players meetings, Players Advisory Council discussions and board sessions ... had more than 40 conversations with PGA Tour corporate sponsors, engaged extensively with current and prospective media partners and retained experienced outside experts to help us evaluate competitive models and long-term economics."

"As we go into next season every player now knows what the are playing for and will have the opportunity to earn their spot for 2028," he said.

The two boards approved the model Rolapp laid out on Sept. 15: A Championship Series, with around two dozen events and at least $20 million purses, and a Challenger Series of around 20 tournaments with purses of at least $4 million. The two series will co-exist throughout the season, with the Championship Series culminating in a two-week Tour Championship that will end with a match play event to determine the season champion.

The Championship Series will start with the four major championships (Masters, U.S. Open, PGA Championship, British Open) and The Players Championship, along with familiar stops such as the Arnold Palmer Invitational in Orlando, Cadillac Championship at Doral, The Memorial in Dublin, Ohio, RBC Heritage on Hilton Head Island, S.C., The Travelers in Hartford, Conn., the Truist Championship in Charlotte, N.C., GO by Raymond James in Greensboro, N.C., and The Sentry at Torrey Pines.

Rolapp said bidding for title sponsorship of Championship Series event has ended and he said the Tour has "multiple bids under consideration for the final open PGA Tour Championship Series slots."

He said the Challenger Series "is similarly off to a fast start commercial," with signed agreements for four events and a "line of sight" for venues and partners for half the schedule."

The Tour will unveil its full schedule for the 2028 PGA Tour Championship Series, including sites for the 2028 Tour Championship, on Feb. 22, 2027, during a live Golf Channel broadcast event at PGA Tour headquarters in Ponte Vedra Beach.

In addition to giving players access off the Korn Ferry, the PGA Tour University Rankings of the top college players, and PGA Tour Americas, the Tour will partner with third-party operators on a network of affiliated events with the top players rewarded with competitive benefits throughout the year.

Those events could be domestic or international, with a list developed by next year.

The Tour also will have a unified ranking system for Korn Ferry membership, with the rankings updated on a weekly basis. The rankings will include non-PGA Tour or Korn Ferry Tour members and the top 75 will be exempt into each Korn Ferry Tour field.

The national qualifying tournament, currently held in Ponte Vedra Beach, will continue to provide more opportunties. The top five players in the qualifier will advance to the PGA Tour Challenge Series and the next 25 will earn Korn Ferry Tour membership.

Among the eligible players for the first season will be PGA Tour winners in 2026 and 2027. Those who won majors and The Players will be eligible through 2029; the top 30 players from the 2026 FedEx Cup points list; and the top 70 players from the 2027 FedEx Cup points list.

The members of the Challenger Series includes winners of FedEx Cup Fall and opposite events (held during the weeks of majors or Signature events) in 2027; Nos. 96-175 on the 2027 FedEx Cup Fall points list; Nos. 11-40 on the 2027 Korn Ferry Tour points list; and the top 10 players from the 2027 PGA Tour Q-School.

After 2028, the top 90 on the Championship Series points list will retain their status for 2029, joined by the top 20 on the Championship Series points list Going forward, winners of The Players and majors will get a three-year exemption (reduced from the current five years).

Rolapp also announced that Player Advisory Chairman Lucas Glover will join the PGA Tour Boards, with Adam Scott taking over the seat held by Patrick Cantlay, who is leaving the board. Scott's term will be three years.

He also took note of the end of Jay Monahan's tenure as PGA Tour Commissioner. Monahan had been the Commissioner since 2017 and guided the Tour through rock moments such as the COVID-19 pandemic and LIV Golf making its failed but expensive venture into the global golf landscape.

"His leadership has been guided by his love of the game, and by his love of the Tour," Rolapp said of Monahan. "During the most consequential moments in our sport's history, he never lost sight of what mattered most: strengthening the Tour and positioning it for the future."

Rolapp also paid tribute to NBC golf anchor Dan Hicks, who passed away last week.

"His passion for the game and his warm spirit left a warm impact on everyone who watched," Rolapp said.

#2766. T.J. Paganetti returns to Eagles as a consultant

The Eagles are currently averaging 88.6 rushing yards per game, which puts them at No. 28 in the league and one spot behind the Saints.

#2764. The Lakers’ struggle to maintain big leads is becoming impossible to ignore

Despite riding an eight-game winning streak and being tied with the San Antonio Spurs for the West's No. 1 seed, the 12-2 Los Angeles Lakers have continually squandered sizable leads. Rarely has it actually resulted in a loss — only once, against the Golden State Warriors on Oct. 30, when they blew a 38-15 first-quarter lead — but they're routinely allowing opponents back into games after building a comfortable advantage.

Most recently, they did so Sunday night in their 131-123 win over the Phoenix Suns. With 33.9 seconds remaining in the third quarter, the Lakers led 106-78. With 4:40 to go in the fourth, Phoenix had trimmed the deficit to six at 117-111. What should have been a final period allowing Luka Dončić to steal a bit more rest than normal became a crunch-time contest because Los Angeles couldn't manage its big lead.

"We're working too hard and we're playing too hard to build these leads. It's a disservice to each other to take your foot off. … You have to play for 48 minutes and we're going to need our defense to win us games," head coach JJ Redick said postgame. "These guys want to be a top-10 defense. We think we can be a good defensive team. I believe we can be a good defensive team. This has been consistent now. This is a trend. [We've] got to be better in the fourth quarter, but we did a lot of really good things to win the game."

Sunday was hardly the first instance this season in which the Lakers have spun an easy win into a pressurized late game scenario. Even ignoring towering first-quarter leads — which are often fool's gold in today's NBA with the breakneck pace and 3-point proliferation — like the ones they established against Golden State and the Detroit Pistons, they're still making a nasty habit of adding unnecessary stress to these wins.

They were up 16 with 2:28 left against the Toronto Raptors on Nov. 1 and brought in their end-of-bench players, who allowed Toronto to make it a five-point game with 43 seconds remaining, which prompted Redick to sub back in his starters. Five days later, a 13-point edge early with 4:21 to go against the Portland Trail Blazers was whittled down to five with 1:59 left.

According to NBA.com, the Lakers are second in first-quarter rating (plus-18.7), sixth in second-quarter net rating (plus-12.5), first in third-quarter net rating (plus-24.5) and 29th in fourth-quarter net rating (minus-24.4).

"In the fourth quarter, this whole season it's been kind of like a trend where it's just, we're kind of letting it get away from us. Maybe it's because we do have that lead and so we can get complacent, but it's something that we're obviously going to have to work on because we can't keep blowing double-digit leads in the fourth quarter," Jake LaRavia said Sunday. "We've had plenty of games where guys shouldn't be finishing the games and they're having to come back in and finish the games. We've got to do a better job collectively at finishing the game."

Dončić has been incredibly dominant this season. The Lakers have largely been tremendous as well. He's leading the charge but plenty of others are playing excellent basketball. They should be able to hold the fort for a few minutes when he exits with a big lead and give their superstar point guard a little more rest during these blowouts. Remedying that is critical — and they're fully aware.

"We know that's one of our weaknesses for that fourth quarter for sure when Luka goes out," Quentin Grimes said Sunday. "We got to just be better as a unit when he does go [off] the floor, still play our game but still play Laker basketball. Still be physical, still be tenacious on defense so we don't keep dwindling leads like that because it's a bad habit we've been doing."

Dončić knows it, too, and that concerning pattern was at front of mind during his postgame interview Sunday night, when he dropped 43 points and nine assists.

With their four-game road trip now in the rearview mirror, the Lakers return home to face the Charlotte Hornets Tuesday, when they'll hope to win their ninth consecutive contest and do so a little more easily than some of these recent victories.

"What it does is blame Jesus for consequences which were not his fault," Pique said in his first public comments on the decision.

Owono, 25, has said he is enduring the "hardest days of my life" after De Miguel's death, which Pique described as an "accident" that was "totally inexplicable".

FC Andorra coach Carles Manso said the goalkeeper will decide if and when he returns to football after the tragedy

"Jesus will need more than 12 games in order to be available and compete," said Pique.

"No suspension will bring Demi back to us," added Pique, referring to De Miguel by a popular nickname.

Spanish police sources have told AFP an investigation into what happened had been opened under judicial supervision, a standard procedure for unnatural deaths.

#2761. The NFL's Week 12 flex decision is looming

Thanksgiving week will entail a smorgasbord of football. But not every course will be particularly appetizing.

The NFL's brand-new embrace of the previously quiet night before Thanksgiving, only a few years after Black Friday was invaded, creates nine separate windows for the weekend — and seven standalone games.

The Wednesday night clash, pitting the 6-4 Packers against the 6-4 Rams, has huge implications for the slow-moving game of wild-card musical chairs in the NFC. The early Thanksgiving contest has the 7-2 Bears visiting the 7-2 Lions in a massive NFC North battle.

The Eagles-Cowboys game at 4:25 p.m. ET is becoming increasingly irrelevant — and it'll still get more than 40 million viewers because any game in that window would generate a huge audience. The Thursday night game, between the 7-2 Chiefs and the 8-1 Bills, will be very relevant to the AFC West championship and the No. 1 seed in the conference.

On Friday, it's the 7-2 Broncos at the 6-3 Steelers, which also has major playoff implications.

Then comes the late-afternoon Sunday window. At 4:25 p.m. ET, the 8-1 49ers host the 6-4 Seahawks.

Unfortunately, it doesn't end there. On Sunday night, the 5-5 Patriots (who likely won't have quarterback Drake Maye) visit the 0-8 Chargers.

If the NFL is going to slide that game out of prime time, Tuesday is the deadline for doing it. For the Eagles-Commanders game in Week 8, the league opted not to move a more consequential game into the Sunday night spot. Will the NFL again stand pat, keeping the Pats and Chargers as, to borrow a classic line from John Madden, the last shot out of a Roman candle?

The problem is that the playing of five games on Wednesday through Sunday leaves few options for Sunday night. In the 1:00 p.m. ET window, the games are Saints-Bengals, Raiders-Browns, Ravens-Texans, Giants-Colts, Jets-Dolphins, and Falcons-Vikings. Titans-Jaguars is due to begin at 4:05 p.m. ET, with Commanders-Cardinals being the other 4:25 p.m. ET game.

There's no Sunday afternoon game (other than Seahawks-49ers) that carries huge meaning for both teams. The best option for a flex would be Falcons-Vikings, since it's the first home game of Kirk Cousins 2.0 — and it comes against the team that signed him to a deal with multiple years of full guarantees before shocking everyone (including Cousins) the following month by selecting Michael Penix Jr. with the eighth overall pick in the draft.

The good news is that the Monday night game between the 4-5 Panthers and the 3-6 Buccaneers has direct relevance to the NFC South title, and the guaranteed postseason home game that comes with it. Still, the current Sunday night game stands out as being something that few will be interested in watching, especially after a five-day turkey-and-pigskin-induced coma.

In the end, it's always the league's decision. Under the rules the NFL has put in place, that decision is due to be made tomorrow.

#2760. NFL confirms Aidan Hutchinson's tackle of Drake Maye was not a hip drop

Lions defensive end Aidan Hutchinson and Patriots quarterback Drake Maye were injured on the same play Sunday. Hutchinson sacked Maye on third down with 9:28 left in the first quarter.

He ripped Maye down from behind, with his right arm reaching around Maye’s right shoulder pad. Hutchinson landed on Maye’s lower leg, injuring the quarterback’s ankle. Hutchinson seriously injured his right knee in the celebration.

Fox rules analyst Mike Pereira said on the broadcast that Hutchinson’s tackle was “not a hip drop.”

“Three things have to happen: You have to unweight yourself, swivel all around the back and land on the back of his leg below the knee,” Pereira said. “This was more of a horse collar-type pulled down from behind. [But] certainly not a horse collar penalty.”

Walt Anderson, the NFL’s officiating rules analyst, confirmed Pereira’s explanation through a league spokesman, via Mike Reiss of ESPN. The league said it was not a hip-drop tackle because Hutchinson did not swivel or unweight himself.

Patriots coach Mike Vrabel called Hutchinson’s tackle “just part of the game.”

“However it happened, I don’t think it was anything intentional,” he said postgame.

Maye remained in Germany for further evaluation, but he could miss the rest of the season with his injury. Hutchinson’s injury was season-ending, as he will need surgery to repair his torn ACL and other damage.

#2754. Are big leads safe with the Celtics now? They seriously might be.

Celtics fans felt like they were watching the same horror movie play out for the upteenth time when Boston blew a 22-point lead to the Detroit Pistons on Opening Say. The classic "they can't hold a lead" and "the Celtics are choke artists" takes flooded timelines throughout the community.

Frankly, those reactions were valid. This particular Cs regime has had its fair share of disappointing collapses in meaningful games. Dropping what should've been a surefire win against another top Eastern Conference opponent isn't the way anyone wanted to start this new season with a refurbished core.

Fast forward about a month, and Sunday's complete and utter beatdown of Cooper Flagg and the Dallas Mavericks was a reminder that it's #DifferentHere.

Boston jumped out to a quick 5-0 lead. Then it became 14-4. Then 31-6.

The Celtics' eventual 42-9 lead at the end of the first quarter set a record for the largest margin to open a game in franchise history.

A record-setting lead like that would be nearly impossible to blow. So it's not all that shocking that the Cs walked out with a win. The stability they played with, though, that was impressive. Boston led by many as 41, and Dallas never managed to get themselves within 25 points after falling behind early.

This was a true wire-to-wire display of dominance from the first-place Celtics. That dominance, though, isn't the expectation.

"It's not about hanging onto leads," head coach Joe Mazzulla told reporters postgame. "That's a passive mindset. It's not about building on them. It's understanding the ebbs and flows of the game and constantly answering the questions: Are we playing well? Having a good process? Or are we not playing well? And that's something you learn over the course of the season."

Fans don't really feel the same as Mazzulla does about leads. They expect a win once the Cs distance themselves from opponents. There's no debate as to whose opinion should matter more, but it's funny how different the viewpoints are.

Nonetheless, Boston answered those questions well on Sunday. The 16-point swing from being up 41 to 25 doesn't feel all that drastic when you've got such a luxurious cushion. It's still 16 points. If they were up 16 and allowed an opponent to tie, we'd all be talking like the sky is falling.

Even Dallas had peak momentum (if you could even call it that), the Celtics never panicked and maintained full control.

That's been the story ever since the start of the season. Sunday's win was the sixth straight for the Cs, who have won 11 of 12 since losing to the Pistons.

As the NBA's leader in point-differential, they've built their fair share of big leads. They've led by 12 or more in nine of their 11 wins.

In that same breath, you haven't heard much about them letting go of the rope. Boston's victories over the Chicago Bulls (twice), Milwaukee Bucks (the game in Milwaukee), and Charlotte Hornets all followed similar scripts to Dallas' demise.

These — for what it's worth — are all teams that you'd expect the Cs to keep down once they got them on the mat. The team did their part in keeping the fans happy in these spots.

In the other four big-lead spots, they met their coach's more realistic expectations. They managed to stay the course and remain steady when faced with the ebbs and flows of a typical NBA game.

This feels like their more important display of character. It's the fact that they're able to bend without breaking — something I vividly remember writing about it when they won the title in 2024.

It helps that their recent win over the Toronto Raptors brought Boston's "clutch" record to 5-1 this season. The Celtics' have actually been excellent in the final minutes of nailbiters. The've scored the ball well and made things difficult for opponents. Their 39.5 net rating is fourth-best in the NBA, as is their 5.7% turnover rate.

The sample size is exceptionally small, but these numbers carry the simple message that the Celtics lock in when the time calls for it. Even beyond the final five minutes of games, it's not hard to notice this. It's why they've held of big charges from the teams who actually manage to erase the large leads.

Frankly, there's no way I would've believed last season's team would stave off a surging Philadelphia 76ers team on the road like this group did last week.

Not only did they stop Philly's momentum, but they turned that close third-quarter battle into an absolute joke in the fourth quarter.

It's extremely early and the sample size is, again, small, but this Celtics team might be trying to tell us that they've got something special in there.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2750. Professor B: A contrarian view of Nets point guard dilemma

The Nets' loss to Memphis was bad in several ways, but many fans fastened on the starting lineup, which fell behind by eight points in the first 6 minutes, as the source of the trouble.

"Maybe start a PG—you have two decent ones in Traore and MBJ."

"We purposely handicapped ourselves by starting Egor at point guard. What was the point of that?"

"To Jordi, just play more Mikel Brown Jr. This team doesn't have a real PG. … Everyone tries to score on their own. Porter and Randle like to score on their own, especially Randle."

"That starting lineup Jordi put out today was absolutely disgrace to the fans and the game of basketball itself. What is he trying to prove? This isn't a fantasy game Jordi. Positions matter."

For the game as a whole, that starting lineup had a net rating of -2.7 per 100 possessions, hardly"disgraceful." (The team's net rating in the other 35 minutes was -24.5 per 100 possessions.) Theclamoring for a traditional point guard was mostly beside the point, a frustrated response to a badgame. Yet, it fit with a broader narrative questioning the Nets' lineups and offensive style in the firstdozen games of the season.

One frequent complaint is that rookie lottery pick Mikel Brown, Jr., has played just 31% of the team'sminutes, often out or limited due to an early-season ankle roll, "management" of a prior back injury, andillness. Fans are understandably eager to see this presumed key to the team's future on the court.Sophomore Nolan Traore has shown flashes in his absence, but he has played just 22 minutes per game,sitting frequently even when Brown has been unavailable, with the Nets relying instead on 6'9" EgorDëmin to man the point.

Meanwhile, veteran forward Julius Randle has led the team in usage, often serving as a hub of theoffense. Many fans have complained that Randle pounds the ball, stifling the flow of the offense andtaking shots from his teammates. Randle came to the Nets to be a "floor raiser," but some see him as anobstacle to progress and want to trade him as soon as possible.

An obvious problem with this narrative is that the Nets have played better without their "true" pointguards than with them. In 241 minutes with Traore on the court, they've been outscored by 10.1 pointsper 100 possessions. In 182 minutes with Brown on the court, they've been outscored by 11.3 points per100 possessions. In 185 minutes with neither on the court, they've outscored their opponents by 2.8points per 100 possessions. They have been a better-than-average NBA team when both sit, and one ofthe worst teams in the league when either is on the court!

Of course, those numbers depend a lot on who plays with whom. With small samples and a lot ofsquinting, it's possible to see promise—or doom and gloom—almost anywhere. But even looking at minutes shared with the Nets frontcourt starters, Traore has a net rating of +6.5, Brown (in just 18minutes) -10.4, and "not a point guard" Dëmin +21.9.

One logical reaction to that fact is to consider development the Nets' top priority. Traore and Brown areyoung players with a lot of potential, second year player Traore even younger than rookie Brown. Brown, in particular, is the Nets' highest draft choice in 16 years since before they made the move from Newark to Brooklyn.

Having wasted an entire season to be able to draft him, it only makes sense to double down on thatinvestment. If that dooms the Nets to bottom-dweller status this season, so be it. However, few of thefans clamoring for more of Traore and Brown make that argument. Most seem convinced—contrary towhat the stats say so far—that playing them more would produce immediate improvement.

NBA tracking data allow for a deeper dive into the performance of the Nets' top four ball-handlers, asmeasured by touches per minute on the court. The two "traditional" point guards, Traore and Brown,unsurprisingly lead the team in touches per minute. They've done a good job of limiting turnovers,which is often a fault of young ball-handlers. But their touches have been relatively unproductive,leading (directly or indirectly) to .26 and .24 points per touch, respectively. (The average scoring rate forthe team as a whole is .29 points per touch. That difference may sound small, but with almost 8offensive touches per minute of game action, it amounts to about 15 points per game.)

Randle, whose supposed ball-hogging and flow-stopping have made him a fan whipping boy for many ofthe Nets' early-season woes, has been massively more effective with the ball in his hands, generating.334 points per touch. And that is not just "trying to score on his own." He also has more assists pertouch (and more potential assists, including missed shots) than any of the Nets' other top ball-handlers.Despite slightly sub-par shooting efficiency and a rash of turnovers (easily the highest turnoverpercentage since his rookie season), he has been one of the Nets most effective offensive players so farthis season. (Only Michael Porter, Jr., and hot-shooting Moe Wagner have produced more points pertouch, and only Dëmin, Danny Wolf, and Day'Ron Sharpe have higher offensive ratings.)

Dëmin's ball-handling profile is distinctive, perhaps a reflection of the Nets' vision of him as a high-IQconnector. He holds the ball less than Brown, Randle, or Traore, and takes fewer shots per touch.

Nonetheless, Demin's assists and points per touch are roughly comparable to Traore's and Brown's. After anearly-season slump, his true-shooting percentage is now well above last season's, and well above theteam and league averages. Whether he operates more as a distributor or as an off-ball scorer may varyfrom night to night; but for now, his team-high offensive rating probably ensures that he will haveplenty of opportunity to refine his role(s).

How the Nets manage the tension between development and respectability over the course of thisseason remains to be seen. As fans, we can only hope that the performance of the youngsters improvesto the point that Jordi Fernandez and his staff see them as attractive options, even on a team that istrying to win.
