#2745. Preview: Nets look to get on track against a Sixers team without LeBron

It's been rough for Brooklyn, and on Monday night, they'll have unwelcoming division foe at Barclays Center.

Jaylen Brown and the new-look Philadelphia 76ers are coming to Brooklyn, and with the Nets sitting at 3-9, there could be plenty of opposing fans making themselves at home in the stands. If you were looking for a reason to watch LeBron James, then you're out of luck. He's out of this one due to low back/sciatica management — it's also the first night of a back-to-back for Philly.

The Nets return home following a 129-110 loss to the Memphis Grizzlies on Saturday, dropping to 3-9. Meanwhile, Philadelphia enters at 11-3, riding a much different wave of momentum. If recent history tells us anything, the Sixers have enjoyed their trips to Brooklyn — they're 6-2 in the last eight games at Barclays Center.

Who: Philadelphia 76ers (11-3) at Brooklyn Nets (3-9)

Let's start with the obvious. The Nets haven't exactly had Philadelphia's number lately, dropping three of four meetings last season, including both games at Barclays Center. Not exactly fond memories for the Brooklyn faithful.

Unfortunately for Brooklyn, this version of the Sixers isn't looking any easier.

Philadelphia is coming off a 131-124 victory over Indiana on Sunday, with Jaylen Brown scoring 31 points and Tyrese Maxey adding 27. LeBron James contributed 15 points and 11 assists, passing former Net Jason Kidd for third on the NBA's all-time assists list. Not bad for a guy who's been in the league longer than some of Brooklyn's players have been alive.

The Nets, meanwhile, are hoping to bounce back from Saturday's loss to Memphis. Julius Randle led Brooklyn with 22 points, while Day'Ron Sharpe added 16 points and 11 rebounds.

The Grizzlies jumped out to a 38-23 lead after the opening quarter Saturday, leaving the Nets playing catch-up for most of the night. Against a Philadelphia team with this much offensive firepower, that's a dangerous habit. For Brooklyn to have a chance Monday, it'll need to bring more urgency from the opening tip, take better care of the basketball, and make Philadelphia work defensively.

The one advantage? The Sixers are playing their second game in as many nights. Maybe the Nets can take advantage.

And some late news: Josh Minott is indeed frustrated about his playing time. On Saturday, he threw up an obscene gesture at the Smoothie King Center which the NBA caught on camera and on Monday fined him $25,000.

Joshua Jefferson — OUT (G League Assignment)

Dominick Barlow — OUT (Injury/Illness – Low Back Stress Reaction)

Adem Bona — OUT (Injury/Illness – Cervical Spine Sprain)

Joel Embiid — Questionable (Injury/Illness – Right Foot Plantar Fascia Injury Management)

LeBron James — OUT (Injury/Illness – Low Back/Sciatica Management)

We originally had LeBron James, but he was deemed out in the afternoon. But seriously, who else were we going to pick? The 'King' has been making life miserable for the Nets for more than two decades, dating all the way back to their days in New Jersey, averaging 27.6 points, 7.2 rebounds, and 7.6 assists against the franchise, compiling a 41-23 record.

As for Jaylen Brown, he's averaging 21.9 points per game — his lowest since he was 23 year old during the 2019-20 season. Context is key here, though. The Sixers are stacked with talent, so much to the point that James is crusing at 41 years old and Joel Embiid is far from his prime self, yet the Sixers are still 11-3.

Headlines surrounding Brown have been all about Jayson Tatum, but he's quietly having one of his most efficient season ever, shooting 49.5% from the field (2nd-best in his career) and 38.7% from three (3rd-best in his career). The 2023-24 Finals MVP is an asset no matter where he goes, no matter who he's surrounded by. He propelled a Celtics team who didn't have Jayson Tatum all last season. Now, he's bought into what Philly's provides, which varies depending on the night.

With James out and Embiid questionable, expect him to try and get hot early. Maybe there's added incentive against the Nets.

#2744. Arkansas State reportedly fires coach Butch Jones in sixth season amid losing skid

Arkansas State fired Jones on Monday, according to The Athletic's Chris Vannini. The move, which has not yet been made public, comes with two games left in the regular season.

Jones has been at Arkansas State since 2021. He'll now finish with a 30-43 record with the Red Wolves, though Jones has led the program to three straight bowl games, most recently the Xbox Bowl last season, and back-to-back winning records.

But this season has not been great. The Red Wolves have lost four of their last six games and now sit at just 4-6 on the year. They can still earn a trip to a bowl game if they win their last two games. They'll take on Louisiana Tech next on Saturday before wrapping up the regular season against Troy on Nov. 28.

Arkansas State marked the fourth head coaching job that Jones has had in his career. He got his start at Central Michigan, and had a short stint at Cincinnati before he was hired at Tennessee in 2013. Jones led the Volunteers for five seasons and compiled a 34-27 record before he was fired during the 2017 campaign. Jones did spend time as an assistant under Nick Saban at Alabama before landing in Jonesboro.

According to USA Today, Arkansas will owe Jones more than $1.4 million.

#2743. Robert Saleh: We've put more on Cam Ward's plate and he's proving he can handle it

The Titans lost their third straight game on Sunday, but head coach Robert Saleh continued to like what he saw from quarterback Cam Ward during Jacksonville’s 40-23 win.

Ward was 28-of-39 for 231 yards, two touchdowns and an interception while running three times for 39 yards. That marked a season-high in completion percentage for Ward and he has completed at least 64.5 percent of his passes in all three of the losses.

That run coincides with what Saleh called a decision to “shift our philosophy with how we wanted to approach offense and put more in his plate with regard to being a general at the line of scrimmage.” Saleh said that Ward has done “a really nice job taking command” and has shown the Titans what they were hoping to see in the process.

“To be fair to Cam, we’ve said from the beginning we were going to give him every opportunity to prove that he is a franchise quarterback of the Tennessee Titans,” Saleh said at a Monday press conference. “So, philosophically, the change was: How are we going to evaluate whether or not is he capable of being our franchise quarterback if we don’t give him the opportunity to prove it? And I think over the last three weeks he has done that. Or, at least is doing it.”

The Titans scored 84 points in their first six games and they’ve put up 83 in the three games since the tweak in their offensive approach. That step in the right direction has not resulted in wins, but it is reason to hope that will change if Ward is able to sustain the improvement that the Titans have seen over the last few weeks.

#2742. 49ers stun Cowboys, Chiefs and Bengals impress - NFL week 10

The NFL's brightest stars lit up week 10 of the 2026 season with some astonishing plays on Sunday.

The likes of Brock Purdy, Christian McCaffrey, Patrick Mahomes, Jahmyr Gibbs and Ja'Marr Chase showcased their skills with some explosive action to help their teams to victory.

The San Francisco 49ers produced a historic display in Dallas while there were also big wins for the Kansas City Chiefs, Detroit Lions and Cincinnati Bengals.

It means the 49ers and Buffalo Bills continue to share the league's best record (8-1), while five teams moved to 7-2, including the in-form Jacksonville Jaguars.

Christian McCaffrey scored his final touchdown just as the San Francisco 49ers' general manager John Lynch made his way to the sideline in the fourth quarter [Getty Images]

The San Francisco 49ers and Dallas Cowboys have a fierce rivalry stretching back decades and they delivered another classic with a shootout in Texas.

Brock Purdy showed why he is among the leading contenders for the Most Valuable Player award as the 49ers won 46-40, racking up 584 yards of offence - the most Dallas have ever allowed at home.

San Francisco had three touchdowns of more than 65 yards, while Purdy threw for a career-high 389 yards and three touchdowns. The quarterback even blocked for one of the 49ers' other three scores, a 70-yard run by Deebo Samuel on a reverse trick play.

The highlights were two Purdy passes to Christian McCaffrey. The first saw Purdy show remarkable poise as he scrambled out of the pocket before finding McCaffrey for a 74-yard score, while the second was a perfectly-executed play over the middle that saw McCaffrey sprint in untouched from 66 yards.

Dual-threat running back McCaffrey finished with three scores and 223 total yards, including a career-high 161 receiving yards.

Purdy's MVP rival Josh Allen had a relatively quiet game as the Buffalo Bills won 31-12 at the New York Jets, while the Jacksonville Jaguars won 40-23 at the Tennessee Titans.

Including the end of last season, the Jaguars have won 15 of their past 17 regular-season games and must be considered Super Bowl contenders, especially after bringing in three key defensive players before the trade deadline on Tuesday.

Joe Burrow became the new MVP favourite last week and continued his superb form as the Cincinnati Bengals romped to a 38-18 win in their divisional match-up against the Pittsburgh Steelers.

New Manchester United boss Mauricio Pochettino was among the crowd as Burrow was involved in four touchdowns for the fifth straight game, tying an NFL record.

The Bengals quarterback had 300 passing yards before half-time and finished with 409. Most of them were to Ja'Marr Chase, who claimed 231 yards from just 11 catches for the 7-2 Bengals.

Their divisional rivals, the Cleveland Browns, pulled off another comeback as they recovered from 17-0 down to beat the Houston Texans 27-24.

The Browns have won their opening four home games for the first time since 1963 and are now 6-4.

Quarterback Deshaun Watson led the Browns to a game-winning drive in the fourth quarter of all six of those wins and he said this one was "definitely special" as it came against his former team.

Amon-Ra St Brown met Bayern Munich forward Jamal Musiala at the Allianz Arena and waved the German flag before and after Sunday's game [Getty Images]

Munich staged the penultimate international game of the season and just playing in Germany was a "dream come true" for the Detroit Lions' wide receiver Amon-Ra St Brown.

The 27-year-old's mother is German, and he and his two brothers speak the language fluently and have been regular visitors to their mother's homeland.

St Brown, who arrived wearing lederhosen and waved the German flag as he ran on to the field, scored two touchdowns as the Lions charged to a 35-10 win over the New England Patriots.

Yet St Brown was outshone by team-mate Jahmyr Gibbs, who had 99 receiving yards, 153 rushing yards and two touchdowns, taking him to 62 overall - a new NFL record in a player's first four seasons.

The Patriots lost Drake Maye to an ankle injury when he was sacked on the game's opening drive by Aidan Hutchinson, who injured his knee as he celebrated, and the Lions fear it is a significant injury.

Detroit, the Minnesota Vikings and Chicago Bears are now all 7-2 in the ultra-competitive NFC North, with the Vikings bouncing back after suffering back-to-back defeats.

Kirk Cousins returned to Minnesota before the trade deadline on Tuesday and the veteran quarterback led them to a 30-10 win at their other divisional rival, the 6-4 Green Bay Packers, with their defence claiming eight sacks - the most in a single game this season.

The Las Vegas Raiders allowed Cousins to rejoin Minnesota as they felt Fernando Mendoza, this year's first overall draft pick, was ready to become their starting quarterback.

Mendoza's first start came against the Seattle Seahawks, the Super Bowl champions, and he managed to run in a 10-yard touchdown on the Raiders' opening drive.

But the Raiders' offence then struggled and Mendoza was sacked six times as the Seahawks won 21-13.

"It's good to get punched in the face because now it's a great opportunity to grow and learn," said Mendoza.

Seattle quarterback Drew Lock made two big touchdown passes on the run, one to Cooper Kupp and the other a 71-yard score by Jaxon Smith-Njigba.

How mum's struggle inspired Mendoza's rise to top NFL draft pick

The Kansas City Chiefs' run game has caught the eye so far this season, in particular new signing Kenneth Walker, but he was out injured on Sunday as Patrick Mahomes reminded everyone he has one of the best arms in the business.

For the first time since 2022, the three-time Super Bowl winner threw for five touchdowns, plus 348 yards, as Kansas City won 35-13 at the Atlanta Falcons.

Running back Emmett Johnson stepped up in the absence of Walker to claim 101 rushing yards, 70 receiving yards and two touchdowns.

Mahomes made explosive plays to Cyrus Allen and Andrew Armstrong for their first NFL scores, while fellow wide receiver Josh Downs scored on his debut after the Chiefs traded for him on deadline day.

That took Mahomes to 332 career touchdown passes, including the play-offs, putting the 31-year-old top of the all-time list for the most in a player's first 10 seasons.

The Chiefs are 7-2 and will need their receiving corps to ease the burden on Walker during a tough five-game stretch after next week.

Smyth scores 55-yard field goal in Saints defeat

#2741. What We Learned from the Spurs win over the Jazz

Those of us who have been roaming the beat of hyper-focused amateur basketball blogging for long enough know that, at any given moment, we are prone to spouting off the odd exaggeration or two. An embellishment, if you will. Saying something as innocuous as "that's one of the coolest plays I've ever seen" is a part of the set decoration around here and, if I could take you behind the curtain even further, I'd venture to say that unsheathing our hyperbolic sword to wax poetic about the tragedies and triumphs of regular old basketball moments is actually the whole gig. It's our mandate from heaven. From the mundane, we must divine the remarkable.

Now, taking that rather large grain of salt and tucking it away in your subconscious, I invite you to entertain the idea that the 1st quarter of basketball the Spurs played against the Warriors on Friday night was maybe the greatest 12 minutes of basketball I've seen this group play. Ever? I mean, who has the time to research forever, but for the sake of this conversation, let's say, yeah, that was the best stretch I've ever seen. Everything worked. They opened the game on an 18-0 run and the quarter ended 38-14. The offense seemed to flow in and out of infinite possibilities at random. A three, a dunk, a layup, a middie. The basket glowed in a shining iridescence of joy and wonder at the possibilities before it.

The defense, on the other hand, appeared to absorb the deep grey of the NBA Cup court and became a void into which offense flattened out into a shadow of itself. The Warriors, 5-for-25 in the quarter, were swallowed up in this cloud, shrinking and disappearing into nothingness while our silver-clad demons continued to gleefully romp through this realm of dreams and nightmares.

That quarter was so insane that I'm still sitting here thinking about it four days later. In fact, as I watched that very same group of basketball gods look exceedingly mortal out there in Utah yesterday afternoon, the vividness of their display against the Warriors only became more stark in comparison. As inevitable as the Spurs seemed the other night, the letdown was equally so.

Sunday afternoon was a slog. An uphill battle against a team desperate to find a foothold in this season. You could feel the wild energy radiating off the Jazz the entire game. A burbling, untethered sort of raw power that seemed equally likely to hit the target or harm innocent bystanders. Mo Bamba went 6-for-6 from three and 0-for-3 at the line. There was nothing precise or controlled about their approach; instead, they seemed animated by an unseen force trying to make something, anything, out of what is rapidly becoming a lost season.

Into this tempest rode the good ship Spurs, and immediately the goal was to weather it. Survive and endure. Perfection is not something that can be found in a storm, and so they abandoned it. Everything became less fluid and more practical. They held back, they picked their spots, and they took the opportunities provided to them.

Even as the Jazz surged ahead again and again, the Spurs calmly stayed the course. They applied the pressure slowly and deliberately until the end when, their opponent's frenzied blows finally slowing, they steadily pushed through and saw it out. No real theatrics. No otherworldly displays. Just a group playing with a sort of dogged determination and certainty that, in the end, their process would take them where they needed to go.

The contrast of these two performances is fascinating to observe side by side, although, as we've discussed ad nauseam, it's hard to know exactly how much, if any, of this translates toward the ultimate goal of redemption at the end of the season. The Warriors were without Curry. The Jazz were without hope. Every team the Spurs face has some sort of flaw or hindrance that keeps them from revealing the true nature of these Spurs, and so we are left to our own devices. Forced to spend the next few months reading tea leaves and trying to understand what exactly these guys might be capable of.

The idea that the Spurs have the ability to touch perfection, to wield it, if only for a moment, is a tantalizing one. It's an exciting new dimension to their ceiling, and the thought of them unleashing it in a playoff atmosphere sends chills down my spine. You can conjure that vision in your mind, can't you? The crowd feeding off it. The sounds growing louder. The players bouncing and vibrating off each other in a kind of combustion designed to obliterate their opponent from the face of the earth. Somewhere in there, the light goes strange and your voice drops an octave. We would be conquerors. We would be kings. We would be gods, beautiful and terrible as the Dawn!

But we've seen how that goes in the playoffs, haven't we? We've seen this group in their bag against elite competition and we've seen how quickly that high can dissipate until all of a sudden there's a vacuum. It's cold and dark when that power deserts you and, in that void, you can lose track of how you climbed that mountain in the first place.

These Spurs didn't have an answer last June when they reached for divine inspiration and came up empty. They became shells of themselves and watched as the tide washed over them, extinguishing what was left of their dream.

As Stephon Castle casually loped down the court before throwing down a nonchalant windmill dunk to officially put this game to bed yesterday, I felt a profound satisfaction come over me. In the 4th quarter, they calmly erased the Jazz's 5-point lead. Dylan Harper, quietly and competently, poured in 18. Victor controlled the paint, Steph, Devin and Keldon repeatedly took advantage of Utah's desperation, and before you knew it the game was over. It was always over. It was over before it started.

It wasn't the greatest 12 minutes of basketball I've seen this group play, but, in a way, that was even more impressive.

Sure, so I maybe downplayed the extent to which Dylan Harper's 4th quarter was amazing up there because it fit the narrative I was trying to sell. So sue me! The hyperbole sword cuts both ways! But that was main body Charlie, and we're in Takeaway land now, where we're free to say whatever the hell we want about Harper's 4th quarter takeover. It's telling that the Spurs don't need him to become an apex predator all the time, but it's a remarkable card that they get to play in certain spots. The Spurs had spent the night being mildly competent, getting mostly the same looks they're used to and simply not executing. Shots weren't falling. Passes were slightly off, cuts were a step slow. The beautiful game wasn't so beautiful at the moment, and what it needed was a tool to cut through the red tape. Harper turned into a machete. He looked fast, thorough, and sharp as a tack. Put simply, he just relentlessly looked like the best player on the court. It was a career high in points for him, a record that I'm sure will be fleeting. What a player.

I'm almost sad that the Spurs relative competence erased the 6 of 6 miracle night from Mo Bama. Genuinely incredible thing to witness.

The Luke Kornet situation is so fascinating. The effect of having Wembanyama around makes everything else really difficult to contextualize. Is Kornet bad at basketball, or is he just normal and the dropoff from Vic's insanity so steep that it makes you feel like you're getting the rug pulled out from under you? You can even see it with our guys. They're out there throwing weird, very Wemby-coded lob passes to this poor guy who's just trying to crash the basket, and he looks silly when the ball glances off his hands or the backboard or whatever. Meanwhile we're all sitting there like, "Geez, we gotta figure out this backup center situation." But I truly don't know what there is to be done. Vic can't play 48 minutes. Whoever comes in is never going to be him.

I can feel the walls closing in a little bit on Keldon Johnson's time as a Spur. Maybe it won't be this year, but it feels like something eventually might have to give where we can't afford to pay him we he deserves to get paid. I dunno. I'm trying not to think about it and I'm hoping I'm wrong. I'm so glad he has a mustache this year.

Aren't mustaches sort of played out at this point?

Yeah, like, weren't we doing ironic mustaches in the hipster community around 2009? People were tattooing little mustaches on their finger as a bit? You don't think we've overplayed that hand as a society?

I don't think we, as a society, are capable of speaking out as one and declaring anything definitively good, bad, cool, dumb or otherwise. If you don't like a mustache, that's your business. I think the courage and self confidence it takes to only grow out the hair on your upper lip reflects something about what's going on in your soul. Mustaches can be evil. They can be goofy. They can be stoic. They can be boring. They can represent the apex of what it means to be middling, no nonsense, run-of-the-mill accounting type or it can be the pièce de résistance on the most interesting man in the world. Masculinity is a spectrum upon which the mustache squarely sits.

What do you think Keldon's mustache says about him?

Says he can pull off a hook shot in traffic, I'll tell you that much.

#2739. Mike Vrabel disagrees with Milton Williams's claim that field conditions caused injuries

On Sunday in Germany, both teams had key players go down with significant injuries. And both injuries could be season-ending.

After the game, Patriots defensive tackle Milton Williams blamed the injuries suffered by Lions defensive end Aidan Hutchinson (knee) and Patriots quarterback Drake Maye (ankle) on the playing surface in Munich.

"Two top players in this league, probably got season-ending injuries playing on that [field]," Williams said, via Andrew Callahan of the Boston Herald. "It was bad. But like I said, I ain't making no excuses. [The Lions] executed. They played on the same field we did, and they executed and made more plays than we did. Give them credit."

On Monday, Patriots coach Mike Vrabel disagreed with Williams's opinion.

"I don't think Drake Maye's injury was caused by the grass," Vrabel told reporters, "and certainly we've seen other players that have celebrated or have left their feet and come down and had issues at that. So the field was the same for both teams, and that's kind of what the field was."

Vrabel stopped short of commenting on whether the field was an issue independent of the injuries. But he's right; it's the same for both teams.

Still, we know by now that every field is different. And there's surely a difference between American fields that host up to 10 games per year every year and the international one-off surfaces.

#2738. Three Stars: Ohio State bombards Prairie View A&M in the second half, wins 86-59

After getting thumped by UConn over the weekend, the Ohio State men's basketball team (3-2) returned home Monday night for the start of a four-game homestand. The Buckeyes won't go on the road again until they travel to Lincoln for the Big Ten opener on December 2.

On Monday night, Ohio State faced off against a fellow Land-Grant university — Prairie View A&M. The Panthers came into Monday night's game with a 2-3 record, having beaten two non-Division-I schools to start the season and have since lost games to UT Rio Grande Valley, SMU, and Vanderbilt.

Rolling with the same starting lineup that he's used all season long (Justin Pippen, John NMobley Jr., Anthony Thompson, Amare Bynum, and Andrija Jelavic), Jake Diebler's Buckeyes didn't come out scorching the nets by any means, but did manage to double up Prairie View 16-8 by the under-12 media timeout with 11:51 remaining in the first half. Jimmie Williams carried over his impressive play from over the weekend (17 points against UConn) with two early three-pointers on Monday night.

Diebler and Ohio State made a pretty obvious effort to get Thompson going early, with the freshman taking and missing four shots in the first 10 minutes of Monday. Despite his struggles so far this year, (5-for-22 shooting entering this game) Ohio State got him the ball as often as they could early in this game.

Ohio State was never able to open up a double-digit lead on Prairie View in the first half, with the two teams trading baskets for much of the opening 20 minutes. Ohio State went to the halftime locker room with a 34-29 lead, with Jimmie Williams leading the pack with 11 points on 3-for-6 shooting. As a unit, Ohio State shot 32.1% in the first half, and was 5-for-17 (29.4%) from three-point range. Prairie View A&M's leading scorer Michael Eley came into Monday night's game averaging 15.4 points per game, and had 13 points on 6-for-10 shooting in the first half.

Pippen opened the second half with a three-pointer and it took Ohio State just over three minutes in the second half to push the lead to double-digits, 42-32. The Buckeye defense locked in and forced shot clock violations on back-to-back possessions heading into the under-16 media timeout. For the first time in the entire game, the Buckeyes looked fully engaged and effective on that end of the floor.

Despite the Panthers' best effort to delay the inevitable, the dam eventually did break. The Pippen three-pointer to open the second half was the beginning of what would become a 14-3 run for Ohio State, stretching the lead out to 63-44 with just under nine minutes remaining in the game. Even Thompson got in on the action with a nifty up-and-under layup in transition, which was his first basket of the game.

A pair of free throws (initiated by a technical foul on the Prairie View coaching staff), followed up by a huge dunk by Josh Ojianwuna put Ohio State up 73-50. A Pippen three — his third of the game — put Ohio State up 81-55 and Diebler began subbing the starters out with two minutes left.

It was a real life embodiment of the "They had us in the first half" meme, but Ohio State cleaned up their act in the back 20 minutes and blew out Prairie View, 86-59. With the win, Ohio State improved to 12-0 all-time against the Southwestern Athletic Conference (SWAC).

In honor of the Ohio State men's hockey team upsetting No. 1 Wisconsin over the weekend, here's a hockey-style "Three Stars of the Game" for Ohio State, although "stars" might be stretching it just a bit for this particular game:

For the second consecutive game, Williams was the one pressing the issue on offense for Ohio State. The senior from Solon had 11 points in the first half for the Buckeyes, including a pair of three-pointers. He was 3-for-6 in the opening frame, while the rest of the Buckeyes shot a combined 6-for-22 (27.2%).

Pippen had a solid offensive game on Monday night, and it seemed like his three-pointer less than 40 seconds into the second half set the tone for Ohio State for the final 20 minutes. However, his defense against Prairie View's leading scorer, Michael Eley was more impactful. After scoring 13 in the first half, Eley struggled to get going in the second half, hitting two of his first eight shots and going 1-for-3 at the free throw line.

On a night when Mobley was within 1,000 career points and could have forced things to get there, Ohio State's junior guard instead moved the ball when nothing was there for him, seeking out the best possible shot on offense. He was able to connect with Bynum on several lobs to the basket in the second half as the Buckeyes began to push the pace a bit more and get Prairie View backpedaling on defense.

Mobley entered the game needing 24 points to reach 1,000 in his Ohio State career. He finished with 10 points on 3-for-6 shooting, which means he'll need 14 against UMBC on Thursday night to reach 1,000.

The Magic remain without Paolo Banchero, who is out with a right high ankle sprain. Jonathan Isaac (left knee injury management), Noah Penda (partially torn right hamstring), and Izaiyah Nelson (left ankle injury recovery) are also out.

Jalen Suggs and Tristan da Silva have both returned from injuries and are off the injury report. Franz Wagner has carried the load without Banchero, averaging 24.4 points, 8.3 assists, and 6.8 rebounds per game.

The Celtics beat the Magic 108-97 in Orlando on November 1st.

Celtics-Magic tips off on Monday night at 7pm ET.

#2733. The extensive officiating adjustments worked, but were they necessary?

Friday’s nationwide ground stop didn’t affect any of the NFL teams that needed to travel from one place to the other for Week 10 games. It dramatically affected the officiating assignments.

But not because the officials assigned to work the various games couldn’t get to where they needed to be. The league decided to reshuffle the deck in order to avoid the mere possibility that the officials wouldn’t get to their assigned games.

It was a proactive approach by the notoriously reactive NFL. But it still may have been an overreaction. And it introduced the risk that something unusual could have occurred.

For the twelve games played on Sunday, the assigned referee worked only two of the games. One of those games was played on Sunday morning (New York time) in Germany, where travel undoubtedly occurred before Friday’s shutdown of the aviation system.

Sunday night’s game was due to be worked by Clete Blakeman as the referee, with umpire Scott Walker, down judge Andy Warner, line judge Kent Payne, field judge Karina Tovar, side judge James Coleman, back judge Jonah Monroe, replay official Chad Adams, and replay assistant Adam Choate.

Instead, from the official Game Book, this was the crew: referee Don Willard, umpire Terry Killens, down judge Dana McKenzie, line judge Jay Bilbo, field judge Jabir Walker, side judge Justin Larrew, back judge Dino Paganelli.

Chad Adams was still the replay official; however, all replay officials reportedly went to the league office and not their game sites.

Willard was not scheduled to work in Week 10; he ultimately served as the referee for two games. Killens was due to work the Jaguars-Titans game as a member of referee Alex Moore’s crew; like Willard, Killens worked Dolphins-Colts and Steelers-Bengals. McKenzie, like Killens, is a member of Moore’s crew, which was due to be in Nashville.

Bilbo is a member of referee Shawn Smith’s crew, which was due to work Panthers-Saints. Walker, who also worked two games, is a member of Land Clark’s crew, which was scheduled to work the Chargers-Ravens game on Monday night.

Larrew, the fourth two-games-in-one-day official, is assigned to referee Shawn Hochuli’s crew, which was scheduled to work the Chiefs-Falcons game. Paganelli is also a member of referee Shawn Smith’s crew, which was due to work Panthers-Saints.

Monday night’s game, by the way, will be handled by referee Adrian Hill. Hill’s crew was scheduled to work the Rams-Cardinals game on Sunday.

While we don’t have the time, the patience, or frankly the desire to prepare a full list of the crews that were supposed to handle the 11 domestic Sunday games and the crews that actually did, the fact that 10 of the 11 referees ended up elsewhere says it all.

Here’s the real question. Were they necessary? In lieu of performing major surgery to the officiating lineup for 11 Sunday games (and, apparently, the Monday night game), the NFL could have committed to getting every official where they needed to be, for the games they were scheduled to work.

Everyone else got to where they needed to be. The teams, the broadcasters, everyone. It presumably could have been done, if the NFL had decided to commit any and all resources to making it happen.

Which brings us to an inescapable conclusion. In order to avoid the hassle — and the expense — of ensuring that the officiating crews made it to their assigned locations before kickoff on Sunday, the league had no qualms about making massive, eleventh-hour changes to the assignments. Which included the unprecedented move of requiring four officials to work a game, to take a two-hour ride to another city, and to work another game.

For the details on working two games in one day, check out the story from Kalyn Kahler about the unprecedented doubleheader for four officials.

“It’s exhausting because you’re looking for something on every single play,” ESPN rules analyst and former NFL V.P. of replay Russell Yurk told Kahler regarding the difficulty of working two games in a day. “You’re analyzing every single play. You’re watching your players. You’re watching your keys, you’re making judgment calls, and it doesn’t really let up the entire game.”

Fox rules analyst and former NFL senior V.P. of officiating Dean Blandino called assigning officials to two games a “last resort.”

"[I]t would be like playing two football games back-to-back,” Blandino told Kahler. “There’s the physical aspect of running and the mental exhaustion after a game because you are doing a pre-snap routine for every snap, and then the eight-second play, and then a post-snap routine.

“The officials look like they are not exerting themselves the same way the players are, but they don’t get breaks. They don’t play one side of the ball. They are out there every snap and every kick. Then to say now I have to reset without any recovery time? That is a challenge.”

The league could have embraced the challenge (and the expense) of getting all officials where they needed to be. It surely would have been easier than completely disrupting the existing assignments, forcing officials to work games they hadn’t prepared to handle, and requiring NFL executive Ramon George (whom the league declined to make available for an interview with Kahler) to move the various pieces around in a way that made it all work, even if the “last resort” of using four officials for two games had to be used.

Given the wealth and power of the NFL (most if not all owners have a private jet, or jets), the league could have gotten everyone where they needed to be. Either it wanted the risk of a single snafu to be as small as possible (even if that meant blowing up the existing assignments) or it hoped to make the expense as small as possible.

It’s hard not to think cost was a factor. And the NFL is very fortunate that Sunday’s games went smoothly, after the major disruption to the officials’ itineraries.

Still, these games are too important for a rolling of dice or the pinching of pennies, relatively speaking. It would have been, in our view, far more prudent to open the checkbook and spend whatever was needed to get the officials to the places where the officials were originally assigned to be.

#2730. Arizona women’s basketball signs top 15 class for 2027

Arizona head coach Becky Burke welcomed a top 20 class this season. The class she signed for next season is even better.

Burke signed a class of four on Wednesday, Nov. 11. The 2027 group is ranked 10th by ESPN and 15th by 247sports. ESPN gives all four signees four-star ratings.

The 2026 class, which was ranked No. 17 by ESPN and No. 20 by 247sports, had a heavy focus on athletic guards. The three top-ranked recruits—Jasleen Green, Makayla Presser-Palmer, and Simba Finley—are all listed as either a guard or guard/forward on this year's roster. Next year, the focus is on size.

"I think we we addressed that with the 2026 class," Burke said. "I think we addressed it even more with the 2027 class, and then just people that give us different looks. We didn't sign another Jas in the 2027 class. We signed a player that could complement her very well. We didn't sign a Bre [Williams]. We signed another big that will complement her well. So, really getting a variety of skill sets. You know, getting people that are going to complement one another versus just tacking the same players on top of each other. But we were 6, 6-1, 6-3, 6-4. I mean, with those four, so yeah, we definitely did address the the length and size for sure."

There is disagreement about which signee is the headliner of the class, although the majority leans towards 6-foot small forward/wing Brieana Brown. Others put 6-foot-3 post Celsy Colombo in that position. The other two members of the group are 6-foot wing Corinne Lomax and 6-foot-2 forward Ashley Vande Ven.

Brown comes in as the 40th-ranked player in the ESPN Sportscenter Next 100. That's the highest-ranked recruit of Burke's career.

Rivals is the next service that has Brown as Arizona's top recruit, but there is conflict about the rankings on that website. On the main recruiting rankings, Rivals has her at No. 46 overall, No. 11 at the small forward position, and No. 4 in the state of California. On her individual recruit page, the service places her at No. 57 overall, No. 12 at the small forward position, and No. 5 in the state of California.

Prep Girls Hoops also puts Brown atop the list of Arizona recruits. That service places her No. 74 overall.

While Prospects Nation doesn't have Brown as the top player in the class, she isn't far behind one of her teammates. She is the No. 47 player overall and No. 16 forward according to that service.

247sports and Prospects Nation both have Colombo as the top incoming recruit. ESPN does not rank her among the top 100, but she does have a four-star rating on the site.

Prospects Nation has the post as the No. 36 player in the class. She's listed as the No. 11 power forward.

247sports has Colombo No. 38 nationally, No. 10 at the power forward position, and No. 2 in the state of New York in its internal rankings. The composite, which is an average of her rankings across several services, has her at No. 48 overall, No. 11 at her position, and No. 2 in the state. Colombo is the only player in the class ranked by 247sports.

Lomax is second on the list of Arizona recruits according to ESPN. The wing is ranked No. 65 overall. While Prospects Nation does not rank her among its top 60 players, she is one of its Elite 150 which includes players who don't receive individual rankings but are considered among the best prospects in the class.

Vande Ven comes in at No. 82 according to ESPN. The Canadian is also listed in the Elite 150 of Prospects Nation.

With two strong high school classes back-to-back and a transfer class that included several players with multiple years of eligibility, Burke is looking towards the future. She knows it's tough to keep a group intact these days, though.

"I was telling Bre a couple weeks ago, if this group wants to stick around to build something special here in Arizona, they have that opportunity to do that," Burke said. "This thing could get really fun and really scary in a hurry…but these days, it takes a committed group that care about the right things and really, really, truly want to build this thing and stick around."

#2729. Brian Schottenheimer on confidence in Christian Parker: We're still working through the process

The Cowboys may not choose to make a change, but it’s hard to feel confident that there’s going to be a major difference between their play over the first 10 weeks and their play over the rest of the season if everything about the defensive approach remains the same.

#2728. Steelers grades: Aaron Rodgers, Mike McCarthy trying too hard to relive their glory years

We're going to do something a bit different this week. Usually, you'd see Joey and I going through individual performances or position groups and giving our thoughts. In Week 10, though, the Pittsburgh Steelers showed nothing but negatives, and we'd just be shouting into an echo chamber. So instead, we decided to just share our thoughts on the negative performance as a whole.

Joey Bray: It would be hard to give any single position group or player better than a "C" grade for their performance last night. I have been trying to think of the worst Steelers loss I have ever witnessed, and this is certainly up there, if not at the top of the list. I would argue that this was even worse than the team's most recent playoff loss to the Houston Texans last January. At least in that game, the team had a fighting chance going into the final quarter down one point.

I think we learned what the Steelers really are last night. Pittsburgh was always bound to get off to a hot start this season due to the first half of their schedule being fairly easy. However, this is going to be a bumpy second half of the season. Sure, the team will probably pick up a few more wins against teams like the Eagles, Panthers, and Titans, but they have not proven they can beat any kind of quality opponent yet.

They are a team that is fighting for a playoff spot, but still firmly below the class of the AFC. With an offense quarterbacked by a 42-year-old who is 28th in EPA/play, coupled with a lackluster and injured receiver room, there is only so far you can go. A quality team cannot have complete and total failures on offense at this point in the season, especially against divisional opponents.

The Steelers pass rush could not get their hands on Joe Burrow last night, and he picked apart their secondary all night long. The Steelers have major holes on both sides of the ball that they will need to address in the offseason. For now, they are playing the patchwork game while trying to limp into the playoffs for another year with a team that has desperately needed a reset for a long time. Pittsburgh's ceiling is the same as it has been for years now, good enough to make it to the postseason, but not good enough to hang with the teams that are serious about winning a Super Bowl.

Jarrett Bailey: You can tell how a Steelers game will go from their first offensive drive. Look at what they did against the Saints. Jaylen Warren ran the ball down the field and they were in the red zone in four plays. They ran the ball for just under 200 yards and averaged just under five yards per carry, which is how they won the game. Against the Browns, they ran the ball four out of five plays on their second drive (after seemingly learning from their first series that went three-and-out) and scored a touchdown to go up 10-0. Despite being one of the best rushing teams in the NFL, the Steelers came out in empty on their first play against the Bengals. A bobbled snap and rushed throw led to an incompletion, followed by the shotgun pitch that never works and then another incompletion. Going empty right away just felt like the Steelers thinking they could beat the Bengals at their own game, which they absolutely cannot do. It's easy to say in hindsight, sure, but look at what the Steelers' receivers bring to the table in terms of separation. Per Next Gen Stats, 120 receivers ran a route and were targeted in Week 10. The Steelers' ranking in average yards of separation when targeted are as follows:

This isn't an offense capable of being a spread system. There's a stubbornness to the offense. Mike McCarthy and Aaron Rodgers want to be the 2010 Packers. They don't have the bandwidth to do so, and their pride and/or egos get in the way too often. And look, we all have that. I used to be a guy who got up at 7:00 AM after going to bed at 1:00 AM. That works when you're 19, not so much almost a decade later. You have to adjust. And for Rodgers, he has to accept that he isn't the star of the show anymore. He isn't the main event wrestler that is going to be winning world titles – it's okay to be Adam Copeland winning the TNT Championship and Tag Team Championships or John Cena getting a late-career run with the United States Championship. Rodgers has to be willing to allow that to happen, and at times it feels like he's trying to relive the glory years.

This isn't all to bash on Rodgers because everyone loves to find excuses to do that. The Steelers have just proven to be a much better, and much more efficient offense, when they are a run-first team. It's who they are when they are most effective, and they need to accept it rather than run from it.

#2727. Austin Reaves ‘improving,’ no structural damage with injury

After exiting Wednesday's win over the Milwaukee Bucks midway through the third quarter, Los Angeles Lakers star Austin Reaves missed Sunday's victory against the Phoenix Suns with a bruised heel. Two days prior, head coach JJ Redick spoke with reporters and said Reaves is day-to-day while considering the injury to be minor. Prior to the game Sunday, Redick provided an update on Reaves.

"He's improving. Again, it really comes down to just like a pain tolerance thing," Redick said. "Nothing structural beyond a bruise. I think every day it's been better and we'll see where he's at in 48 hours when we play on Tuesday."

While it's far from set in stone, it sounds like Reaves has a chance to play Tuesday against the Charlotte Hornets. Although he was ruled out on Saturday for the Suns game, Redick didn't declare him out when he talked with the media on Friday, so it seems as though this is a game-to-game injury, at worst. That's an encouraging prognosis and suggests Reaves will be back in the lineup before too long.

The Lakers play again Wednesday against the Utah Jazz for the second half of a back-to-back set, so it's possible Reaves would only suit up for one of those games regardless, if he's even ready to go. He missed the second leg of Los Angeles' back-to-back last week against the Atlanta Hawks, too, so caution may be the guiding principle here.

In Reaves' absence Sunday evening, Luka Dončić continued his dazzling play behind 43 points — including 20 in the first quarter — and nine assists while Quentin Grimes rejoined the squad after missing the previous two games with a finger injury and added 22 points on 7-of-10 shooting (5-of-6 beyond the arc). Collin Sexton notched 16 points on 6-of-11 shooting to extend his own recent stretch of strong play, too.

Of course, the Lakers are at their best with Reaves available, so they'll hope he's back as soon as possible and it's a positive development that he's merely navigating a pain tolerance dynamic rather than any serious issues. The Lakers and Hornets face off at 8 p.m. Tuesday night.

#2724. Aidan Hutchinson has torn ACL, out for season

On Sunday, reports emerged that Detroit Lions defensive end Aidan Hutchinson was feared to have suffered a season-ending knee injury, but the team would continue to do more tests when they arrived back in the United States. Unfortunately, those fears were confirmed back in Michigan, as Hutchinson tore his ACL and will miss the rest of the Lions' 2026 season, according to NFL Insider Tom Pelissero.

If there is any positive news to glean from this situation, it is that additional testing revealed no further knee damage, which was also a major concern immediately after the injury.

Still, it's a huge blow to the Lions' 2026 season, considering Hutchinson was the most disruptive force on the team's defense. Through 11 games, Hutchinson had amassed 6.0 sacks, one forced fumble, and 52 pressures (trailing only Buffalo EDGE Greg Rousseau). More than that, he was a force multiplier, making his teammates around him better by drawing more attention and bodies away from them.

On Sunday night after the Lions' win over the New England Patriots, coach Dan Campbell noted they'll need several players to step up if Hutchinson's absence lasts.

"We gotta go. We'll see where Hutch is at, but we've gotta step up now," Campbell said. "We need some guys to really step up—and if that is the case (with a serious Hutchinson injury), fill that role."

Detroit has been able to put together more of a diverse pass rush this year. Hutchinson has only been responsible for 6.0 of the team's 32 sacks (he had 14.5 of the team's 49 last year). The Lions have gotten a fair amount of production from Alim McNeill (6.5 sacks), DJ Wonnum (6.0), Derrick Moore (4.0), and Skyler Gill-Howard (2.0) along their defensive front.

"We like 'em. That's why we acquired them when we did," Campbell said of their remaining defensive linemen. "And I know this: Derrick Moore gets better every game. The more reps he gets, he gets better. It was good to get Wonnum back today. We know what Mac [McNeill] is, Sky. So can Tyleik (Williams). And all those guys, even Levi (Onwuzurike), (Tyler) Lacy, they play a different flavor, but they push the pocket; they got movement. But I think the important thing is, man, we knew we had to do to get out of this game with a win. We had to play really good defense."

For Hutchinson, it's a devastating blow to his career. Just two years ago, he suffered a gruesome broken leg that ended his 2024 season. And while he returned to full health and form in both 2025 and 2026, recovering from a torn ACL brings a whole new set of challenges for Hutchinson.

#2722. Warriors rookie takes dramatic step in the barbershop; earns eye-popping comparison

NEW ORLEANS — You know the saying: Look good, feel good.

The bold and brash Warriors rookie slogged through the worst game of his young career the last time he took the court. He went 1-for-13 from the field and the Warriors, in their first game without Steph Curry, were handed nearly a 50-point loss by the red-hot Spurs.

Yaxel Lendeborg #1 of the Golden State Warriors and Stephon Castle #5 of the San Antonio Spurs play during the NBA Cup in San Antonio, Nov. 13, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

"He actually told me before he took his hood off and showed me his haircut," Golden State forward Georges Niang said. Or else, he may not have even recognized the rookie.

"Ain't nothing wrong with a low Caesar, man. He looks professional."

Lendeborg was so distraught over the loss that he needed a new hairdo that showed he meant business. Mission accomplished. Somewhere on a barbershop floor here in the Big Easy is the big hair that Lendeborg once embodied.

"He chopped all his hair off. You'll see," coach Steve Kerr said. "I didn't even recognize him. Apparently that's his response to getting beat by a hundred – to go right to the barber."

Yaxel Lendeborg of the Golden State Warriors arrives at the Frost Bank Center before a game against the San Antonio Spurs in San Antonio, Friday, Nov. 13, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

It wasn't quite a hundred, but it felt like it to Lendeborg, who used the same terminology in his customarily honest comments after the loss. After all, on his run to a national championship last season with Michigan, the Wolverines lost three games — by a combined 16 points.

"We just lost by like 100," Lendeborg said Saturday night. "So you know I'm f—ed up mentally right now. … I was flustered the entire game. Couldn't make a damn layup."

Kerr saw the comments after the game and used Lendeborg to make a point to the rest of the team when they regrouped for practice Sunday at Tulane University's gym.

"I loved how honest he is, how vulnerable he is. There's no pretense. He's just telling you exactly how he feels," Kerr said. "Our message today was how you respond."

Even in his short NBA career, Lendeborg hasn't had many tough nights personally. Over his first 12 games before Saturday night, he had scored in double figures 11 times, averaging 21.7.

Yaxel Lendeborg of the Golden State Warriors ties his shoes during a game against the San Antonio Spurs in San Antonio, Friday, Nov. 13, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Lendeborg and the Warriors get their first chance to put the blowout behind them Monday night in New Orleans against a Pelicans team they beat by 51 last week.

Of course, that was with a healthy Curry and the Warriors rolling amid an eight-game winning streak.

Without Curry, who will miss at least the next two weeks with a tear in his oblique, the Warriors' fate largely hinges on Lendeborg's ability to move on from his bad game.

"I'm excited for Yaxel," Niang said before tipoff. "He has raw emotion, which is great. It's like the purity of what's going on. Yaxel's just gonna say how he actually feels. And you're like, Oh, well I do feel that. Maybe I don't say it. But that's the best thing about him."

Niang, a veteran in his 10th season playing for his sixth different team, has shared a locker room with lots of different personalities. To find a comparison for Lendeborg, though, he had to go across sports — to the gridiron.

"I don't know if you find that [honesty] from any other player in the league of his stature," Niang said. "I'm not gonna do him a disservice and put these two in the same category, but you think about someone in football who's raw with his emotions, like Jameis Winston, right?

"Yaxel's probably a little more serious than some of the stuff you get from Jameis Winston, no disrespect, but that raw emotion is awesome to see."

Golden State Warriors player Yaxel Lendeborg looks on during a game against the San Antonio Spurs in San Antonio, Texas, Friday, Nov. 13, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Niang reminded Lendeborg that it was just one game of 82, and in November, without Curry, to boot. But, he said, "I think he likes to feel his emotions and kind of go through it."

Everyone processes losses differently. Kerr, a five-time champion as a player, joked that it took him until the final of his 15 seasons before he was able to

"My last year I was finally fine," he said. "My first 14 years I was a mess. I'm kind of kidding. It's one of the hardest things to do when you're a competitor — to get over a bad loss and a bad personal game. … It's not necessarily learning how to lose; it's about learning how to respond to a loss."

Travis Hunter came away with an impressive interception in the Jacksonville Jaguars' Week 10 win over the Tennessee Titans.

Fears that Detroit Lions edge rusher Aidan Hutchinson suffered a torn ACL in his right knee were confirmed after additional exams, according to The Ringer's Tom Pelissero. Though tests reportedly revealed no other significant damage to Hutchinson's knee, he is expected to miss the remainder of the 2026 season with the injury.

Hutchinson injured his knee on the Lions' first defensive series versus the New England Patriots on Sunday in Munich. After sacking New England's Drake Maye, Detroit's fifth-year star jumped in celebration and landed awkwardly on his right leg. Hutchinson immediately went to the turf. As teammates and players approached, he gestured to the front of his right knee.

Hutchinson reportedly underwent an MRI after Detroit's 35-10 win that showed a torn ACL. Lions head coach Dan Campbell said the defensive end would undergo further testing when the team returned to the U.S. before making an official announcement.

"We'll know more when we get back in the States," head coach Dan Campbell said postgame, via the Detroit News' Nolan Bianchi. "Anytime you lose a player of his caliber, the type of teammate he is, he's a captain of ours, that doesn't make things easy."

Meanwhile, a fan captured video of Hutchinson in a wheelchair at the airport prior to the Lions traveling back to Michigan on Sunday.

Maye sustained an injury to his ankle on the same play and the Patriots fear the QB may have suffered a fracture that would end his season. The third-year star stayed in Germany for additional testing that could determine if he needs surgery, Patriots head coach Mike Vrabel confirmed during a radio interview Monday.

In nine games this season, Hutchinson recorded 6 sacks and 24 total tackles in addition to 1 pass defended and 1 forced fumble.

Hutchinson's torn ACL is the second serious, season-ending injury that he has sustained during his NFL career. During the 2024 season, he suffered a broken fibula and tibia in his left leg during a Week 6 win over the Dallas Cowboys. Hutchinson recovered to play in all 17 games last year, compiling 14.5 sacks.

The Lions' 2022 first-round draft pick (No. 2 overall) out of Michigan, Hutchinson has been named to two Pro Bowls and earned second-team All-Pro honors during his career. He's compiled 49 sacks in 65 games with 200 tackles and 5 interceptions.

Last year, Hutchinson signed a four-year, $180 million contract extension (including $141 million in guaranteed money). That extension kicks in next season following the fifth-year option the Lions picked up this year.

#2718. The NFL's Week 12 flex decision is looming

Thanksgiving week will entail a smorgasbord of football. But not every course will be particularly appetizing.

The NFL’s brand-new embrace of the previously quiet night before Thanksgiving, only a few years after Black Friday was invaded, creates nine separate windows for the weekend — and seven standalone games.

The Wednesday night clash, pitting the 6-4 Packers against the 6-4 Rams, has huge implications for the slow-moving game of wild-card musical chairs in the NFC. The early Thanksgiving contest has the 7-2 Bears visiting the 7-2 Lions in a massive NFC North battle.

The Eagles-Cowboys game at 4:25 p.m. ET is becoming increasingly irrelevant — and it’ll still get more than 40 million viewers because any game in that window would generate a huge audience. The Thursday night game, between the 7-2 Chiefs and the 8-1 Bills, will be very relevant to the AFC West championship and the No. 1 seed in the conference.

On Friday, it’s the 7-2 Broncos at the 6-3 Steelers, which also has major playoff implications.

Then comes the late-afternoon Sunday window. At 4:25 p.m. ET, the 8-1 49ers host the 6-4 Seahawks.

Unfortunately, it doesn’t end there. On Sunday night, the 5-5 Patriots (who likely won’t have quarterback Drake Maye) visit the 0-8 Chargers.

If the NFL is going to slide that game out of prime time, Tuesday is the deadline for doing it. For the Eagles-Commanders game in Week 8, the league opted not to move a more consequential game into the Sunday night spot. Will the NFL again stand pat, keeping the Pats and Chargers as, to borrow a classic line from John Madden, the last shot out of a Roman candle?

The problem is that the playing of five games on Wednesday through Sunday leaves few options for Sunday night. In the 1:00 p.m. ET window, the games are Saints-Bengals, Raiders-Browns, Ravens-Texans, Giants-Colts, Jets-Dolphins, and Falcons-Vikings. Titans-Jaguars is due to begin at 4:05 p.m. ET, with Commanders-Cardinals being the other 4:25 p.m. ET game.

There’s no Sunday afternoon game (other than Seahawks-49ers) that carries huge meaning for both teams. The best option for a flex would be Falcons-Vikings, since it’s the first home game of Kirk Cousins 2.0 — and it comes against the team that signed him to a deal with multiple years of full guarantees before shocking everyone (including Cousins) the following month by selecting Michael Penix Jr. with the eighth overall pick in the draft.

The good news is that the Monday night game between the 4-5 Panthers and the 3-6 Buccaneers has direct relevance to the NFC South title, and the guaranteed postseason home game that comes with it. Still, the current Sunday night game stands out as being something that few will be interested in watching, especially after a five-day turkey-and-pigskin-induced coma.

In the end, it’s always the league’s decision. Under the rules the NFL has put in place, that decision is due to be made tomorrow.

#2717. Bayern chief reveals Guardiola had Kompany on three-man shortlist to replace him at Man City

It has previously been revealed that Bayern Munich contacted Pep Guardiola before appointing Vincent Kompany as head coach in 2024 to hear the then-Manchester City manager's thoughts on the Belgian.

Guardiola knew Kompany from his time as a player and captain at Manchester City and had also faced him as a fellow manager in England, where Kompany was in charge of Burnley before being relieved of his duties.

The Catalan coach clearly thought highly of the former Belgium international. In fact, as Bayern supervisory board member Karl-Heinz Rummenigge has now revealed, Guardiola even considered Kompany one of the top three candidates to succeed him at Manchester City at the time.

“We thought we’d hear what Pep had to say before making our final decision. So I called him,” Rummenigge told Bundesliga.com.

"We had a long conversation, lasting an hour and a half. Pep had nothing but praise for him. At some point, I asked him to give me an authentic, honest answer. He told me that he had a shortlist. Manchester City had asked him to put one together in case he ever decided to leave, and Vincent Kompany was one of the three candidates on the last shortlist he had drawn up.

“I then asked him one final question: ‘Do you believe he can coach Bayern Munich?’ He said, ‘One hundred per cent. Absolutely.’”

The conversation helped convince Rummenigge that Kompany was the right man for the job. He subsequently shared Guardiola's assessment with the rest of Bayern's hierarchy.

“I told them I’d spoken to Pep. I’d never heard him speak so highly of another person as he did of Vincent Kompany. From his point of view, there was absolutely nothing to stop us offering him a contract,” he recalled.

Kompany went on to join Bayern in 2024, while Guardiola was succeeded by Enzo Maresca as Manchester City manager last summer.

#2716. Ex-Cowboys DL believes Christian Parker change ‘obviously’ has to happen

Ex-Cowboys DL believes Christian Parker change 'obviously' has to happen appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

How long can the Dallas Cowboys go on like this? Can an actual solution be found before season's end? First-year defensive coordinator Christian Parker has been skewered since Week 1, and the condemnation is only intensifying following a 46-40 loss versus the San Francisco 49ers. Dak Prescott and the non-George Pickens members of the offense probably deserve more blame than they are getting for this latest defeat, but one cannot ignore the gaping holes that always seem present on the other side of the ball.

Former Cowboys defensive end Marcus Spears is growing tired of trying to dissect the unit's problems. He believes the first order of businesses is crystal clear. He also thinks the issues run far deeper than one area of weakness.

"I would actually love to have the enthusiasm and energy it takes to go after this team, but we've been seeing it every week," Spears said on ESPN's "NFL Live." "We've been seeing this defense do this every week. It's gotten to a point now where you shouldn't ask if a coaching change needs to be made. The answer is, obviously. There are players that look worse in Week 10 than they did in Week 1, 2, 3 and 4.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

"I don't want to be hyperbolic. It's cool to get all excited about Dallas' defense. They've been awful for two years…. The problem is you haven't had a head coach that determines a culture on the football team, because they are always limited to how the hierarchy is in Dallas… This is a microcosm of how this organization is run."

It would not be a proper Cowboys rant without taking an implied shot at Jerry Jones, would it? Many fans share in Spears' frustration. The owner is not going to discipline himself, however, so that leaves Parker as the primary target, at least for now.

Dallas surrendered a pitiful 584 total yards to the 49ers, allowing its opponent to average 7.4 yards per rush and 11.2 yards per pass. No defense has allowed more total yards per game than this one (393.8). The Cowboys have tried working around Christian Parker, but the situation is not improving. Heck, they combusted against the Arizona Cardinals.

This is not new, though. Matt Eberflus also struggled profusely in the DC role last season and Mike Zimmer was not deemed the answer either. Fans can say that Parker is in over his head, but respected play-callers have also failed in Jerry World. When such a trend occurs, it is time for an organization to consider a philosophical shift.

At minimum, a serious reflection period is required. If the Cowboys' defense cannot withstand the Tennessee Titans in Week 11, then there may not be anything left to say.

#2715. Week in Review: Spurs’ young core spreads the wealth as win streak extends to 12

Welcome to Week in Review: a Monday feature that looks back at the week that was for the San Antonio Spurs, takes a look at the week ahead, and more. Enjoy!

Week 3: The Spurs extended their winning streak to 8 with three blowout wins — two expected ones at home against the Jazz and in Sacramento, plus a somewhat surprising one at Portland — sandwiched around an all-time classic against the Mavericks. The Spurs got out to a 19-point lead before blowing it in all-too-familiar fashion, but Victor Wembanyama channeled his inner Wilt Chamberlain to help gut out the one-point win with a historic 53-point, 24-rebound, 8-block effort.

Battling off some demons in what is often a challenging venue, the Spurs went into Phoenix and demoralized another middling opponent trying to find its footing. They countered a 32-point outing from Devin Booker with 40-11-8 showing from Wemby, who even had Suns fans cheering after it became clear that the Spurs had this one in the bag, and they decided to just enjoy the show.

It wasn't their prettiest showing, but the Spurs weathered an insanely hot start from the Bulls — who at one point in the second quarter were shooting 83% from both the field and three — before bouncing back and with a dominant second half to overcome a 22-point deficit for their 10th straight win.

123-85 win vs. Golden State Warriors (NBA Emirates Cup)

An anticipated match-up lost its luster after vintage Steph Curry was ruled out with an oblique tear, but the Spurs didn't let that get in their way. Motivated by being home after a four-game road trip, they put on a show for their home crowd by starting on a franchise-record 18-0 run and never looked back. Stephon Castle finally broke out of his offensive slump with 32 points and 6 assist. The Warriors were so demoralized that Draymond Green went back to his shtick of trying (but somehow failing) to get himself ejected, and Rookie of the Year candidate Yaxel Lendeborg admitted to being so "f***ed up mentally" after the game that he…cut his hair? (I get it after a series of bad games — Wemby and Keldon Johnson did it last January — but one game?)

This one had all the hallmarks of a trap game, and it almost worked: right back out on the road, altitude, Sunday matinee, 11-game winning streak vs. 11-game losing streak, and an opponent so shorthanded they have nothing to lose. For the second time this season, the Spurs went into Utah and had to work for the win. Mo Bamba gave them the Curry performance they missed the game before by going 6-6 from three (his first six attempts of the season!), but again, the Spurs weathered the storm and countered with a career-high 31 points from Dylan Harper. As weird as it is to say about a 1-13 team that they swept, I'm glad the Spurs are done with the Jazz for this season.

John Schuhmann, NBA.com — 1 (last week: 1)

OffRtg: 119.2 (3) DefRtg: 106.6 (3) NetRtg: +12.6 (2)

The Spurs were down 22 points in Chicago on Wednesday, and they trailed by three with a little more than three minutes left in Utah over the weekend. But their winning streak is now at 12 games, and they remain at the top of the Western Conference.

With another 12 blocks (and only four fouls) over the last three games, he has 2.3 times as many blocks (60) as fouls (26), which would easily be the highest ratio for a player with at least 50 blocks in the 54 seasons that they've been counted, topping his own mark of 1.7 blocks per foul two seasons ago.

According to Synergy tracking, the Spurs now lead the league in both transition points per game (29.4) and transition points allowed per game (16.4). Over their last five games, they've outscored their opponents by 116 points (155-39) in transition.

Coming up: One other team ranks in the top six in both transition points and transition points allowed per game, and it's the Pistons, who the Spurs will host on Tuesday. After that, they'll face three teams – the Clippers, Pelicans and Kings – in the bottom four in the West.

Law Murray, The Athletic — 1 (last week: 1)

Make that three straight weeks of wins for the defending Western Conference champions. Harris, San Antonio's top offseason acquisition, still hasn't debuted. But the Spurs are the only team in the league that is top three on both offense and defense, and they won games with Devin Vassell and De'Aaron Fox missing at least one start apiece.

Kurt Helin, NBC Sports — 1 (last week: 2)

San Antonio has a net rating of +12.6, just off Oklahoma City's historic rating (+12.7) in 2024-25 season, when the Thunder won 68 games and the NBA title. The Spurs are looking to become the first team to lose in the NBA Finals and then return to that big stage since the Cavaliers in 2018 — and they are off to a fast start. San Antonio has a top-three offensive and defensive rating. San Antonio ranks inside the top 3 in both offensive and defensive rating.

#2713. Mike Vrabel disagrees with Milton Williams's claim that field conditions caused injuries

On Sunday in Germany, both teams had key players go down with significant injuries. And both injuries could be season-ending.

After the game, Patriots defensive tackle Milton Williams blamed the injuries suffered by Lions defensive end Aidan Hutchinson (knee) and Patriots quarterback Drake Maye (ankle) on the playing surface in Munich.

“Two top players in this league, probably got season-ending injuries playing on that [field],” Williams said, via Andrew Callahan of the Boston Herald. “It was bad. But like I said, I ain’t making no excuses. [The Lions] executed. They played on the same field we did, and they executed and made more plays than we did. Give them credit.”

On Monday, Patriots coach Mike Vrabel disagreed with Williams’s opinion.

“I don’t think Drake Maye’s injury was caused by the grass,” Vrabel told reporters, “and certainly we’ve seen other players that have celebrated or have left their feet and come down and had issues at that. So the field was the same for both teams, and that’s kind of what the field was.”

Vrabel stopped short of commenting on whether the field was an issue independent of the injuries. But he’s right; it’s the same for both teams.

Still, we know by now that every field is different. And there’s surely a difference between American fields that host up to 10 games per year every year and the international one-off surfaces.

#2711. Sophie Cunningham Hits Luka Doncic With $50 Million Reality Check

Sophie Cunningham did not just clap back at a silly comparison; she exposed a massive financial gap that fans often ignore. When her podcast co-host Drew Hanlen tried to compare her shot attempts to Los Angeles Lakers star Luka Doncic, Cunningham set the record straight.

The Indiana Fever standout made it clear that the two players operate in completely different worlds, not just in skill but in pay. According to Sports Illustrated, she pointed out that Doncic earns $50 million a year while she takes home $700,000 on her WNBA check.

The numbers Hanlen used to make his point were wild, but they missed the real story. The NBA star had already attempted 221 shots through just nine games, while Cunningham took 226 attempts over the entire 43-game 2026 WNBA season.

Hanlen noted that Doncic had taken 102 free throws in that small sample, whereas Cunningham managed only 74 free throws all of last year. Hanlen explained the roles differ significantly, yet Cunningham insisted the stats are the sole factor.

Cunningham's response highlighted how the new collective bargaining agreement has changed the game for women in basketball, even if the gap remains huge. Her 2026 WNBA earnings greatly exceeded previous amounts due to a new CBA that drove historic player pay hikes.

Sports Illustrated reported she signed a one-year Fever contract and earned above the league average of just over $500,000. The prior year's average sat near $120,000, demonstrating genuine advancement for the league.

Yet the difference between the top NBA earners and top WNBA stars is still staggering. Cunningham made her feelings known when she said, "He also makes $50 million a year. I make $700,000 on my WNBA check." It is hard to argue with that math when looking at the sheer volume of shots taken by a player with that kind of financial security versus a player fighting for every dollar.

Instead of comparing herself to a global superstar with unlimited shot attempts, Cunningham wants people to look at players with similar roles and stats. She told Hanlen, "You should compare me more to Quentin Grimes."

She pointed out that they average around 8-10 points and claimed, "My 3-point shooting is actually better than the both of them." Her final word on the subject was simple: "If we're gonna compare, let's compare."

Now that her time with Indiana could be done, the market will decide her next big move.

#2710. JJ Redick puts Lakers ‘trend’ on blast despite 8th straight win

PHOENIX — The Lakers' eighth straight win didn't stop coach J.J. Redick from getting real about one of the team's troubling trends.

The Lakers' victory over the Suns on Sunday played out similarly to other wins they've had this season: An explosive first quarter from Luka Dončić leading to the Lakers having an advantage and a double-digit lead going into halftime before a dominant third quarter gives them a comfortable lead.

Los Angeles Lakers head coach J.J. Redick, center, isn't happy with how his team has handled big leads late in games. AP Photo/Darryl Webb

And then they let go of the rope before pulling out a victory after nearly blowing a significant lead, which is exactly what happened when the Suns cut the Lakers' 31-point lead in the third quarter to five in the fourth before the Lakers won the game by eight.

"I told the team this after the game: We're working too hard and we're playing too hard to build these leads," Redick said. "It's a disservice to each other to take your foot off the gas."

The Lakers are one of the league's worst fourth quarter teams. AP Photo/Aaron Gash

The Lakers, who are tied for the league's best record at 12-2, have the league's second-best net rating in first quarters (plus-18.7), sixth-best rating in second quarters (plus-12.5) and best net rating in third quarters (plus-24.5).

But they have the second-worst net rating in fourth quarters (minus-24.4) because their defense falls off a cliff, leading to them blowing big leads.

The Lakers' 131.3 defensive rating in fourth quarters is the league's second-worst mark.

Los Angeles Lakers forward Ziaire Williams (11), Luka Doncic (77), Jake LaRavia (12) and Walker Kessler (14) wait during a timeout against the Phoenix Suns, Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026, in Phoenix. AP Photo/Darryl Webb

The Lakers left Phoenix with the win despite nearly blowing a 31-point lead. Getty Images

"You have to play for 48 minutes and we're going to need our defense to win us games," Redick said. "These guys want to be a top-10 defense, we think we can be a good defensive team, I believe we can be a good defensive team. This has been consistent now, this is a trend. Gotta be better in the fourth quarter."

The Lakers will tie their longest winning streak under Redick if they beat the Hornets on Tuesday.

But the former NBA sharpshooter isn't allowing his team's current success to distract from the areas they need to improve at.
